| Priority or Standard | Priority |

| Received Date(s) | May 8, 2023 |

| Division/Office | Division of Diabetes, Lipid Disorders, and Obesity (DDLO) Office of Cardiology, Hematology, Endocrinology and Nephrology (OCHEN) |
| Reviewer Name(s) | Raymond Soccio, MD, PhD |
| Review Completion Date | November 7, 2023 |
| Established/Proper Name | Tirzepatide |
| (Proposed) Trade Name | Zepbound |
| Applicant | Eli Lilly and Company (Lilly) |
| Dosage Form(s) | Injection 2.5 mg/0.5 mL in a single-dose pen Injection 5 mg/0.5 mL in a single-dose pen Injection 7.5 mg/0.5 mL in a single-dose pen Injection 10 mg/0.5 mL in a single-dose pen Injection 12.5 mg/0.5 mL in a single-dose pen Injection 15 mg/0.5 mL in a single-dose pen |
| Applicant Proposed Dosing Regimen(s) | The recommended starting dosage of ZEPBOUND is 2.5 mg injected subcutaneously once weekly. After 4 weeks, increase the dosage to 5 mg injected subcutaneously once weekly. (b) (4) (b) (4) increase the dosage in 2.5 mg increments after at least 4 weeks on the current dose. The maximum dosage of ZEPBOUND is 15 mg injected subcutaneously once weekly. |
| Applicant Proposed Indication(s)/Population(s) | As an adjunct to a reduced-calorie diet and increased physical activity for chronic weight management in adults with an initial body mass index (BMI) of: • 30 kg/m2 or greater (obesity) or • 27 kg/m2 or greater (overweight) in the presence of at least one weight-related comorbid condition (e.g., hypertension, dyslipidemia, (b) (4), type 2 diabetes mellitus, obstructive sleep apnea or cardiovascular disease). |
| Recommendation on Regulatory Action | Approval |
| Recommended Dosing Regimen(s) | The recommended starting dosage of ZEPBOUND is 2.5 mg injected subcutaneously once weekly. The 2.5 mg dosage is for |

---

Clinical Review
Raymond Soccio, MD, PhD
NDA 217806
Zepbound (tirzepatide)

|  | treatment initiation and is not intended for chronic weight management. After 4 weeks, increase the dosage to 5 mg injected subcutaneously once weekly. The dosage may be increased in 2.5 mg increments, after at least 4 weeks on the current dose. The recommended maintenance dosages of ZEPBOUND in adults are 5 mg, 10 mg, or 15 mg injected subcutaneously once weekly. Consider treatment response and tolerability when selecting the maintenance dosage. If patients do not tolerate a maintenance dosage, consider a lower maintenance dosage. The maximum dosage of ZEPBOUND is 15 mg injected subcutaneously once weekly. |
| --- | --- |
| Recommended Indication(s)/Population(s) | As an adjunct to a reduced-calorie diet and increased physical activity for chronic weight management in adults with an initial body mass index (BMI) of: • 30 kg/m2 or greater (obesity) or • 27 kg/m2 or greater (overweight) in the presence of at least one weight-related comorbid condition (e.g., hypertension, dyslipidemia, type 2 diabetes mellitus, obstructive sleep apnea, or cardiovascular disease). |

## 1.1. Product Introduction

Tirzepatide (proposed proprietary name: Zepbound, also known as LY3298176) is a dual agonist 
of the glucagon-like peptide-1 (GLP-1) and glucose-dependent insulinotropic polypeptide (GIP) 
receptors. The Applicant, Eli Lilly and Company (Lilly), proposes an indication as an adjunct to a 
reduced-calorie diet and increased physical activity for chronic weight management (CWM) in 
adults with an initial body mass index (BMI) of:

• 30 kg/m<sup>2</sup> or greater (obesity) or

$$
\mathrm{kg}/\mathrm{m}^{2}
$$

• 27 kg/m<sup>2</sup> or greater (overweight) in the presence of at least one weight-related comorbid 
condition (e.g., hypertension, dyslipidemia, type 2 diabetes mellitus, obstructive sleep 
apnea [OSA], or cardiovascular disease).

$$
\mathrm{kg/m^{2}}
$$

Tirzepatide is intended for once-weekly subcutaneous (SC) injection at three maintenance 
dosages of 5, 10, or 15 mg. To reduce the likelihood of gastrointestinal (GI) adverse events, a 
dose-escalation regimen is proposed, with a starting dose of 2.5 mg once weekly for 4 weeks 
and then dose increases every 4 weeks to 5, 7.5, 10, 12.5, and 15 mg/week. Tirzepatide is a 
drug-device combination with each dose delivered in a single-dose pen-injector prefilled with 
one of the six doses in a 0.5 mL volume.

Tirzepatide SC injection at these same dosages via the same device and dose escalation 
schedule is currently approved in the U.S. for treatment of type 2 diabetes (T2D) under NDA 
215866 (proprietary name Mounjaro). While tirzepatide is the first approved dual agonist for 
both incretin hormone receptors GLP-1R and GIPR, GLP-1R only agonists are approved and 
widely-used for T2D and CWM, so parts of this review will refer more generally to class effects 
of incretin drugs. For the CWM indication, the Division granted tirzepatide Fast Track 
Designation and Rolling Review (see Section 3.2), and this NDA was reviewed on a priority 
timeline.

## Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness

The Applicant has demonstrated substantial evidence of effectiveness to support approval of 
tirzepatide for the proposed indication as an adjunct to diet and exercise for CWM in patients 
with obesity or with overweight and weight-related comorbidities. To support the efficacy and 
safety of tirzepatide for the proposed indication, the Applicant has provided clinical data from 
two adequate and well-controlled phase 3 trials in subjects with obesity/overweight: GPHK 
(SURMOUNT-1) in a trial population with obesity/overweight without type 2 diabetes (T2D) and 
GPHL (SURMOUNT-2) in a trial population with obesity/overweight and T2D. The weight loss 
achieved in these two trials is statistically significant and clinically meaningful. At maintenance

---

Raymond Soccio, MD, PhD

NDA 217806

Zepbound (tirzepatide)

dosages of tirzepatide 10 mg or 15 mg weekly, the placebo-corrected mean weight loss at one 
year in the intention-to-treat (ITT) population was -16.4 to -17.8% from baseline in subjects 
without T2D (trial GPHK) and -9.6 to -11.6% from baseline in subjects with T2D (trial GPHL), and 
-11.9% with the lowest maintenance dosage of 5 mg in subjects without T2D (trial GPHK). In 
both trials, weight loss with tirzepatide was associated with improvements in risk factors for 
cardiovascular disease (glycemia, blood pressure, and lipids).

## Benefit-Risk Integrated Assessment

Tirzepatide is a selective dual agonist of the receptors for glucagon-like peptide-1 (GLP-1) and glucose-dependent insulinotropic polypeptide 
(GIP) and is currently approved in the U.S. as a subcutaneous (SC) injection at dosages up to 15 mg weekly for the treatment of type 2 diabetes 
(T2D). The Applicant now submits NDA 217806 to support marketing approval of tirzepatide for chronic weight management (CWM) in adults
with obesity (body mass index [BMI] ≥30 kg/m<sup>2</sup>) or with overweight (BMI ≥27 kg/m<sup>2</sup>) and one or more weight-related comorbidities. 
Obesity and overweight impact millions of Americans, and excess weight is a risk factor for many common health conditions including 
hypertension, dyslipidemia, T2D, coronary heart disease, stroke, gallbladder disease, osteoarthritis, sleep apnea and respiratory problems, and 
some cancers. Individuals with obesity report impaired physical functioning, mental health conditions such as depression, and a decreased 
quality of life. The standard-of-care for treatment is lifestyle therapy, such as reduced calorie intake, nutritionally complete diets, increased 
physical activity, and behavioral modification. Patients with morbid obesity (BMI ≥ 40 kg/m<sup>2</sup> or ≥ 35 kg/m<sup>2</sup> with weight-related comorbidities) 
may benefit from bariatric surgery, with weight loss, cardiometabolic improvements (including resolution of T2D), and prolonged life. 
Historically, pharmacologic treatments for CWM were not widely used, likely due to a combination of factors including lack of insurance 
coverage, limited weight loss efficacy, safety issues, and historical concerns about the benefit-risk balance that have led several obesity drugs 
to be removed from the U.S. market. However, there is now rapidly growing public acceptance and utilization of injectable GLP-1R agonists like 
semaglutide (proprietary name Wegovy), which is highly effective for weight loss with a well-established safety profile. There is an unmet need 
for additional CWM drugs achieving even greater degrees of weight loss, and for multiple marketed drugs to ensure availability and potentially 
reduce costs.
Tirzepatide was studied with dose escalation to maintenance dosages of 5, 10, and 15 mg by weekly SC injection, in a large phase 3 program 
that meets the Division’s expectations for size and scope. At dosages of 5, 10, and 15 mg, tirzepatide was shown to be effective for CWM at one 
year in a trial of subjects without diabetes (placebo-corrected weight loss of -11.9%, -16.4%, and -17.8%, respectively). In another trial of
tirzepatide exclusively in subjects with T2D, tirzepatide at dosages of 10 and 15 mg gave placebo-corrected weight loss -9.6 and -11.6%. Though 
the tirzepatide 5 mg weekly maintenance dosage was not studied for CWM in subjects with T2D (who can already use this approved dosage for 
glycemic control), this 5 mg dosage was studied in previous trials for T2D and showed significant weight loss in subjects with T2D. Furthermore, 
the 5 mg dosage consistently showed evidence of better tolerability compared to the higher maintenance dosages of 10 and 15 mg weekly, 
supporting approval of this 5 mg dosage as safe and effective for CWM in patients with or without T2D. On all three tirzepatide maintenance 
dosages, weight loss was achieved and maintained for up to 72 weeks of treatment in all populations studied (with and without T2D). The
<u>majority of subjects on any maintenance dosage of tirzepatide achieved at least 5% body weight loss, a clinically meaningful threshold, at 72</u>

$$
(B M)\geq40kg/m^2or\geq35kg/m^2
$$

Clinical Review Raymond Soccio NDA 217806 Zepbound (tirzep

MD, PhD atide)

weeks. The treatment effects observed with tirzepatide in this program are clinically meaningful and larger in magnitude than has been seen with any currently approved drug for CWM. Weight loss with tirzepatide was associated with clinically meaningful improvements in glycemia, blood pressure, and lipids in most trials. There was also some evidence for improvements in patient-reported physical functioning with tirzepatide, but the clinical meaningfulness of these changes is uncertain.

GLP-1R agonists have a well-characterized safety profile, and the CWM and T2D development programs for tirzepatide (a GLP-1R agonist with dual agonism at the GIPR) have not identified any additional safety concerns. There is a boxed warning for thyroid C-cell tumors and medullary thyroid carcinoma based on nonclinical data. Other described or theoretical safety concerns include gastrointestinal adverse events, volume depletion leading to acute kidney injury, gallbladder disease, acute pancreatitis, hypersensitivity, hypoglycemia, worsening of diabetic retinopathy, and psychiatric disorders including suicidality.

In summary, the weight loss efficacy of tirzepatide appears substantial and could, in some patients, reach the >20-25% weight loss from baseline typically achieved after bariatric surgery. The safety concerns for tirzepatide are well-established for the class of incretin drugs and can be appropriately mitigated in the post-market setting mainly via labeling and routine pharmacovigilance. While there is no evidence for reduced efficacy or safety with long-term use, it is unavoidable that there remains uncertainty regarding longer-term use of tirzepatide (or other incretin drugs) beyond the periods studied in trials. On balance, the benefit-risk assessment for tirzepatide for the indication of CWM in adults with overweight/obesity appears favorable.

## 2. Therapeutic Context

### 2.1. Analysis of Condition

According to the U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC), obesity (defined as BMI 
≥30 kg/m<sup>2</sup>) is a common, severe, and costly health condition.<sup>1</sup> The U.S. obesity prevalence in 
the three years prior to the COVID-19 pandemic (2017 to March 2020) was 41.9%, increased 
from 30.5% two decades earlier (1999 to 2000). During the same time, the prevalence of severe 
obesity (defined as BMI ≥ 40 kg/m<sup>2</sup>) increased from 4.7% to 9.2%.<sup>2</sup> Obesity-related conditions 
include heart disease, stroke, T2D and certain types of cancer, which are among the leading 
causes of preventable, premature death. According to the 2013 American Heart Association / 
American College of Cardiology (AHA/ACC) guidelines on the management of overweight and 
obesity, obesity raises the risk of morbidity and mortality from atherosclerotic cardiovascular 
disease (ASCVD), and is also associated with increased risk of all-cause mortality.<sup>3</sup> The 
estimated annual medical cost of obesity in the U.S. was nearly $173 billion in 2019 dollars, and 
annual medical costs for adults with obesity were $1,861 higher than for people with healthy 
weights.<sup>4</sup> Obesity is notoriously difficult to treat yet affects millions of American adults, 
reducing their overall health, lifespan, and quality-of-life, and is therefore a significant public 
health issue.

$$
\geq30kg/m^2)
$$

$$
BM1\geq40kg/m^2)
$$

As described in the FDA draft guidance for developing CWM drugs,<sup>5</sup> weight change is the 
primary endpoint of interest in clinical trials for the development of obesity drugs. Among 
individuals with overweight or obesity, weight is an easily measured surrogate for body 
adiposity, and long-term weight loss in the range of 5 to 10% is associated with improved 
glycemic control, blood pressure, and lipid parameters.<sup>6</sup>

It is presumed that salutary changes in cardiovascular and metabolic risk factors will translate 
into ASCVD benefit, such as reductions in the incidence of myocardial infarction and stroke. 
Although some, but not all, observational studies suggest that modest degrees of intentional

Raymond Soccio, MD, PhD

Zepbound (tirzepatide)

weight loss in overweight and obese individuals can reduce the incidence of some cancers, 
cardiovascular disease, and all-cause mortality, weight loss (at least at the magnitude seen in 
trials of most other CWM drugs; see Table 1) has not been established as a surrogate for ASCVD
benefit. The Look AHEAD (Action for Health in Diabetes) trial<sup>7</sup> was a randomized controlled trial 
in over 5000 patients with T2D comparing an intensive lifestyle intervention (including weight 
loss) to standard-of-care for a follow-up of 10 years. The trial was stopped early (9.6 years) for 
futility of the composite primary outcome (first occurrence of CV death, non-fatal MI, non-fatal 
stroke, or hospitalized angina); HR 0.95 (95% CI 0.83, 1.09). Weight loss was greater in the 
intervention group (8.6% at 1 year, 6.0% at study end) as compared to the control group (0.7% 
at 1 year, 3.5% at study end). Weight loss drugs with sympathomimetic effects may also cause 
CV harm.<sup>8</sup>

Although two GLP-1R agonists (weekly semaglutide and daily liraglutide) have been approved 
to reduce the risk of major adverse cardiovascular events (MACE: CV death, non-fatal 
myocardial infarction, or non-fatal stroke) in adults with T2D and established CV disease, 
cardiovascular outcomes in subjects with obesity, who receive pharmacologic treatment for 
weight loss, are unknown. A cardiovascular outcome trial (CVOT) with semaglutide in subjects 
with overweight/obesity and without T2D (SELECT) is recently completed, and a CVOT is
ongoing for tirzepatide in populations with obesity. The results of these two CVOTs will help 
clarify the potential benefits of drug-induced weight loss on cardiovascular outcomes.

## Analysis of Current Treatment Options

As of this writing there are 5 drugs approved in the U.S. for CWM in obesity: orlistat, 
phentermine/topiramate, naltrexone/bupropion, liraglutide, and semaglutide (see Table 1). 
(Note that setmelanotide is indicated only in patients with rare monogenic causes of obesity).

Phentermine and other amphetamine congeners were approved in the U.S. primarily in the 
1950s and 60s with a short-term use limitation. However, this class continues to be frequently 
used for prescription weight loss, despite a lack of long-term safety and efficacy data.

<sup>7</sup>The Look AHEAD Research Group. Cardiovascular effects of intensive lifestyle intervention in type 2 diabetes. N 
Engl J Med 2013; 369: 145-54.

<sup>8</sup>James WPT, et al. Effect of sibutramine on cardiovascular outcomes in overweight and obese subjects. N Engl J 
Med 2010; 363:905-17.

The widespread use of semaglutide has drastically changed the pharmacological treatment of obesity in the U.S., but there remains an unmet need for drugs capable of achieving even greater degrees of weight loss, and for multiple marketed drugs to help ensure availability and potentially reduce costs.

## 3. Regulatory Background

### 3.1. U.S. Regulatory Actions and Marketing History

Tirzepatide was approved as Mounjaro on May 13,2022, under NDA 215866, as an adjunct to diet and exercise to improve glycemic control in adults with T2D. There are 3 ongoing PMRs for Mounjaro:

- A randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial in pediatric T2D patients (note that pediatric studies will also be required for CWM, see Section 12)

- A milk-only lactation study (see Section 8.8.2)

- A medullary thyroid carcinoma (MTC) registry-based case series study, as required for all incretin drugs (see Section 8.5.1).

Please refer to Section 8.9.1 for details on the post-marketing safety surveillance for tirzepatide.

## Summary of Presubmission/Submission Regulatory Activity

This section provides the most relevant regulatory history of tirzepatide for CWM under IND 
139721. Note that the early development of tirzepatide occurred for the T2D indication under 
IND 128801, as detailed in the review of T2D NDA 215866.<sup>9</sup> The CWM pre-IND 139721 was 
opened on May 18, 2018. An End-of-Phase 2 (EOP2) meeting was held on June 26, 2019, with 
agreements including a gastric emptying study in subjects with overweight/obesity and without 
T2D (study GPHU in this application) and phase 3 trial design, including an early SURMOUNT-1 
Ambulatory Blood Pressure Monitoring (ABPM) substudy to inform heart rate effects, adding a 
5 mg maintenance dosage, and having four phase 3 SURMOUNT trials in the initial NDA. The
IND was submitted on October 30, 2019, with GPHK/SURMOUNT-1 as the IND-opening trial, 
and it was deemed safe-to-proceed on November 29, 2019.

## Foreign Regulatory Actions and Marketing History

Tirzepatide is approved and marketed in the U.S. and other countries for T2D, including the EU, 
Canada, Australia, Japan, and Switzerland. To my knowledge, tirzepatide has not been approved 
for a weight management indication anywhere in the world to date.

# 4. Significant Issues from Other Review Disciplines Pertinent to Clinical <u>Conclusions on Efficacy and Safety</u>

## Office of Scientific Investigations (OSI)

OSI was consulted to inspect 6 sites, 2 in Argentina, 3 in the continental U.S., and one in Puerto 
Rico. These sites accounted for 8.4% of the subjects enrolled in the two phase 3 CWM trials: 
118 of 2539 (4.6%) in trial GPHK and for 178 of 938 (19.0%) in trial GPHL. Sites were selected in 
consultation with Dr. Ling Yang, OSI reviewer. The rationale for the six selected clinical sites for 
inspection was primarily based on high efficacy, financial disclosures, complaints, and lack of 
previous compliance inspection.

The following sites were inspected, and no significant deficiencies were identified:

• Drs. Federico Manghi (Argentina, GPHK Site #503 with 62 subjects enrolled and GPHL 
Site #54333 with 63 subjects enrolled)

• Dr. Diego Aizenberg (Argentina, GPHL Site #85301 with 81 subjects enrolled)

• Dr. Timothy Smith (Saint Peters, MO, GPHK Site #114 with 29 subjects enrolled)

• Dr. Seth Forman (Tampa, FL, transferred from Dr. Tanya Bogle; GPHK Site #111 with 27 
subjects enrolled)

• Dr. Gregorio Cortes-Maisonet (San Juan, Puerto Rico, GPHL Site #92464 with 18 subjects 
enrolled)

• Dr. Joanna Van (Tustin, CA, GPHL Site #83971 with 16 subjects enrolled)

Please refer to Dr. Yang’s OSI review dated September 12, 2023, for further details. She 
concluded that, based on the overall inspection results of these clinical investigators and the 
regulatory assessments, the data generated by these sites are verifiable. Per OSI, trials GPHK 
and GPHL appear to have been conducted adequately and the clinical data submitted by the 
Applicant appear acceptable in support of the respective indication, and I concur.

## 4.2. Product Quality

For all drug substance information, this NDA cross-references the approved NDA 215866, 
tirzepatide for T2D (Mounjaro), and no new chemistry, manufacturing, and controls (CMC) 
information was submitted. The integrated quality review for the current NDA by the Office of 
Pharmaceutical Quality (OPQ) was signed by application technical lead Dr. Theodore Carver on 
October 6, 2023. This integrated review notes that the drug products in this NDA, including the 
six strengths of tirzepatide, are identical to the equivalent pen injector drug products approved 
in NDA 215866.

## Clinical Microbiology

Tirzepatide is not an antimicrobial or antiviral. The microbiology review by Drs. Aditi Das and 
Yuansha Chen was part of the integrated quality review of this NDA dated October 6, 2023, and 
no deficiencies were found. Under the current NDA, an information request was conveyed to 
the Applicant regarding validation of endotoxin testing, and their reply was deemed adequate. 
Please refer to NDA 215866 and its microbiology review dated December 13, 2021, for the rest 
of the sterility assurance and microbiological quality of the drug product assessment.

## Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology

For all nonclinical information, this NDA cross-references the approved NDA 215866, tirzepatide 
for T2D (Mounjaro). No new toxicology studies were conducted to support the CWM indication, 
as a complete nonclinical program was conducted and reviewed to support the T2D indication 
(IND 128801 and NDA 215866). No new nonclinical studies were required by FDA as the dosage 
of tirzepatide (up to 15 mg once weekly) and the age group (adults) are identical to those in the 
approved indication.

Below is a summary of select nonclinical data from the application and nonclinical review:

• Nonclinical pharmacodynamic data included effects of tirzepatide on gastric emptying in 
mice, and effects on food intake and body weight in rats fed a high fat high sucrose diet 
and diet-induced obese mice. Studies were also performed in knockout mice and cells 
lacking the GLP-1R or GIPR. For instance, tirzepatide stimulated cAMP production and 
lipolysis in mature adipocytes that express the GIPR and not the GLP-1R, indicating an 
effect of GIPR agonism. Consistent with this, a published study (not part of this NDA) 
showed in mouse and human islets that both the GIPR and GLP-1R contribute to

tirzepatide effects on insulin secretion.<sup>11</sup>

• To test for QT prolongation potential, the Applicant performed *in vitro* human ether-ago-go related gene (hERG) assays showing no inhibition at the highest concentration 
tested (300 µM) and ECGs in monkeys with single and repeat tirzepatide dosing, 
showing no QTc prolongation (consistent with the human ECG data, see Section 8.4.9).

• To test for mutagenesis and carcinogenesis, the Applicant performed an *in vivo* 
micronucleus genotoxicity study was conducted in rats (no evidence of genotoxicity), a 
26-week study in RasH2 transgenic mice (no C-cell hyperplasia or neoplasia), and a 2-
year carcinogenicity study in rats (increases in C-cell adenomas and carcinomas in both 
sexes). Due to species differences in GLP-1 receptor expression and activation, the 
relevance of these findings to humans is unclear, as reflected in the class labeling for 
incretins.

• The Applicant conducted fertility, embryo-fetal development, and prenatal and 
postnatal development studies in Sprague Dawley rats. They also conducted an embryofetal development study in a second species, New Zealand White rabbits, as well as a 
juvenile toxicology study in rats to evaluate tirzepatide effects on growth and 
development in support of clinical pediatric development. Generally, most maternal and 
fetal effects were likely secondary to effects on food consumption and body weight, 
though increased incidences of malformations and developmental variations were 
observed at the highest 0.5 mg/kg dose in rats.

Dr. Elena Braithwaite, of the Division of Pharmacology-Toxicology for Cardiology, Hematology, 
Endocrinology and Nephrology (DPT-CHEN), reviewed the T2D NDA (dated March 16, 2022)<sup>12</sup>
and the current NDA (dated October 14, 2023); refer to these for a detailed discussion of the 
nonclinical findings, which were generally consistent with other GLP-1R agonists. The 
nonclinical team recommends approval.

## Clinical Pharmacology

The clinical pharmacology data for this Application were reviewed by Office of Clinical 
Pharmacology (OCP): Drs. Mohamad Kronfol and Edwin Chow from the Division of 
Cardiometabolic and Endocrine Pharmacology (DCEP) and Drs. Justin Earp and Jiajun Liu from
the Division of Pharmacometrics (DPM). The OCP/DCEP review determined that the clinical 
pharmacology data submitted to this NDA were acceptable and recommended approval from a 
clinical pharmacology perspective. Please refer to their review (dated October 11, 2023) for 
more detailed information.

The clinical pharmacology of tirzepatide had been well-characterized in the development 
program for T2D and reviewed under NDA 215866 and cross-referenced for this CWM NDA. 
Note that the phase 3 trials for T2D and CWM used the identical formulation, route of 
administration, and dosage escalation regimen. Please refer to the clinical pharmacology team 
primary review of T2D NDA 215866 dated March 18, 2022, by Drs. Mohamad Kronfol, Elyes 
Dahmane, and Yuching Yang.<sup>13</sup>

The clinical pharmacology studies conducted under the T2DM NDA 215866 included relative 
bioavailability, single- and multiple-ascending dose, disposition of radioactivity, hepatic and 
renal impairment, drug-drug interaction, and pharmacodynamic studies. The Applicant submits 
three additional clinical pharmacology studies or reports that were reviewed as part of this 
NDA:

• a new gastric emptying study (GPHU)

• a mechanistic study on caloric intake (GPGT)

• a new population PK and pharmacokinetic-pharmacodynamic (PK/PD) report in the 
indicated population.

Below is a summary of information from the OCP review of tirzepatide.

<u>Mechanism of Action/Pharmacodynamics</u>

Tirzepatide is an agonist at the GIP and GLP-1 receptors. Tirzepatide is a 39 amino acid synthetic 
peptide engineered from the GIP sequence and includes a C20 fatty diacid moiety. Based on 
known physiology and pharmacology of GIP and GLP-1, dual signaling at both receptors is 
proposed to result in improved weight loss and changes in carbohydrate and lipid metabolism.

The Applicant constructed population body weight models for phase 3 CWM trials GPHK and 
GPHL, and the models adequately described tirzepatide dose-dependent body weight 
reductions over time. The Applicant also constructed a PK/PD model for safety, which 
reasonably described the occurrence of nausea, vomiting, and diarrhea adverse events.

In new trial GPHU and previously-reviewed trial GPGA, tirzepatide reduced gastric emptying in 
subjects with and without T2D. Trial GPHU enrolled 36 subjects (30 of whom completed the 
trial) with overweight or obesity, 18 without T2D and 18 with T2D, to measure the effect of 
tirzepatide on the PK of acetaminophen as a probe of gastric emptying. Acetaminophen PK was 
measured in each subject on Day -1 in the absence of tirzepatide, on Day 2 after receiving 
tirzepatide 5 mg on Day 1, and on Day 37 after a rapid 6-week dose escalation to tirzepatide 15
mg (5 mg on Day 8; 10 mg on Days 15, 22, and 29; 15 mg on Day 36). There was an observed 
delay in acetaminophen tmax of approximately 1 hour and a maximum decrease in Cmax of 
approximately 55% on Day 1 after an initial 5 mg dose of tirzepatide, with no clinically relevant

$$

$$

Phase 1 trial GPGT assessed insulin and glucagon secretion during a clamp, and also assessed *ad 
libitum* food intake showing reduced calorie and macronutrient intake after 28 weeks of 
tirzepatide treatment. The trial enrolled 117 subjects with T2D and BMI 25-45 kg/m<sup>2</sup>, assessing 
*ad libitum* food intake during a 45-minute buffet meal at noon at baseline and after 28 weeks of 
treatment with tirzepatide (via phase 3 dose escalation to 15 mg), semaglutide, or placebo. The 
Applicant concludes that at Week 28 tirzepatide lowered food intake by -309.8 kcal (95%CI - 
423.0, -196.6) from baseline versus placebo. The Applicant concludes that at Week 28 
tirzepatide lowered carbohydrate, protein, and fat intake by -117.8 kcal (95% CI -172.8, -62.9), -
59.9 kcal (-84.0, -35.7), and -142.5 kcal (-207.2, -77.8) respectively from baseline versus 
placebo. This study supports the statement in Section 12.2 (Pharmacodynamics) of labeling on 
food and calorie intake.

$$

$$

## Absorption

Tirzepatide is administered as a weekly SC injection into the abdomen, thigh, or upper arm (the 
last of which may require another person to inject). Following SC administration, approximately 
80% of an administered dose is absorbed, with maximum plasma concentrations (Cmax) 
occurring between 8 to 72 hours (tmax) with a median of approximately 24 hours. PK is dose 
proportional in the dose ranging from 5-15 mg based on the population PK analysis.

$$
(C_{max})
$$

## Distribution

The mean volume of distribution of tirzepatide at steady state is approximately 9.7 L, and it is 
extensively bound (99%) to plasma albumin. Due to a prolonged half-life (t1/2), steady-state 
plasma concentrations are typically achieved following four weeks of weekly dosing.

$$
(\mathbf{t}_{1/2})
$$

## Metabolism and Elimination

Tirzepatide is primarily eliminated by metabolism (i.e., proteolytic cleavage of the peptide 
backbone, beta-oxidation of the C20 fatty diacid moiety, and amide hydrolysis), with a mean 
clearance of 0.056 L/h and an elimination t1/2 of approximately 5 days. This relatively long t1/2
enables once-weekly dosing. The four minor tirzepatide metabolites are produced by 
proteolytic cleavage of tirzepatide, each accounting for <5.7% of circulating radioactivity from a 
4.1 mg radiolabeled dose of tirzepatide in study GPHX. Renal excretion is the primary route of

$$
\mathfrak{t}_{1/2}
$$

$$
\mathfrak{t}_{1/2}
$$

radiolabeled dose is recovered as metabolites in urine (50%) and feces (20%).

## Intrinsic Factors

In the DPM review of the population PK model, it was noted that body weight was the only 
statistically significant covariate identified on tirzepatide PK. Yet, weight-based dose
adjustment was not deemed as warranted due to the moderate impact on PK and the proposed
titration dosing approach. Additionally, age, sex, race, renal impairment, hepatic impairment, 
antidrug antibody (ADA) status, or ADA titer were not found to significantly impact tirzepatide 
PK, and no dosing adjustment was deemed necessary based on these factors in obese or 
overweight subjects.

Following a single tirzepatide 5 mg dose in subjects with renal (mild, moderate, severe, and end 
stage renal disease [ESRD]; study GPGG) and hepatic (Child-Pugh classification of mild, 
moderate, and severe; study GPGQ) impairment, no meaningful changes in tirzepatide 
pharmacokinetics (AUC, Cmax, and t1/2) were observed compared to subjects with normal renal 
and normal hepatic function, respectively. Based on data from study GPHI, the clinical 
pharmacology team felt that dose adjustments would not be necessary based on differences in
selected injection site (abdomen, thigh, or upper arm).

$$
C_{\max},\mathrm{and}\;t_{1/2})
$$

## Drug-Drug Interactions

*In vitro* data showed that tirzepatide does not appear to inhibit or induce cytochrome P450 
enzymes or inhibit renal and hepatic drug transporters. Therefore, the potential for tirzepatide 
to interact with drugs dependent on these enzymes or transporters for metabolism or 
elimination is low.

However, in trials GPGA and GPHU, tirzepatide delayed gastric emptying, and thus could 
potentially affect the absorption of concomitant orally administered medications. The Applicant 
claimed that their physiologically based pharmacokinetic (PBPK) modeling predictions did not 
show clinically meaningful changes in absorption of concomitant medications (e.g., 
atorvastatin, digoxin, lisinopril, metformin, metoprolol, sitagliptin, and S-warfarin), and
therefore no dosage adjustments would be required. The OCP team reviewed the Applicant’s 
PBPK analysis and felt that it was adequate to provide qualitative assessment of the effect of
tirzepatide-mediated delayed gastric emptying on the pharmacokinetics of these medications.

In study GPGR, coadministration of a single 5 mg dose of tirzepatide with a combination oral 
contraceptive (COC; ethinyl estradiol 0.35 mg and norgestimate 0.25 mg) lowered the AUCtau of 
ethinyl estradiol by approximately 21% (geometric mean 0.788, 90% CI: 0.73, 0.85),
norelgestromin by 23% (0.775, 90% CI: 0.71, 0.84), and norgestimate by 21% (0.792, 90% CI:
0.614, 1.02), compared to administration of the COC alone. In addition, the Cmax of ethinyl
estradiol, norelgestromin, and norgestimate were lowered by approximately 59% (i.e., 
geometric mean 0.41, 90% CI: 0.355, 0.473), 55% (0.451, 95% CI: 0.402, 0.505), and 66% (0.345,

$$
\mathsf{AUC_{tau}}
$$

$$
C_{max}
$$

---

Due to the findings reported in study GPGR, the Clinical Pharmacology review team
recommended that proposed labeling inform prescribers that concomitant use of tirzepatide 
with oral hormonal contraceptives may reduce the efficacy of the oral hormonal contraceptive. 
They acknowledged that tachyphylaxis may develop after multiple dosing as evident by the 
acetaminophen marker data from study GPGA in the tirzepatide 5 mg dose cohort. However, 
they expressed concern for a loss of efficacy of the COC during the time when tachyphylaxis is 
taking place (i.e., after initiation and following each dose escalation of tirzepatide). Considering 
that animal reproduction studies report that there may be risks to the fetus from exposure to 
tirzepatide during pregnancy, patients using oral hormonal contraceptives should be advised to 
switch to a non-oral contraceptive method or add a barrier method of contraception for four 
weeks after initiation and for four weeks after each dose escalation of tirzepatide.

Other key points from the clinical pharmacology reviews were:

• The to-be-marketed formulation and device were used the phase 3 CWM trials, and are 
identical to the formulation and device currently approved for T2D under NDA 215866, 
so no bridging studies were necessary.

• The proposed tirzepatide dosing and dose escalation strategies for CWM (currently 
approved for T2D under NDA 215866) were supported by phase 2 trials GPGB and GPGF. 
The dose escalation strategy used in phase 3 (starting at 2.5 mg with titration every 4 
weeks) reduced discontinuations due to GI adverse events compared to more rapid 
dose escalations.

• No dedicated thorough QT study was performed, as detailed in Section 8.4.9. 
Concentration-QT analyses using ECG readings from phase 1, 2, and 3 trials did not 
indicate any unexpected or important effects of tirzepatide on the QTc interval at 
clinically relevant exposures associated with the proposed dosages (up to 15 mg once 
weekly).

• Immunogenicity (anti-drug antibody [ADA] status and titer) did not have apparent effect 
on the PK of tirzepatide (see Section 8.4.10).

• Handling of missed dose was assessed by the population PK analysis in cross-referenced 
data from T2D NDA 215866, supporting the Applicant’s proposal to administer 
tirzepatide as soon as possible within 4 days (96 hours) of a missed dose, or otherwise 
to skip the dose (if more than 4 days has passed) before resuming the regular weekly 
regimen.

since the tirzepatide auto-injector device is the same as approved under tirzepatide T2D NDA 
215866, originally developed by the Applicant for its GLP-1R agonist dulaglutide (Trulicity, BLA

Under tirzepatide NDA 215866, Dr. Sreya Tarafdar, from the CDRH Division of Health Technology 3 C (Division of Drug Delivery, General Hospital & Human Factors) provided a consult review dated January 31, 2022.<sup>14</sup> Overall, this CDRH review considered the following to be acceptable: device description, labeling (contains all required elements), design controls, risk analysis, design verification, clinical validation (no AEs reported that would indicate a device issue), and facilities and quality systems. There also were no outstanding deficiencies for this Application from the device perspective, and CDRH did not recommend any Postmarketing Commitments (PMCs) or Requirements (PMRs) under the T2D NDA 215866.

The auto-injector safety features include a locking mechanism to prevent inadvertent
activation, a first audible click when the button is pressed for activation, needle insertion to SC
depth, automatic delivery of drug product, a retraction delay to ensure complete dose delivery, 
automatic needle retraction with a second audible click for injection completion, and a 
retraction lock to prevent re-activation. The instructions for use call for pressing and holding
the injection button for up to 10 seconds, though they note that holding is not necessary except 
to help keep the auto-injector steady. Under NDA 215866, Dr. Tarafdar of CDRH felt that the 
device constituent parts of the combination product were approvable and recommended 
approval.

## Consumer Study Reviews

For this tirzepatide NDA for CWM (Zepbound), the Applicant proposed to leverage human 
factors validation data from the currently approved tirzepatide for T2D (Mounjaro) under NDA 
215866.<sup>15</sup> Based on FDA feedback from the CWM pre-NDA meeting under IND 139721, the 
Applicant submitted with the NDA first sequence on November 17, 2022, a use-related risk 
analysis (URRA) and justification not to submit results of a human factors validation study. 
FDA’s Division of Medication Error Prevention and Analysis 1 (DMEPA 1) provided a URRA
review from the human factors evaluator Dr. Neha Kumar, dated May 9, 2023. This review 
agreed with the sponsor’s justification, noting that:

• Physical characteristics did not differ between Zepbound and Mounjaro, with the same 
auto-injector pen, drug product, and strengths.

• User tasks did not differ between Zepbound and Mounjaro.

• Instructions for use did not differ between Zepbound and Mounjaro, except for the 
brand name text.

• No differences were identified between the user groups for Zepbound versus Mounjaro 
that would impact the proposed intended users’ interactions with the user interface. 
Intended patient users of Zepbound did not differ meaningfully from Mounjaro users in 
characteristics of vision, hearing, physical strength, tactile sensitivity, cognitive abilities, 
mental and emotional state, handedness, dexterity characteristics, and comorbidities.

• The human factors study for Mounjaro included subjects with T2D and overweight or 
obesity.

Since these human factors studies were completed, post-marketing reports identified a 
potential user error with the auto-injector, in which some patients used the device upside 
down with the needle facing their thumb and the button facing the intended injection site. 
DMEPA recommended that the Applicant include a “Needle End” phrase on the pen container 
label for tirzepatide, and the Applicant agreed.

<sup>15</sup>Review of NDA 215866,

https://www.accessdata.fda.gov/drugsatfda_docs/nda/2022/215866Orig1s000OtherR.pdf

---

In conclusion, the DMEPA review of this NDA’s URRA did not identify any new, differing, or 
unique risks for the proposed product, and agreed with the Applicant’s justification for not 
submitting the results from another human factors validation study with their NDA for the 
proposed Zepbound (tirzepatide) auto-injector. Besides the labeling of the auto-injector pen as 
described above, DMEPA had no human factors recommendations for this marketing 
application.

### 5.2. Review Strategy

I reviewed all the information contained in this document.

I focused my efficacy assessment on the two phase 3 CWM clinical trials GPHK and GPHL. In this 
review, I mainly present the Applicant’s efficacy results, noting where relevant when the FDA 
Office of Biostatistics (the primary statistical review by Dr. Kyunghee Song dated October 10, 
2023) confirmed or supplemented the analyses. To assess the weight loss of efficacy of the 
tirzepatide 5 mg weekly dosage in subjects with T2D (since this maintenance dosage was not 
evaluated in trial GPHL, see Section 6 below), I also reviewed the weight data in the T2D 
SURPASS 1-5 trials under NDA 215866. The efficacy review also references some analyses and 
assessments performed by reviewers from the DCOA staff. My review focused primarily on the 
prespecified primary and key secondary endpoints, trial endpoints that informed the 
assessment of benefit-risk, and those endpoints being considered for inclusion in product 
labeling.

My strategy for safety review is detailed in Section 8.1. Briefly, my primary focus for safety 
review was the two phase 3 CWM trials GPHK and GPHL, which were reviewed both individually 
and pooled. To confirm and expand upon the Applicant’s safety results, I conducted safety 
analyses myself using the Applicant’s datasets. I also considered the Applicant’s data and 
results from subjects with BMI ≥27 kg/m<sup>2</sup> in their T2D phase 2/3 trials. Blinded data (including 
the Applicant’s 120-day safety update) and post-marketing information were reviewed in 
selected sections of the safety review. In some cases, the safety review references analyses and 
assessments conducted by reviewers from other FDA offices and divisions, including OBP, OCP, 
Office of Surveillance and Epidemiology (OSE), DPT, and Division of Pediatrics and Maternal 
Heath (DPMH).

## 6. Review of Relevant Individual Trials Used to Support Efficacy

This NDA includes two adequate and well-controlled trials intended to show substantial 
evidence of effectiveness of tirzepatide for CWM. Trials GPHK (SURMOUNT-1) and GPHL 
(SURMOUNT-2) are similar in design with two key differences:

• SURMOUNT-1 excluded subjects with diabetes and SURMOUNT-2 exclusively enrolled 
subjects with T2D.

• SURMOUNT-1 had four arms (placebo and tirzepatide 5, 10, and 15 mg weekly), while 
SURMOUNT-2 had three arms (placebo and tirzepatide 10 mg and 15 mg weekly), not 
studying the 5 mg dosage.

Since these two trials are otherwise similar in design, eligibility criteria, and endpoints, they will 
be described concurrently in Section 6.1, rather than in individual sections, to avoid 
unnecessary repetition.

---

## Trials 18F-MC-GPHK and 18F-MC-GPHL (SURMOUNT-1 and -2)

## 6.1.1. Study Design

## Overview and Objective

## Trial GPHK

Full Title: Efficacy and Safety of Tirzepatide Once Weekly in Participants without Type 2 
Diabetes Who Have Obesity or Are Overweight with Weight-Related Comorbidities: A 
Randomized, Double-Blind, Placebo-Controlled Trial (SURMOUNT-1)

Short Title: Efficacy and Safety of Tirzepatide Once Weekly Versus Placebo in Participants Who 
are either Obese or Overweight with Weight-Related Comorbidities (SURMOUNT-1)

Trial GPHK is the larger of the two phase 3 safety and efficacy trials in this NDA, designed to 
demonstrate that tirzepatide administered SC at dosages of 5 mg, 10 mg, or 15 mg once-weekly 
as an adjunct to reduced-calorie diet and increased physical activity, is effective (reduces body 
weight) and safe for the treatment of patients with overweight or obesity. Secondary objectives 
were to assess the effects of tirzepatide on cardiovascular risk factors (lipids, blood pressure, 
glucose metabolism), clinical outcome assessments, and other factors related to body weight.

## Trial GPHL

Full Title: Efficacy and Safety of Tirzepatide Once Weekly in Participants with Type 2 Diabetes 
Who Have Obesity or Are Overweight: A Randomized, Double-Blind, Placebo-Controlled Trial 
(SURMOUNT-2)

Short Title: Effect of Tirzepatide versus Placebo in Participants with Type 2 Diabetes Who Have 
Obesity or Are Overweight (SURMOUNT-2)

Trial GPHL shares objectives with GPHL, except in an overweight/obesity population exclusively 
with T2D, and not testing the tirzepatide 5 mg weekly dosage.

## Trial Design

<u>Basic study design:</u> Trial GPHK included a 72-week randomized, double-blinded, four-arm, 
placebo-controlled main phase (Figure 2) and a 104-week treatment extension for subjects with 
prediabetes at baseline. Only the 72-week main phase was included in this NDA, as the 
extension phase is ongoing. The design of the 72-week main phase is typical for CWM trials 
(while the extension proposes to address progression of prediabetes to diabetes).

Trial GPHK also included two sub-studies (reviewed in Section 8.7):

• The Dual-Energy X-ray Absorptiometry (DXA) Addendum involved body composition 
DXA scans performed at Baseline and Week 72 (or early termination) in a subset of 
subjects.

• The ABPM addendum involved 24-hour monitoring at Baseline and Week 36 in a subset 
of subjects.

Trial GPHL has a similar design (Figure 3) to the 72-week main phase of GPHK, without the substudies or 2-year treatment extension.

Trial locations: Both trials GPHK and GPHL were multicenter, multinational trials with sites 
primarily in the United States (U.S.), Central and South America including Mexico, and Asia. 
Although some sites were outside the U.S., the results are applicable to the U.S. population as 
described in Section 6.1.2.

<u>Choice of control group:</u> In trials GPHK and GPHL, a placebo control arm is reasonable and 
typical for current CWM trials to support approval.

<u>Diagnostic criteria:</u> Both trials used standard definitions of obesity (BMI ≥30 kg/m<sup>2</sup>) and 
overweight (BMI ≥27 kg/m<sup>2</sup>) with weight-related complications for enrollment. In trial GPHL, 
the definition of T2D diagnosis for enrollment relies on World Health Organization (WHO) 
classification or other locally applicable standards.

$$
(\mathrm{BM}\mid\geq30\mathrm{kg/m^2})
$$

$$
(\mathrm{BM}\mid\geq27\mathrm{kg/m^2})
$$

<u>Key inclusion/exclusion criteria:</u> As noted above, the key difference between trials GPHK and 
GPHL is the exclusion or inclusion, respectively, of subjects with T2D: subjects with T2D were 
ineligible in GPHK (though subjects with prediabetes were allowed) and only subjects with T2D 
were eligible in GPHL. Both trials excluded subjects with type 1 or other forms of diabetes.

Both trials GPHK and GPHL enrolled subjects with obesity (BMI≥30 kg/m<sup>2</sup>). Trial GPHK also 
enrolled overweight (BMI ≥27 and <30 kg/m<sup>2</sup>) subjects with one or more weight-related 
comorbidities (hypertension, dyslipidemia, OSA, or cardiovascular disease), while any subject

$$
(\mathrm{BMI}\geq30\mathrm{kg}/\mathrm{m}^{2})
$$

$$
<30kg/m^2)
$$

---

Clinical Review
was eligible for trial GPHL since T2D is a weight-related comorbidity.

Trial GPHL included subjects with well-controlled T2D only (HbA1c 7-10%, inclusive, no recent 
severe hypoglycemia or hyperglycemia) treated by lifestyle alone or with any oral 
antihyperglycemic drugs, excluding DPP-4 inhibitors, insulin, and any oral or injectable GLP-1R 
agonist. A dilated fundoscopic exam was required during the screening period of trial GPHL to 
exclude those with proliferative diabetic retinopathy, diabetic macular edema, or 
nonproliferative diabetic retinopathy requiring acute treatment.

Both trials enrolled adult (age ≥18 years) subjects, with male and female subjects using defined 
and appropriate contraception as necessary to avoid pregnancy in women of child-bearing 
potential who are subjects or partners of male subjects.

Key exclusions in both trials were typical for CWM trials:

• secondary causes of obesity or weight gain (endocrine, monogenic, syndromic, certain 
medications, tetrahydrocannabinol [THC]), or abnormal TSH

• change in body weight >5 kg within 3 months of screening, or prior/planned surgical, 
endoscopic, or device treatment for obesity

• renal impairment (estimated glomerular filtration rate [eGFR] <30 mL/min/1.73m<sup>2</sup>)

• uncontrolled hypertension, congestive heart failure (NYHA class IV), heart attack or 
stroke within 3 months

• active or untreated malignancy (except for defined low-risk cancers), or remission for 
less than 5 years

• chronic hepatitis or abnormal liver chemistry (alanine transaminase [ALT] >3x ULN, 
though NAFLD was allowed)

• active or unstable severe psychiatric disorder within 2 years, lifetime history of suicide 
attempt, abnormal scores on standard psychiatric screen instruments (detailed in 
Section 8.5.9 below), diagnosed eating disorder, or substance abuse

• hematologic conditions that could interfere with HbA1c measurement (such as 
hemolytic or sickle cell anemia)

Both trials also have typical exclusions for incretins based on known safety issues detailed in 
Section 8.5 below:

• severe GI disease including gastroparesis

• history of pancreatitis

• elevated calcitonin, or personal or family history of MTC or multiple endocrine neoplasia 
type 2 (MEN2, a rare familial cancer syndrome that includes MTC).

In general, these exclusions are appropriate and currently standard for CWM trials. A small 
minority of the treatment population for obesity in the U.S. is likely excluded from this trial 
population (for instance, due to the renal, psychiatric, liver, and pancreatic criteria).

---

<u>Dose selection:</u> The Applicant justifies dose selection in both trials based on exposure-response 
modeling in phase 1 and 2 studies to predict weight loss. In general, dose selection for incretins
balances efficacy versus GI tolerability, and dose selection for tirzepatide in these trials appears 
reasonable, with a sufficiently broad ranges of dosages studied in early phase trials.

The 5 mg weekly dosage was studied in trial GPHK and in the development of tirzepatide for 
T2D, but not in trial GPHL, which the Applicant justifies since this dosage was considered 
unlikely to address the unmet need for >10% weight loss in patients with T2D (note also that 
patients with T2D can already take approved tirzepatide at this dosage for glycemic control).

<u>Study treatments:</u> In both trials, tirzepatide was administered SC once weekly, with doseescalation for improved GI tolerability, starting at 2.5 mg weekly and increasing the dose by 2.5 
mg every four weeks (to 5, 7.5, 10, 12.5, and 15 mg). Therefore, in the three treatment arms, 
the maintenance dosages of 5 mg, 10 mg, and 15 mg weekly were reached at Week 4, Week 12, 
and Week 20, respectively (Table 3). This dose escalation was developed in early phase studies 
of tirzepatide, notably phase 2 trial GPGF to investigate the impact of different starting doses 
and escalation regimens on tolerability. Subjects were also instructed at randomization on 
study drug administration via auto-injector device (single dose prefilled pen).

**Table 3. Tirzepatide Dose Escalation Schedule in Trials GPHK and GPHL**

| Treatment Group | Treatment Period Intervals Weeks 0 to 4 | Treatment Period Intervals Weeks 4 to 8 | Treatment Period Intervals Weeks 8 to 12 | Treatment Period Intervals Weeks 12 to 16 | Treatment Period Intervals Weeks 16 to 20 | Treatment Period Intervals Week 20 through End of Treatment Period |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| TZP 5 mga | 2.5 mg | 5 mg | 5 mg | 5 mg | 5 mg | 5 mg |
| TZP 10 mg | 2.5 mg | 5 mg | 7.5 mg | 10 mg | 10 mg | 10 mg |
| TZP 15 mg | 2.5 mg | 5 mg | 7.5 mg | 10 mg | 12.5 mg | 15 mg |

**_Reviewer comment: Note the 2.5, 7.5, and 12.5 mg dosages were only used for 4-week 
periods during dose escalation and were not used for maintenance, nor for dose reduction as 
described below. This trial design element has implications for Section 2 of labeling (Dosage 
and Administration), as described in Section 10 below._**

<u>Dietary restrictions/instructions:</u> Tirzepatide was developed for CWM as an add-on treatment 
to a reduced calorie diet and increased physical activity. All subjects received an initial 
consultation with a qualified dietician to set lifestyle goals (hypocaloric diet with energy deficit 
of 500 kcal/day based on estimated total expenditure, physical activity ≥150 minutes per week)

---

Clinical Review
Raymond Soccio, MD, PhD
NDA 217806
Zepbound (tirzepatide)

that were reinforced by the dietician during study visits. For all study visits, subjects were to be 
fasting for approximately 8 hours without eating or drinking (except water), for accurate 
measurement of body weight and fasting laboratory values (such as lipids, glucose, insulin, and 
fatty acids). These dietary instructions are reasonable.

<u>Assignment to treatment:</u> Randomization procedures involved a computer-generated random 
sequence via an interactive web response system (IWRS). In trial GPHK, randomization was 
1:1:1:1 to placebo or one of the three tirzepatide arms (5, 10, or 15 mg), stratified by country, 
sex, and prediabetes status. Trial GPHL randomized subjects 1:1:1 to placebo or tirzepatide 10 
or 15 mg, stratified by country, sex, and types of medication(s) used for T2D (categorized by 
potential impact on weight):

• significant weight loss: SGLT2 inhibitor alone or combined with any except a 
sulfonylurea or thiazolidinedione (TZD)

• significant weight gain: sulfonylurea alone or combined with any except an SGLT2 
inhibitor, or TZD alone or combined with any except an SGLT2 inhibitor

• weight neutral or others: lifestyle only, metformin monotherapy, or other combinations 
not above.

**_Reviewer comment: This stratification based on the anticipated weight effects of 
antihyperglycemic medications is reasonable to help ensure that background therapy is not 
confounding the weight effects of tirzepatide in subjects with T2D. There is still potential for 
confounding due to initiation of rescue therapy for hyperglycemia, as described below._**

<u>Blinding:</u> Investigators, site staff, clinical monitors, and participants were blinded to the 
treatment assignments until the trial was complete. A limited number of Applicant individuals 
were unblinded to treatment assignments as needed for interim safety analyses provided to the 
external DMC, but this unblinded information was not communicated to study sites or the 
blinded study team unless necessary for subject safety.

Placebo was administered in the same auto-injector with a sham dose escalation to ensure 
blinding. There is potential for unblinding of subjects and investigators based on GI tolerability 
issues typical of tirzepatide and other incretin mimetic drugs, glycemic control in subjects with 
T2D (trial GPHL), and weight loss (which, unlike lab-based endpoints, may be directly observed 
by subjects). Nevertheless, because the primary endpoint of body weight reduction is based on 
objective scale-based measurements in clinic, any observed treatment effect is unlikely to be
susceptible to bias from unblinding.

<u>Dose modification or discontinuation:</u> Both trial protocols pre-specified detailed procedures for 
dose interruption, reduction, and discontinuation.

For management of subjects with intolerable GI symptoms, prior to dose changes subjects were 
initially counselled on dietary behaviors (i.e., smaller meals) and could be prescribed

---

Clinical Review
Raymond Soccio, MD, PhD
NDA 217806
Zepbound (tirzepatide)

symptomatic medications (antiemetic or antidiarrheal). Temporary interruptions of one dose 
were allowed for subjects who had taken the previous three weekly doses, with resumption of 
the assigned dose. If intolerable GI symptoms persisted, study drug could be resumed at the 
next-lowest maintenance dose (15 to 10 mg, 10 mg to 5 mg, 5 mg to placebo) in blinded 
manner, with only one such dose reduction allowed per subject in the entire study. These 
procedures are reasonable and justified by known GI tolerability issues for tirzepatide and 
incretin mimetics.

The trial protocol addressed other temporary interruptions (due to subject nonadherence, 
investigator judgement, or AEs), with resumption of the last administered dose (maintenance 
dose or per escalation schedule) if ≤2 doses were missed, and if ≥3 doses were missed 
restarting at 5 mg and repeating the blinded dose escalation.

The criteria for permanent study discontinuation were reasonable and included:

• diagnoses of drug hypersensitivity, type 1 diabetes, MTC, pancreatitis, malignancy, or 
pregnancy

• predefined elevations in serum calcitonin

• BMI ≤18.5 kg/m<sup>2</sup> reached during treatment

• Serious or significant AEs

• Suicidal ideation or behavior

• Liver events or blood test abnormalities (with detailed procedures for data collection 
and follow-up).

<u>Administrative structure:</u> Trial GPHK (but not GPHL) included an independent data monitoring 
committee (DMC) to review unblinded interim safety results. Both trials used a clinical events 
committee (CEC) to adjudicate deaths, MACE events, and pancreatitis.

<u>Procedures and schedule:</u> Trials GPHK (72-week main phase) and GPHL each included:

• a two-week screening period

• a 72-week primary treatment period, with in-person visits at Weeks 4, 8, 16, 20, 24, 36, 
48, 60, and 72, and with early discontinuation if necessary. There were also telephone 
visits scheduled at Weeks 28, 32, 40, 44, 52, 56, 64, and 68.

• a 4-week treatment withdrawal period, with one safety follow-up visit 4 weeks after 
the last dose of study drug to account for the long half-life

In trial GPHK, subjects with prediabetes at baseline continued for an additional 2-year 
treatment period and this extension is ongoing, so 4-week safety follow-up for these subjects 
was not part of the current application.

<u>Concurrent medications:</u> As noted above, both trials excluded subjects on medications 
associated with weight gain (except for weight gain-associated medications for T2D allowed in 
trial GPHL but part of the stratification scheme), including systemic glucocorticoids and certain 
tricyclic antidepressants, atypical antipsychotics, and mood stabilizers used within 3 months of

---

Clinical Review
Raymond Soccio, MD, PhD
NDA 217806
Zepbound (tirzepatide)

screening. Medications for weight loss (including other incretins, stimulants, orlistat, 
phentermine/topiramate, and bupropion/naltrexone) were also not permitted within 3 months 
of screening or during the study period.

In trial GPHK, metformin or any other glucose-lowering medications (even if used for other 
reasons) were not permitted, thus excluding other GLP-1R agonists, DPP4 inhibitors, and SGLT2 
inhibitors.

Trial GPHL allowed oral glucose-lowering medications for T2D as detailed above in 
inclusions/exclusions, with insulin secretagogues like sulfonylureas having their doses halved at 
randomization (or discontinued if at the lowest dose) to minimize risk of hypoglycemia.

Treatment compliance: Compliance was assessed at each study in-person and telephone visit, 
with questioning and counting of unused study drug or empty cartons returned.

<u>Rescue medication:</u> Rescue medications for weight management were not permitted in either 
trial.

In trial GPHK, subjects who developed T2D during the study could initiate medications for 
glucose control with their usual care provider, including metformin but excluding GLP-1R 
agonists or DPP-4 inhibitors.

Since tirzepatide can cause hypoglycemia, all subjects in trial GPHL and those in GPHK who 
developed T2D during the trial were provided with a glucometer, glucose log, and device 
training, and educated on hypoglycemia signs, symptoms, and treatment.

In trial GPHL, new antihyperglycemic medications or dose adjustment of existing medication 
were rescue therapy for severe, persistent hyperglycemia, defined appropriately in the protocol 
depending on the time in the treatment period:

• 2 confirmed glucose values >270 mg/dl over 2 weeks during Weeks 0-8

• 2 confirmed glucose values >240 mg/dl over 2 weeks during Weeks 9-16

• 2 confirmed glucose values >200 mg/dl over 2 weeks during Week 16 or later

• HbA1c≥8.5% during Week 24 or later.

Rescue therapy was at the discretion of the investigator or usual care provider (excluding GLP-
1R agonists, DPP-4 inhibitors, or amylin analogues), with metformin recommended for subjects 
initially treated with lifestyle only and basal insulin considered for subjects already receiving ≥3 
oral medications.

<u>Subject completion, discontinuation, or withdrawal:</u> The primary analysis in both trials was 
performed in a mITT population of all randomized subjects who receive at least one dose of 
study drug, regardless of whether there was discontinuation from the study drug or the study. 
Withdrawn subjects were not replaced. There were adequate procedures to assure safety

<u>Study Design Conclusion:</u> Overall, trials GPHK and GPHL were robustly designed, consistent with 
the Division’s guidance and advice, to yield interpretable study results.

## Study Endpoints

Table 4 summarizes the primary, key secondary, and secondary endpoints (abbreviated P, KS, 
and S, respectively) in trials GPHK and GPHL. The endpoints differ by tirzepatide dosage arms, 
with some endpoints tested in individual dosage arms and others in pooled dosage arms. These 
endpoints in the two individual trials are defined and further detailed below.

---

## Trial GPHK

In trial GPHK, the co-primary efficacy endpoints tested for superiority of tirzepatide 10 mg to 
placebo and/or tirzepatide 15 mg to placebo:

• Percent change in body weight from randomization to Week 72

• Proportion of subjects achieving a weight loss of ≥ 5% from randomization to Week 72.

Key secondary endpoints controlled for Type I error (for the primary 72-week treatment period) 
fell into 4 classes:

• Superiority of tirzepatide 5 mg to placebo for the two Week 72 endpoints above

• Superiority of tirzepatide 10 mg and/or 15 mg doses to placebo for:

o Proportion of subjects achieving a weight loss of ≥10%, ≥15%, or ≥20% from 
randomization to Week 72 o Change in waist circumference (cm) from randomization to Week 72

• Pooled dose analyses for tirzepatide 10 mg and 15 mg versus placebo:

o Percent change in body weight from randomization to Week 20

o Change is SF-36v2 acute form Physical Functioning domain score from 
randomization to Week 72

• Pooled dose analyses for all tirzepatide doses versus placebo, changes from 
randomization to Week 72 in:

o Lipid parameters (mg/dL):  triglycerides, non-HDL and HDL cholesterol

o Systolic blood pressure (mmHg)

o Fasting insulin (pmol/L)

Additional secondary endpoints not controlled for Type I error included:

• Superiority of tirzepatide 5 mg to placebo for other Week 72 endpoints not tested 
above:

o Proportion of subjects achieving a weight loss of ≥10% or ≥15% (note that ≥20% 
was not tested for this dosage)

o Change in waist circumference

o Change in SF-36v2 acute form Physical Functioning domain score

• Superiority of tirzepatide 5 mg, 10 mg and/or 15 mg doses to placebo for:

o Body weight (kg) and BMI (kg/m<sup>2</sup>)

o HbA1c (%) and fasting glucose (mg/dL)

o IWQOL-Lite-CT Physical Function composite score

• Pooled analyses of all tirzepatide doses versus placebo, changes from randomization to 
Week 72 in:

o Diastolic blood pressure

o Additional lipid parameters (mg/dL): free fatty acids; total, LDL, and VLDL 
cholesterol

• Population PK and PD parameters

## Trial GPHL

In trial GPHL, all endpoints were from randomization to Week 72. The co-primary efficacy 
endpoints tested for superiority of tirzepatide 10 mg to placebo and/or tirzepatide 15 mg to 
placebo:

• Percent change in body weight

• Proportion of subjects achieving a weight loss of ≥ 5%.

Key secondary endpoints controlled for Type I error fell into 3 classes:

• Superiority of tirzepatide 10 mg and/or 15 mg doses to placebo for:

o Proportion of subjects achieving a weight loss of ≥10%, ≥15%, or ≥20%

o Change in waist circumference (cm)

o Change in HbA1c (%) and fasting glucose (mg/dL)

o Proportion of subjects achieving HbA1c <7%, ≤6.5%, or <5.7%

---

• Pooled dose analyses for tirzepatide 10 mg and 15 mg versus placebo, change in:

o Lipid parameters (mg/dL):  triglycerides, non-HDL and HDL cholesterol

o Systolic blood pressure (mmHg)

Additional secondary endpoints not controlled for Type I error included changes in:

• Superiority of tirzepatide 10 mg and/or 15 mg doses to placebo for:

o Body weight (kg) and BMI (kg/m<sup>2</sup>)

o 7-point self-monitored blood glucose profiles

o Fasting insulin (pmol/L)

o SF-36v2 acute form Physical Functioning domain score

o IWQOL-Lite-CT Physical Function composite score

o EQ-5D-5L utility and VAS scores

• Pooled dose analyses for tirzepatide 10 mg and 15 mg versus placebo, changes in:
o Diastolic blood pressure

o Additional lipid parameters (mg/dL): free fatty acids; total, LDL, and VLDL 
cholesterol

• Population PK and PD parameters

## Endpoints conclusion

The choice of endpoints and their timing at Week 72 (after 52 weeks at the highest 15 mg 
tirzepatide maintenance dosage) is consistent with FDA guidance<sup>16</sup> and the Division’s 
expectations. Body weight (reported as % change from baseline) has been used previously as a 
basis for full approval, and other endpoints on glycemia, lipids, blood pressure, and physical 
function are supportive.

The physical function endpoints were measured by patient-reported outcome (PRO) 
instruments (see details in Section 6.1.2 below), and the FDA DCOA was consulted to assist in 
review of these.

## Reviewer comment: As discussed below in Section 10, there are potential labeling

**_implications to the Applicant’s choice to treat the 5 mg dosage differently from the 10 and 15 
mg dosages for statistical testing of endpoints in trial GPHK, and to exclude this 5 mg dosage 
from trial GPHL._**

## Statistical Analysis Plan

The SAPs for both trials were finalized before results were known. In trial GPHK, the sample size 
calculation for 2,400 randomized subjects (600 each to the four arms) assumed ≥11% placebo-

<sup>16</sup>FDA Guidance for Industry “Developing Products for Weight Management,” 2007 draft guidance

---

Clinical Review
Raymond Soccio, MD, PhD
NDA 217806
Zepbound (tirzepatide)

corrected body weight loss on tirzepatide 10 mg or 15 mg at Week 72, with a common standard 
deviation of 10% and dropout rate of 10%, giving >90% power to show superiority to placebo. 
In trial GPHK, the sample size calculation for 900 subjects (randomized 300 each to the two 
arms) had the same assumptions, giving >90% power to show superiority to placebo.

In both trials, the Applicant conducted analyses for all primary and key secondary efficacy 
endpoints at Week 72 using two different estimands:

• For submission to FDA, the Applicant used a “hybrid” estimand, defined as the average 
treatment effect of tirzepatide relative to placebo at Week 72, as an adjunct to a 
reduced-calorie diet and increased physical for the randomized participants regardless 
of the adherence to study drug.

• For other regulatory agencies, publications, and other purposes, the Applicant also used 
an “efficacy” estimand, defined as the average treatment effect of tirzepatide relative to 
placebo at Week 72, as an adjunct to a reduced-calorie diet and increased physical 
activity, in the randomized participants had they remained on their randomized 
treatment for the entire planned 72-week treatment duration.

The hybrid estimand in an ITT population is consistent with FDA expectation that the analysis 
includes all randomized subjects without consideration of adherence or intercurrent events.

Please refer to the statistical review by Dr. Kyunghee Song dated October 10, 2023, for a 
detailed discussion of the Statistical Analysis Plans (SAPs), including the multiplicity correction 
and approaches to missing data.

## Protocol Amendments

Trial GPHK had one protocol amendment, dated after the first subject visit (December 4, 2019), 
that was relevant to this submission. Amendment (b), dated October 4, 2021, modified the key 
and additional secondary endpoints, mainly changing the analyses by individual dosages versus 
by pooled dosages and adding the endpoint of ≥20% body weight reduction (see Table 4 above, 
which reflects the final endpoints from this amendment). These minor changes to key 
secondary endpoints do not impact the integrity of the trial or my interpretation of results.

Trial GPHK had no protocol amendments, and the 3 addenda (to address local regulations at 
non-U.S. trial sites in India, Brazil, and Taiwan) were considered non-substantial by the 
Applicant and I agree. However, I noted that some secondary endpoints had minor differences 
in the trial GPHK CSR compared to the protocol, mainly to make trial GPHL endpoints align with 
those in GPHK (adding the ≥20% weight loss responder analysis and allocating lipid parameters 
differently to key and additional secondary endpoints) and add diabetes-related endpoints (two 
lower HbA1c responder analysis thresholds and another PRO). As above for trial GPHK, these 
minor changes to secondary endpoints do not affect trial integrity or interpretation.

## Compliance with Good Clinical Practices

The Applicant attests that trials GPHK and GPHL were conducted in accordance with the
protocols, and consensus ethical principles derived from international guidelines, including the
Declaration of Helsinki and Council for International Organizations of Medical Sciences
International Ethical Guidelines, applicable International Council for Harmonisation (ICH) GCP 
guidelines, and applicable laws and regulations.

Two trials sites for GPHK and GPHL had known or potential GCP noncompliance:

• Site 708 in India had randomized 10 subjects in trial GPHK, and 2 were ongoing, 
presumably in the prediabetes extension study since the primary study period is 
(b) (4), (b) (7)(A)
completed. The site was closed due to GCP noncompliance,

• One site in Japan contracting with the contract research organization<sup>(b) (4)</sup> 
(b) (4), (b) (7)(A)
had suspected GCP violations  This site had 22 
randomized subjects in trial GPHK (site 803) and 26 subjects in trial GPHL (site 23220).

The Applicant performed a sensitivity analysis of each trial’s primary efficacy endpoints
excluding the sites with GCP noncompliance, as detailed below (see below in Section 6.1.2, 
Data Quality and Integrity).

## Financial Disclosure

The Applicant submitted Form FDA 3455 reports as detailed in Appendix 13.2 below. For trial 
GPHK, 7 of 700 investigators (1.0%) reported financial interests/arrangements (mean $98,194, 
ranging from $36,619 to $150,041), and these investigators enrolled 166 subjects out of 2539 
randomized (4.8%). For trial GPHL, 3 of 410 investigators (0.7%) reported financial 
interests/arrangements (mean $83,858, range $40,000 to $120,00), and these investigators 
enrolled 166 subjects out of 938 randomized (6.5%). These potential conflicts of interest do not 
raise significant concerns to me, since the contributions of the investigators with financial 
issues to overall study data was small, and the study design (randomized, blinded, with 
objective endpoints) minimized potential bias. There was no undue bias or influence that could 
affect the outcome of this trial or the review of this NDA.

Reviewer comment: Overall discontinuations were fewer in trial GPHL (subjects with T2D) than in trial GPHK (subjects without T2D). Withdrawal by subjects in placebo arms of CWM trials is expected due to lack of efficacy, while discontinuations due to adverse events are expected in treatment arms for incretins due to GI intolerance (see Section 8.4.3 and 8.5.2 below). Overall discontinuation rates in these trials are less than the Applicant's estimate of 25% for power calculations, and less than that observed in most previous CWM trials, so the rates of discontinuation do not raise concerns about trial integrity.

For both trials GPHK and GPHL, all randomized subjects were included in each prespecified analysis set (Table 7). Therefore, no subjects were inappropriately excluded from analyses.

## Protocol Violations/Deviations

Important protocol deviations were assessed and captured in the Trial Issues Management Plan. For both trials GPHK and GPHL, important deviations appeared to be randomly distributed across treatment arms without any obvious or relevant trends. The Applicant concludes that, due to the limited number and similar distribution across the treatment groups, these protocol deviations were not likely to impact the analyses or conclusions presented in the clinical study reports (CSRs) for the respective trials. I concur that the reported important protocol deviations detailed below are unlikely to have affected the overall conclusions for trials GPHK and GPHL.

Table 8 summarizes the important protocol deviations in trial GPHK.

Table 8. Important Protocol Deviations in Trial GPHK

|  | Placebo | TZP 5mg | TZP 10mg | TZP 15mg | Total |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  | N=643 | N=630 | N=636 | N=630 | N=2539 |
| Subjects with ≥ 1 Important Protocol Deviation | 99 (15.4) | 82 (13.0) | 84 (13.2) | 90 (14.3) | 355 (14.0) |
| Eligibility (inclusion/exclusion deviations) | 16 (2.5) | 17 (2.7) | 24 (3.8) | 27 (4.3) | 84 (3.3) |
| Informed consent | 3 (0.5) | 0 (0.0) | 1 (0.0) | 1 (0.2) | 4 (0.2) |
| Investigational Product | 58 (9.0) | 55 (8.7) | 54 (8.5) | 61 (9.7) | 228 (9.0) |
| Safety | 4 (0.6) | 5 (0.8) | 5 (0.8) | 5 (0.8) | 19 (0.7) |
| Study procedures | 24 (3.7) | 12 (1.9) | 5 (0.8) | 5 (0.8) | 46 (1.8) |

Source: Generated by reviewer based on Applicant GPHK CSR, Table GPHK.4.2, page 64 of 3021

These protocol violations in trial GPHK are divided into 5 categories:

- Eligibility: 84 subjects (3.3%) overall

28 were related to the main study exclusion criteria, including diabetes at baseline (3), mental health exclusions (6), use of weight gain medications (5), and others not specified (10). 6 subjects reported for pregnancy exclusions in this category were not pregnant during the study, but instead they were not on required contraception prior to randomization.

o 56 were related to the ABPM substudy, which required BP <140/90 mmHg for 
enrollment, but these subjects were inadvertently enrolled based on the main 
trial eligibility (BP <160/100 mmHg). Thus, these subjects with SBP up 20 mmHg 
higher than intended and/or DBP up to 10 mmHg higher than intended 
participated in the ABPM substudy. I agree with Applicant that this was unlikely 
to affect subject safety or results of the ABPM substudy.

• Informed consent: 4 subjects (0.2%) overall, with one having informed consent not 
obtained, requiring site re-training.

• Investigational Product: 228 subjects (9.0%) overall

o 151 subjects had incorrect stratification, 84 of them incorrectly stratified as 
having prediabetes and 39 as having normoglycemia at randomization. This error 
was corrected by changing the glycemic status, allowing subjects with 
prediabetes to proceed to the 2-year extension treatment period. 26 patients 
were incorrectly stratified in the ABPM study (for using or not using heart rate 
controlling medications), and this error was corrected for the analysis.

o 33 subjects had low compliance (<75% through 72 weeks)

o 38 subjects had dosing errors (two or more study drug injections within 24 
hours, indicating potential overdose, see Section 8.8.4)

o Other deviations in this category were rare (3 dispensing error, 6 taking 
medication not fit for use)

• Safety: 19 subjects (0.7%) overall

o 17 subjects for pregnancy during the study, including 16 female subjects (who all 
discontinued study drug) and 1 female partner of a male subject. 3 additional 
pregnancies in the trial were not captured as protocol deviations.

o There was 1 delay in reporting a serious event (>24 hours) and 1 other 
unspecified deviation in this category.

• Study procedures: 46 subjects (1.8%) overall

o These mainly involved prohibited concomitant therapies: 6 subjects used 
metformin without a diagnosis of T2D, 14 subjects used weight gain-promoting 
medications, 4 subjects used weight-loss medications (3 in the placebo group 
and 1 in the tirzepatide 10 mg group), 6 subjects used GLP-1R agonists 
(liraglutide or semaglutide)

o 14 subjects had missing body weight at Week 72

o 4 subjects violated unspecified discontinuation criteria

For trial GPHL, Table 9 shows the important protocol violations. The Applicant’s CSR for this trial 
provided fewer details than for trial GPHK, but the deviation categories are similar overall.

**Source:** Applicant GPHL CSR, Table GPHL.4.1, page 52 of 3056

The Applicant also reviewed the protocol deviations attributable to the COVID-19 pandemic, of 
which the great majority were minor. Two important deviations due to COVID-19 occurred in 
trial GPHK (one subject was missing Week 72 weight due to COVID-19, and one subject enrolled 
in another clinical trial [for a COVID-19 vaccine]), and none occurred in trial GPHL.

One serious trial breach was described by the Applicant for trial GPHL:

• “On <sup>(b) (6)</sup>, Site 72763 confirmed that the study binder for Subject Number 
<sup>(b) (6)</sup>was missing. This finding was considered a serious breach, based on the impact 
to the rights of the trial participants and potential risk related to the integrity of this 
participant’s data. The site recreated the study binder to the best of their ability. The 
participant was reevaluated to confirm that they were medically appropriate to stay on 
study drug and any AEs were managed appropriately. Lilly offices for privacy and data 
protection were informed of the situation on <sup>(b) (6)</sup>. Corrective and 
preventive action was developed and implemented. The participant was informed about 
the loss of information. The issue was determined to not have a significant impact to the 
trial data.”

In conclusion, the important protocol deviations, COVID-19 disruptions, and one trial breach 
were unlikely to affect the results of trials GPHK and GPHL.
