Clinical Review
Raymond Soccio, MD, PhD
NDA 217806
Zepbound (tirzepatide)

Within each trial, there are no meaningful imbalances in demographic characteristics across 
treatment groups.

There are several differences between trials:

• Trial GPHK enrolled approximately two-thirds female subjects, while GPHL is sexbalanced.

• Subjects are older in trial GPHL, based on the mean age about 10 years older and ~3 
times more subjects aged ≥65 years or older compared to trial GPHK (~18% versus ~6%).

• Hispanic and Latino ethnicity is common in both trials but more so in trial GPHL than 
GPHK (~60% versus ~50%).

• American Indian or Alaska Native race is reported for 9% of subjects in GPHK and no 
subjects in GPHL.

• Trial GPHK had sites in Mexico, Puerto Rico, and China, while trial GPHL did not.

Across both trials, 43% of subjects were enrolled at sites in the United States (including Puerto 
Rico), with an additional 43% enrolled in Argentina, Mexico, and Brazil, and the remaining 
subjects in Asia and Russia (Figure 6).

The Applicant’s Clinical Overview document provides a rationale for applicability of foreign 
data, noting that:

• All trials were performed in accordance with GCP (and two foreign sites with GCP

---

Clinical Review Raymond Soccio NDA 217806 Zepbound (tirzep

MD, PhD atide)

noncompliance did not affect efficacy results, see Table 29 and Table 30 below)

- All trials met the conditions of 21 CFR 312.120 (Foreign Clinical Studies Not Conducted Under an Investigational New Drug Application)

- Each trial had the same eligibility criteria irrespective of region.

The Applicant concludes that data collected are applicable across geographies. I concur that non-U.S. data in this NDA is applicable to the U.S. population and U.S. medical practice.

Besides T2D, there are other relevant weight-related comorbidities in the GPHK and GPHL trial populations, based on baseline diagnoses (Table 13) or laboratory and vital sign criteria (Table 14, including blood pressure, lipids, and renal function). Within each trial, there are no meaningful imbalances in comorbidity diagnoses across treatment groups. There are several differences between trials:

- Baseline anxiety/depression and polycystic ovarian syndrome (PCOS) are more common in trial GPHK, which enrolled a younger and more predominantly female population.

- Baseline hypertension, dyslipidemia, NAFLD, and ASCVD (all considered metabolic comorbidities) were much more prevalent （~2-fold for each）in trial GPHL, which enrolled an older population with T2D.

- Baseline osteoarthritis, asthma/COPD, OSA,and gout appeared balanced between trials GPHK and GPHL.

The older T2D population in trial GPHL also has higher mean blood pressure, lower mean eGFR, more subjects with renal impairment, and higher mean triglycerides and lower mean HDL-C (consistent with the lipid abnormalities defining the metabolic syndrome). LDL-C is lower in trial GPHL than trial GPHK, likely attributable to more baseline statin use in the older T2D population with higher ASCVD risk in trial GPHL (see below).

Table 15 shows baseline use of medications for dyslipidemia and hypertension in each trial, with high use of both in trial GPHL. reductase inhibitors (statins) and fibrates. <sup>18</sup> in trial GPHK, and their use in trial GPHL is

In conclusion, trials GPHK and GPHL have no apparent imbalances in baseline characteristics between placebo and tirzepatide treatment groups that could potentially impact the interpretation of efficacy or safety in the target population and for the proposed indication. The observed differences in baseline characteristics between trials GPHK and GPHL are consistent with trial GPHL enrolling an older sex-balanced population with T2D and more comorbidities,

## Treatment Compliance, Concomitant Medications, and Rescue Medication Use

Treatment Compliance: In both trials, treatment compliance was defined as taking at least 75% of the required doses of study drug, and compliance for the entire treatment period was high (>95%) and did not differ between placebo and tirzepatide groups (Table 17). If needed, investigators were allowed to temporarily interrupt study drug for any reason. In both trials, subjects missing 3 or consecutive doses were rare (<6%) and not different between placebo and tirzepatide groups. A single dose reduction was allowed for intolerable GI adverse events, and in both trials this was rare, though much more frequent in the tirzepatide arms (5.4%) than placebo (0.1%) as expected given known GI tolerability issues. These compliance measures (in Table 17) did not different meaningfully among tirzepatide dosages in the two trials (not shown), and subjects were included in all safety and efficacy analysis populations regardless of compliance.

Table 17. Compliance with Study Treatment in Trials GPHK and GPHL

| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Overall compliance (≥75% doses taken) | 624 (97.0) | 1849 (97.5) | 302 (95.9) | 605 (97.1) | 926 (96.7) | 2454 (97.4) |
| Subjects missing ≥3 consecutive doses | 35 (5.4) | 104 (5.5) | 12 (3.8) | 36 (5.8) | 47 (4.9) | 140 (5.6) |

Source: Generated by reviewer from Applicant CSRs, Tables GPHK.8.16 and GPHL.8.17

Concomitant Medications: Other weight management medications were not allowed at baseline, so there are no issues with such medications affecting the primary and secondary weight-related endpoints. There were secondary endpoints related to blood pressure, lipids, and glycemia, and compliance with medications for these was not formally measured in the trials. However, the Applicant reports changes in use and dosages for these medications:

- Lipid-lowering medications were not meaningfully changed in either trial (Table 18, Table 20). This is consistent with general clinical practice and guidelines, as statins are generally not discontinued or dose-reduced in the absence of adverse reactions. Therefore, secondary lipid-related endpoints were unlikely to be affected by compliance with lipid-lowering medications.

- In both trials, there were notably more reductions in baseline antihypertensive medications in the tirzepatide 10 mg and 15 mg arms compared to the placebo arms (Table 19, Table 20). Since the secondary endpoint results (see below) showed a decrease in blood pressure in tirzepatide arms, this reduction in antihypertensive 
medications in the tirzepatide arm could be consistent with tirzepatide’s effect on 
lowering blood pressure (i.e., blood pressure was at goal with fewer medications 
needed and/or there was reduction of antihypertensive medications for safety). The 
treatment effect of tirzepatide on blood pressure compared to placebo could 
theoretically have been larger if these antihypertensive medications were not reduced.

• Baseline antihyperglycemic medications were excluded in trial GPHK, and in trial GPHL
there were notable dose reductions in these medications in the tirzepatide 10 mg and 
15 mg arms compared to the placebo arm (Table 20). As above for hypertension, this is 
consistent with tirzepatide’s efficacy for glycemic control, making other medications for 
diabetes unnecessary or unsafe, and these medication changes are predicted to reduce 
the observed treatment effect of tirzepatide versus placebo on glycemia-related 
secondary endpoints.

<u>Rescue therapy:</u> Rescue therapy for weight management was not allowed during either trial, so 
there are no issues about such rescue medications affecting the primary and secondary weightrelated endpoints. Rescue therapy for hypertension and dyslipidemia was not defined in this 
trial, so changes in medications for blood pressure and lipids would only occur as part of the 
subjects’ usual care. Given that tirzepatide resulted in reduction in baseline use of 
antihypertensive and lipid-lowering medications (see above), it is possible that new treatment 
of lipids and blood pressure occurred more in placebo than tirzepatide arms, thus potentially 
reducing the treatment effects of tirzepatide versus placebo on blood pressure and lipid 
endpoint (see below).

Rescue therapy for hyperglycemia was defined in both trials. In trial GPHK, 5 subjects were 
diagnosed with T2D during the trial (3 with prediabetes at baseline) and started metformin 
(n=4) or semaglutide (n=1, a protocol violation included above). Notably, 4 subjects with new 
T2D were in the placebo arm (0.62%) compared to 1 in the tirzepatide 10 mg arm (0.05% of all 
tirzepatide-treated subjects). 15 subjects in trial GPHK received antihyperglycemic medications 
without a diagnosis of T2D:

• 9 received metformin (n = 7) or dapagliflozin (n = 2) to treat prediabetes, glucose 
intolerance, insulin resistance, or PCOS.

• 1 received insulin lispro to treat postoperative hyperglycemia.

• 5 received semaglutide (n = 2) or liraglutide (n = 3) to treat obesity or overweight, after 
having discontinued study drug.

In 12 cases, these were captured as important protocol violations (prohibited medication use). 
Overall in trial GPHK, subjects receiving post-baseline antihyperglycemic medications were 
unlikely to have affected glycemia-related endpoints, due to their small number and

Raymond Soccio, MD, PhD
NDA 217806
Zepbound (tirzepatide)
distribution among placebo and tirzepatide arms.

Rescue therapy for persistent hyperglycemia was well-defined in trial GPHL (see above), 
enrolling subjects with T2D at baseline. As expected given the glucose-lowering efficacy of 
tirzepatide, rescue therapy was >10-fold more common in the placebo arm (101/315, 32.1%) 
compared to the tirzepatide 10 mg (9/312, 2.9%) and 15 mg (8/311, 2.6%) arms.<sup>19</sup>
Antihyperglycemic rescue therapy predominantly in the placebo arm would tend to reduce the 
treatment effect of tirzepatide versus placebo on glycemia-related trial endpoints.

<u>Conclusion on compliance, concomitant and rescue medications:</u>

Overall, compliance with study drug was excellent in both trials. Baseline and rescue use of 
medications for hypertension, lipids, and glucose-lowering was unlikely to alter conclusions 
regarding the related secondary endpoints.

## Efficacy Results – Primary Endpoint

For substantial evidence of effectiveness for the indication of CWM, the Division considers the 
primary endpoint of placebo-corrected mean percent change in body weight from baseline in 
the intention-to-treat population after at least one year on the maintenance dosage. Trials 
GPHK and GPHL both include this primary endpoint with appropriate protocol-specified 
statistical analyses, placebo controls, and durations of maintenance dosage tirzepatide 
treatment (Week 72 represents 52 weeks of maintenance for 15 mg, 60 weeks for 10 mg, and 
68 weeks for 5 mg). For FDA review, the more conservative “treatment-regimen” estimand (in 
the ITT population) is considered rather than the “efficacy estimand” (in randomized subjects 
had they remained on their randomized treatment for the entire planned 72-week treatment 
duration).

For a detailed review of the statistical analyses for the primary endpoints, please refer to the 
FDA statistical review dated October 10, 2023, by Drs. Kyunghee Song and Feng Li (Office of 
Biostatistics, Division of Biometrics II). The co-primary endpoints in both trials were percent 
change in body weight and proportion of subject achieving ≥5% weight loss, with the difference 
from placebo for the tirzepatide 10 mg and 15 mg arms. For both trials GPHK and GPHL
(SURMOUNT-1 and -2), FDA statistical review confirmed the Applicant’s primary efficacy 
analyses (Table 21).

<u>Analysis by population:</u> The data above show in both trials that the treatment effects of 
tirzepatide for weight loss were slightly greater in the Applicant’s “efficacy” population (in 
randomized subjects had they remained on their randomized treatment for the entire planned 
72-week treatment duration) than in the more conservative “treatment-regimen” (ITT) 
population used by FDA.

<u>Handling missing data:</u> As detailed above, the Applicant used a “hybrid imputation” (missing at 
random for exceptional circumstances [COVID-19], retrieved dropouts for other intercurrent 
events). For trial GPHK, the Applicant also presents (Table 25) a sensitivity analysis showing 
nearly identical results if imputed data was imputed solely by retrieved dropouts, showing little 
effect on the overall results for exceptional circumstances in the “hybrid imputation.”

approaches were conducted to evaluate the robustness of the conclusions based on the 
primary analyses. All sensitivity analyses yielded results that were consistent with the primary 
analyses results.” She concluded that “Sensitivity analyses supported the robustness of the 
primary efficacy results.”

As detailed above in the endpoints description in Section 6.1.1, the Applicant also prespecified 
primary, key secondary, and other endpoints that were responder analyses of the proportion of 
subjects achieving specified weight loss thresholds (≥5%, ≥10%, ≥15%, and ≥20%). These results 
were consistent with the distribution above, showed statistical significance, and will be 
tabulated in label Section 14 (Figure 10 summarizes these responder analyses using the 
treatment-regimen estimand). ≥25% weight loss was not a prespecified endpoint, but this 
occurred in 15.3%, 32.3%, and 36.2% of trial GPHK subjects with tirzepatide 5 mg, 10 mg, and 
15 mg, compared to 1.5% of subjects with placebo (treatment-regimen estimand, each P<0.001 
compared to placebo and not corrected for Type I error).

placebo not corrected for Type I error).

Since it can be challenging to interpret responder analyses to quantitatively assess benefit of 
investigational drugs for continuous interval outcomes,<sup>20</sup> the Division does not weigh these 
endpoints heavily in considering efficacy.

<u>Effects in demographic and other subpopulations:</u> The Applicant performed subgroup analyses 
for the following subject characteristics:

• age group (<65, ≥65 years), example graphical data for trial GPHK in Table 27

• race

• sex

• ethnicity

• region of enrollment (US, outside the US)

• baseline BMI category (<30, ≥30 and <35, ≥35 and <40, ≥40 kg/m<sup>2</sup>)

• glycemic status at randomization (normoglycemia versus prediabetes; applicable to 
GPHK/SURMOUNT-1 only)

• baseline HbA1c category (≤8.5%, >8.5%; applicable to GPHL/SURMOUNT-2 only)

• type of antihyperglycemic medications used at randomization (weight loss, weight gain, 
or weight neutral; applicable to GPHL/SURMOUNT-2 only)

For the primary endpoint of percent change in body weight, all subgroups showed significantly 
better weight reduction in the tirzepatide groups compared with the placebo group (using the 
treatment-regimen estimand, Table 28).

There are some statistically significant treatment-by-subgroup interactions in the table above 
(sex, ethnicity, and BMI category in GPHK and HbA1c category in GPHL), which the Applicant 
states have no obvious clinical relevance. These same interactions were identified by the FDA 
statistical review and are discussed below.

The Applicant presented the same subgroup analyses (not shown) for the co-primary endpoints 
of proportion achieving ≥5% weight loss on tirzepatide 10 mg and 15 mg, with a similar lack of 
significant or relevant subgroup effects.

Raymond Soccio, MD, PhD
NDA 217806
Zepbound (tirzepatide)

The FDA statistical reviewer Dr. Song also calculated shrinkage estimates of subgroup treatment 
effects using a Bayesian hierarchical model based on summary sample estimates; for details 
please see her review dated October 10, 2023. Briefly, for both trials the percent change from 
baseline in body weight at Week 72, all subgroups (sex, age, race, ethnicity, geographic region, 
baseline BMI, baseline prediabetes status in GPHK/SURMOUNT-1, and baseline HbA1c in 
GPHL/SURMOUNT-2) reported the upper limit of the 95% confidence interval less than zero, in 
favor of both tirzepatide 10 mg and 15 mg groups. With shrinkage estimates, the upper limits of 
the 95% credible intervals were also less than zero, in favor of tirzepatide (see examples below 
in Figure 13 and Figure 15).

Of note, there were significant interaction effects between subgroups and tirzepatide
treatment in trial GPHK/SURMOUNT-1 (Figure 13, red boxes), with weight reduction apparently 
more favorable:

• for females than for males

• for high baseline BMI than low baseline BMI

• for not Hispanic/Latino than Hispanic/Latino ethnicity

---

**_Reviewer comment: There were four identified interaction terms with a significant p-value for 
the primary endpoint: sex, ethnicity, baseline BMI, and baseline HbA1c. Although statistically 
more weight loss was observed in females than in males, in those with BMI ≥ 35 kg/m<sup>2</sup> than_**

$$
BMI\geq35kg/m^2
$$

**_those with lower BMIs, in those not identified as Hispanic/Latino ethnicity, and in those with_** 
**_T2D and HbA1c ≤ 8.5%, the 95% confidence intervals between subgroups overlapped. Further, 
despite these statistical findings, from a clinical perspective the weight loss was clinically 
meaningful in all subgroups (including men, those with BMI <35 kg/m<sup>2</sup>, those identified as_** 
**_Hispanic/Latino ethnicity, and those with T2D and HbA1c > 8.5%). For example, the difference 
in mean weight loss between females and males was -18.9% vs. -15.1%. An approximately_**
**_15% reduction in baseline weight is clinically meaningful and larger than the average 
treatment effect of lifestyle intervention and most approved CWM drugs (although cross-trial 
comparisons should be made with caution). Therefore, even with evidence of statistical
differences in weight loss in some subgroups, the difference is not considered clinically 
meaningful and labeling should not reflect subgroup-specific efficacy._**

$$
HbA1c\leq8.5\%
$$

<35kg/m^2

<u>Analysis by site:</u> I compared efficacy for the primary endpoints at different trial sites, and no 
anomalies were identified.

## Data Quality and Integrity

OSI inspected 6 trial sites for GPHK and found no significant issues at these sites (Section 4.1).

The Applicant did not analyze efficacy excluding the small number of sites (see Section 6.1.2, 
Financial Disclosures heading above) where investigators received significant financial 
compensation, but this was not considered necessary given the trial design (blinding and 
objective measure of body weight as the primary endpoint).

Two trial sites had known or suspected GCP noncompliance (see Section 6.1.2, Compliance with 
Good Clinical Practices heading above). Subjects at these two sites represented 1.3% of the 
total randomized in trial GPHK and 2.8% of the total randomized in trial GPHL. In response to an 
information request, the Applicant provided sensitivity analyses of the primary endpoints 
excluding these two sites. The results in the two trials were not meaningfully different, as there 
was slightly more weight loss efficacy when these sites with GCP noncompliance were excluded 
(Table 29 and Table 30).

---

## Based on these results, no potential data quality or integrity issues in trials GPHK and GPHL were identified that could have affected the overall efficacy results or conclusions.

(b) (4)

The Division recommends the following secondary efficacy endpoints in CWM

trials:<sup>21</sup>

• Waist circumference

• Lipid parameters

• Blood Pressure and pulse (pulse is considered separately in Section 8.4.6 below)

• Fasting glucose and insulin

• HbA1c (in subjects with T2D)

Song was able to confirm all key secondary endpoint analyses; for details please see her review 
dated October 10, 2023.

to confirm that reductions in weight are associated with expected improvements in other 
weight-related metabolic parameters._**

**_Reviewer comment: Tirzepatide lowers triglycerides and raises HDL-C. Other cholesterol 
values (non-HDL, total, LDL-C, and VLDL-C) correlate with increased ASCVD risk, and 
tirzepatide generally reduced these measurements from baseline. In trial GPHL (subjects with_** 
**_T2D), tirzepatide did not lower LDL-C and had minimal effects on total cholesterol, likely 
attributable to the high baseline statin use and relatively low mean baseline LDL-C values in 
this trial (<100 mg/dL, see above)._**

**_Reviewer comment: The reductions in HbA1c in trial GPHL (>2% with tirzepatide and >1.5% 
more than placebo comparator) are consistent with those observed in the SURPASS trials for_** 
**_T2D, reviewed in NDA 215866._**

The Division’s guidance notes that effective CWM is expected to improve these metabolic 
parameters (waist circumference, blood pressure, lipids, and glycemia), and the results above 
confirm this for tirzepatide. The guidance also notes that many trial subjects will be taking 
baseline concomitant medications for hypertension or T2D, and that an additional important 
endpoint is those subjects who have meaningful dose-reduction or complete withdrawal of 
these medications. In trials GPHK and GPHL, tirzepatide-treated subjects reduced these 
medications more than placebo-treated subjects (see Table 18, Table 19, and Table 20 above, 
under concomitant medications).

The Applicant also presents data from two patient-reported outcomes (PRO) instruments in
trials GPHK and GPHL: the 36-item Short Form Health Survey version 2 (SF-36v2) Acute Form
(Figure 20) and the Impact of Weight on Quality of Life-Lite (IWQOL-Lite, Figure 21). Change 
from baseline in SF-36v2 Physical Functioning Domain Score was a multiplicity-controlled key 
secondary endpoint in trial GPHK (comparing the pooled 10 mg and 15 mg weekly dosages to 
placebo), while this was a secondary endpoint in trial GPHL, and all other PRO-related 
endpoints were secondary or exploratory.

Since only physical functioning PROs <sup>(b) (4)</sup>only this aspect is discussed 
here, as jointly reviewed by Drs. Qing Xie and Selena Daniels of DCOA and Drs. Xin Yuan and Lili 
Garrard of the Patient-Focused Statistical Scientists (PFSS). Please see the joint DCOA/PFSS 
review dated October 18, 2023, for details on the physical functioning endpoints, with the key
results and conclusions summarized below.

The data from trial GPHK showed a statistically significant improvement in SF-36v2 Physical 
Functioning Domain Scores, with similar increases in trial GPHL that were nominally improved
but not significant due to lack of multiplicity control (Table 31). The DCOA/PFFS review noted 
the following issues with this result, despite achievement of statistical significance:

• The differences between treatment and placebo means were small and of uncertain 
clinical meaningfulness.

• Measurement at only two time points (Baseline and Week 72 or early discontinuation) 
could have missed information.

• The baseline scores of 47.9-49.6 in the treatment arms are similar to the general 
population mean of 50, such that subjects were not sufficiently limited in physical 
functioning at baseline for an adequate assessment of improvement.

• There were minimal to no observed changes in median raw scores for the item- and 
domain-level data.

• The changes observed in the normalized scores were also minimal as evidenced by lack 
of clear separation between the treatment arms in the empirical cumulative distribution 
function (eCDF) curves (Figure 22).

A subgroup analysis of those subjects

---

Clinical Review
Raymond Soccio, MD, PhD
NDA 217806
Zepbound (tirzepatide)

with impaired function at baseline could be more informative; based on earlier FDA comments 
the Applicant proposed such exploratory analyses in the SAPs for both trials. The Patient Global 
Impression of Status for Physical Activity (PGIS) has a 5-point scale: “not limited at all,” “a little 
limited,” “moderately limited,” “very much limited,” and “extremely limited.” The Applicant 
defined impairment at baseline as PGIS “moderately limited” or worse, which appears 
reasonable and correlates with lower mean SF-36v2 physical function domain scores. By this 
definition, in trials GPHK and GPHL, 526/2539 (20.7%) and 216/938 (23.0%) of subjects had 
impaired physical functioning at baseline. In this impaired at baseline subgroup, the 
improvements from baseline mean SF-36v2 physical functioning domain scores were nominally 
greater than in the overall population (approximately 3 units in Table 32 below compared to 
approximately 2 units in Table 31 above).

The Applicant used both anchor-based analyses and qualitative methods (cognitive interviews) 
to support the proposed threshold(s) for meaningful within-patient change in the SF-36v2

---

Clinical Review
Raymond Soccio, MD, PhD
NDA 217806
Zepbound (tirzepatide)

Physical Functioning domain score. As detailed in the DCOA/PFSS review, there were limitations 
to the anchor-based analysis (PGIS was not an ideal anchor since it was also used to define the 
impaired at baseline subgroup) and the cognitive interviews (querying the smallest meaningful 
improvement in only one item using a hypothetical approach, rather than a clinically 
meaningful improvements in multiple items using actual responses). Therefore, the DCOA/PFSS 
review concluded it was uncertain whether the observed changes in physical functioning 
scores, ranging from 2-3, were clinically meaningful.

**_Reviewer comment: The discussion above identifies three major limitations to the physical 
functioning results in trials GPHK and GPHL:_**

• **_There was no convincing treatment difference in the overall trial populations._**

• **_There was some evidence of treatment difference in the impaired at baseline subgroup 
that was prespecified, but this analysis was exploratory and not multiplicitycontrolled._**

• **_There remains major uncertainty that the observed change of 2-3 on physical function 
scores represents a clinically meaningful difference._** 
(b) (4)

**_These issues were discussed with leadership of DDLO and DCOA._**

(b) (4)

## Dose/Dose Response

See Section 7.1.4 below.

---

Trial GPHK included percent weight change at Week 20 as a key secondary endpoint controlled 
for Type I error, and there was significant and meaningful weight loss in the pooled tirzepatide 
10 mg and 15 mg groups. This Week 20 time point mainly reflects weight loss during dosage 
titration: subjects following the planned dosage escalation would have been on the tirzepatide 
10 mg maintenance dosage for 8 weeks, and in the tirzepatide 15 mg arm they would be in the 
process of increasing from 12.5 mg to the 15 mg maintenance dosage. >10% placebo-corrected 
statistically-significant weight loss was observed at Week 20 (Table 33).

In trials GPHK and GPHL, the Applicant was able to demonstrate durability of effect throughout 
the intended 72-week planned treatment durations (Figure 25 and Figure 26). Separation of the 
weight curves is apparent as early as Week 4.  Plateau in the curve in the tirzeptide arms occurs 
around Week 60, whereas the plateau occurs in the placebo group around Week 20.

For CWM clinical development programs, 52-week treatment at the intended maintenance 
dosage is considered adequate to support durability of response, showing both the initial 
weight loss and its maintenance over time.

**_Reviewer comment: Weight loss maintenance beyond one year on tirzepatide is a question 
that will be addressed by future trials, including the prediabetes two-year extension in_** 
**_GPHK/SURMOUNT-1 and the phase 3b clinical outcomes trial SURMOUNT-MMO._**

The Applicant also presents data showing the durability over 72 weeks for the improvements in 
secondary endpoints related to blood pressure, lipids, and glycemic parameters (not shown). 
Overall, I concur that the durability of tirzepatide response was demonstrated for the primary 
and secondary endpoints in trials GPHK and GPHL.

## Persistence of Effect

Persistence of effect is not applicable for the two trials in this NDA, which did not test for 
persistence since they only included a four-week (off-treatment) safety follow-up period.

**_Reviewer comment: The Applicant has completed the SURMOUNT-4 randomized withdrawal 
trial. Based on results from a similar trial for semaglutide, this trial is likely to show weight 
regain in tirzepatide-treated subjects who are randomized to switch to placebo versus 
continuing tirzepatide. Therefore, it is unlikely that there will be persistence of the weight loss 
effect off-drug._**

## Additional Analyses Conducted on the Individual Trials

The two substudies in trial GPHK (DXA and ABPM) are primarily reviewed from a safety rather 
than efficacy perspective, so these are detailed in Section 8.7.

## 7. Integrated Review of Effectiveness

## Assessment of Efficacy Across Trials

### 7.1.1. Primary Endpoints

Figure 27 compares in trials GPHK (SURMOUNT-1) and GPHL (SURMOUNT-2) the primary 
efficacy endpoint of percent change in body weight from Baseline to Week 72.

---

**Figure 27. Primary Efficacy Endpoint (Percent Weight Loss) Comparing Trials GPHK and GPHL**

| Category | placebo (%) | TZP 5 mg (%) | TZP 10 mg (%) | TZP 15 mg (%) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| SURMOUNT-1 (72 weeks vs placebo) | -3.1 | -15.0 | -19.5 | -20.9 |
| SURMOUNT-2 (72 weeks vs placebo) | -3.2 |  | -12.8 | -14.7 |

**Source:** Applicant SCE, Figure 2.7.3.30, page 85 of 123

Note: least squares means ± standard errors using treatment-regimen (mITT) estimand

***p-Value <.001 versus placebo, controlled for type 1 error

This primary endpoint is consistent with Division’s expectations and guidelines, and the 
placebo-corrected effect size for tirzepatide is substantial and clinically meaningful. The effect 
size distribution in each trial (see above in Section 6.1.2) shows that the great majority of 
tirzepatide-treated subjects achieve clinically meaningful weight loss.

It is typical for CWM clinical trials to show higher efficacy in subjects without diabetes 
compared to subjects with diabetes, and this was the case for tirzepatide comparing trials GPHK 
and GPHL. Given this differential effect in populations based on T2D status, the separate 
efficacy analyses of the two trials above in Section 6.1.2 are preferred, as a pooled efficacy 
analysis for weight loss is not relevant.

The Applicant also presents evidence supportive of weight loss in the tirzepatide trials 
conducted for the indication of T2D, the phase 3 trials SURPASS 1-5, which for this NDA 
submission were limited to the subset of subjects with BMI ≥27 kg/m<sup>2</sup> at baseline. Of note, all 
five of these trials of subjects with T2D included the tirzepatide 5, 10, 15 mg weekly dosages, as 
opposed to GPHL/SURMOUNT-2 which did not include a 5 mg treatment arm. These SURPASS 
trials also contribute to the overall safety database and are described in more detail below in

Section 8.1. SURPASS-1 and -5 were placebo-controlled trials with 40-week treatment 
durations, SURPASS-2 had an active control of the GLP-1R agonist semaglutide 1 mg weekly
which caused weight loss, while SURPASS-3 and -4 had long-acting insulin active controls which 
caused weight gain.

Weight loss in these SURPASS trials in overweight or obese subjects with T2D was substantial, 
though as expected, less than that achieved in subjects without diabetes treated for longer
duration in trial GPHK/SURMOUNT-1 (shown for comparison in Figure 28 and Figure 29).
Weight loss on the tirzepatide 10 mg and 15 mg dosages in the SURPASS trials was also 
consistent with the weight loss in GPHL/SURMOUNT-2.

---

Clinical Review
Raymond Soccio, MD, PhD
NDA 217806
Zepbound (tirzepatide)

**_the Division’s expectations for CWM trials (i.e., trial design was for glycemic control, weight 
was not a primary endpoint, the BMI ≥27 kg/m<sup>2</sup> subgroup was not prespecified, the duration 
was too short, not all were placebo-controlled), they nonetheless contribute to the 
assessment of efficacy. While the tirzepatide 5 mg weekly dosage was not studied in subjects 
with T2D in CWM trial GPHL/SURMOUNT-2, the SURPASS data supports it as a maintenance 
dosage for use for CWM in subjects with T2D._**

## Secondary and Other Endpoints

See Section 6.1.2 above for Figures comparing secondary endpoints side-by-side for trials GPHK 
and GPHL. In general, the secondary endpoints related to blood pressure, lipids, glycemia, and 
patient-reported outcomes were consistent between the two trials and showed statistical 
significance. These secondary endpoints support the clinical relevance of the weight loss 
achieved with tirzepatide, as it is associated with improvements in cardiovascular risk factors. I 
agree with the Applicant that some of these endpoints are appropriate for inclusion in label 
Section 14, consistent with the labels for other CWM medications, as discussed further in 
Section 10.1.

### 7.1.3. Subpopulations

See Section 6.1.2 above for the subgroup analyses performed in trials GPHK and GPHL. In 
general for each trial, the primary efficacy results were similar in various subpopulations, and 
there were no subpopulations in which results showed clinically meaningful differences.

### 7.1.4. Dose and Dose-Response

Although subjects were randomized to tirzepatide 5, 10, or 15 mg weekly dosage arms, the 
phase 3 trials GPHK and GPHL were not intended to compare differences between tirzepatide 
dosages, but rather to compare each individual dosage to the placebo comparator. In each trial, 
all dosages were superior to placebo for the primary endpoint (percent change from baseline in 
body weight) and most of the secondary endpoints.

**_Reviewer comment: There does appear to be evidence of a dose-response for efficacy since 
placebo-correct weight loss with at Week 72 in trial GPHK was nominally greater at higher 
doses: -11.9%, -16.4%, and -17.8%, respectively, with tirzepatide 5, 10, or 15 mg weekly._**

Based on the totality of evidence, the dose selection in these trials was adequate, as were the 
dosing recommendations for starting dose (2.5 mg), dose escalation schedule (by 2.5 mg every 
4 weeks), and maintenance dosages (5, 10, and 15 mg). In trial GPHK, the lowest 5 mg 
tirzepatide dosage showed meaningfully less weight loss efficacy than the 10 mg and 15 mg

---

Clinical Review
Raymond Soccio, MD, PhD
NDA 217806
Zepbound (tirzepatide)

doses (though these dosages were not directly compared as above). This lowest dosage of 5 mg 
was not investigated in the population with T2D in trial GPHL (a limitation of the trial), and the 
10 mg and 15 mg dosages showed relatively similar weight loss efficacy.

## Onset, Duration, and Durability of Efficacy Effects

See Section 6.1.2 above for the time course of weight loss efficacy in trials GPHK and GPHL.

## Additional Efficacy Considerations

## Considerations on Benefit in the Postmarket Setting

Overall, the two trial populations support that the observed effect of tirzepatide on weight loss 
is generalizable to the broader population of patients with obesity/overweight that might 
receive tirzepatide for CWM in the postmarketing setting, and that the benefit can reasonably 
be expected to be seen in the postmarket setting. Weight loss with tirzepatide and related 
salutary effects in the postmarket setting will depend on a number of factors, including 
compliance with adjunct diet and exercise recommendations and compliance with the drug 
long-term. Although more subjects discontinued tirzepatide due to GI adverse events (AEs) as 
compared to placebo (Section 8.4.3), mean weight loss was only minimally less with the 
treatment regimen estimand (mITT population) compared to the efficacy estimand (assuming 
all subjects completed treatment for 72 weeks). Though not studied in trials supporting this 
NDA, weight regain can be expected upon tirzepatide discontinuation, based on experience 
with other incretins and the anticipated results of the tirzepatide SURMOUNT-4 trial with 
randomized withdrawal. The optimal dosing of tirzepatide beyond one year remains uncertain 
but may be addressed by future trials.

## Other Relevant Benefits

Compared to the most effective currently-marketed weight loss medication (semaglutide 2.4 
mg weekly), tirzepatide offers the same once weekly SC injection with dosage escalation, so 
tirzepatide does not offer a more convenient dosing schedule or route of administration. While 
semaglutide is a selective GLP-1R agonist, tirzepatide is a dual agonist at the GLP-1R and GIP-R. 
While dual agonism may offer theoretical benefits in efficacy and safety as discussed 
elsewhere, these are mostly speculative and should not be considered in the overall 
assessment of tirzepatide’s benefit.

---

Clinical Review
Raymond Soccio, MD, PhD
NDA 217806
Zepbound (tirzepatide)

## Integrated Assessment of Effectiveness

The evidentiary standard of substantial evidence of effectiveness has been met by two 
adequate and well-controlled trials, GPHK and GPHL. The weight loss achieved in these two 
trials is statistically significant and clinically meaningful - and greater in magnitude than that 
observed in trials of other drugs previously approved for CWM. The statistical reviewer Dr. Song 
concluded “The collective evidence from the submitted data demonstrated efficacy of 
tirzepatide in the study population. We recommend approval for the proposed indication based 
on findings from the submitted results.” – and I concur.

At dosages of tirzepatide 10 mg and 15 mg weekly, a mean weight loss at one year in the ITT 
population of -19.5 and -20.9% was achieved in subjects without T2D (trial GPHK, compared to 
-3.1% with placebo) and -12.8 and -14.7% in subjects with T2D (trial GPHL, compared to -3.2% 
with placebo). Furthermore, with the lowest maintenance dosage of 5 mg, -15.0% weight loss 
was achieved in subjects without T2D in trial GPHK - and this 5 mg maintenance dosage showed 
apparent weight loss in shorter duration SURPASS trials for glycemic control in subjects with 
T2D. Across populations in the two phase 3 CWM trials, >79% and up to 90.9% of tirzepatidetreated trial subjects achieved ≥5% weight loss, a threshold the Division has considered for 
clinical meaningfulness (compared to 32.5-34.5% of placebo-treated subjects).

On tirzepatide 15 mg, mean weight reduction of ≥25% was found in 36.2% of subjects without 
T2D and 15.5% of subjects of subjects with T2D (this degree of weight loss was negligible in 
placebo-treated subjects, 1.5% without T2D and 0.3% with T2D). Therefore, a substantial 
number of tirzepatide-treated patients could achieve degrees of weight loss >25%, comparable 
to that achieved after bariatric surgery. Importantly, maintenance of weight loss on tirzepatide 
is likely to require chronic therapy, and it unknown whether weight regain occurs during longterm therapy. Consistent with the observed weight loss having clinically meaningful benefits, in 
both trials weight loss with tirzepatide was associated with improvements in risk factors for 
cardiovascular disease (glycemia, blood pressure, and lipids). Overall, these two trials have 
generated efficacy data that are robust, interpretable, and persuasive in supporting the 
proposed indication of CWM in adults.

## 8. Review of Safety

## Safety Review Approach

This safety review is primarily based on the two phase 3 CWM trials GPHK and GPHL 
(SURMOUNT-1 and -2), which were analyzed individually and pooled. The Applicant submitted 
safety data from these two trials, both of which include at least 52 weeks of placebo-controlled 
exposure to the intended maintenance dosage of tirzepatide (72-week treatment duration,

---

Clinical Review
Raymond Soccio, MD, PhD
NDA 217806
Zepbound (tirzepatide)

with 20 weeks of dose escalation required to reach the highest dose of tirzepatide 15 mg 
weekly). These two trials allow an indication-specific review for CWM.

In addition to the data from these two phase 3 CWM trials, the Applicant’s safety package to 
support the use of tirzepatide for CWM includes supportive data from nine other phase 3 trials 
and two phase 2 trials, primarily conducted in subjects with T2D, for a total of 13 trials (Table 
35).

Ten trials in the safety database (eight phase 3 SURPASS trials and both phase 2 trials) are from 
the tirzepatide development program for T2D. Nine of the T2D trials were included the original 
submission for NDA 215866 (tirzepatide for T2D), and one other T2D phase 3 trial (GPHO) has 
completed since that NDA submission. For these ten T2D trials included in the safety database 
for the current CWM NDA, the Applicant analyzed data only from subjects with overweight or 
obesity (BMI ≥27 kg/m<sup>2</sup>). In the T2D phase 2 and global phase 3 trials, 78-89% of trial subjects 
had overweight or obesity, and in the three regional phase 3 trials limited to Japan or the Asia-
Pacific, 45-53% of subjects had overweight or obesity (see above, Table 35).

$$

---

The Applicant has submitted four pooled analysis sets of trial safety data (see Table 35 above 
and Table 36 below); trial GPHL (SURMOUNT-2) was not included in the Applicant’s pools as it 
was submitted in the second sequence of the rolling review:

• Analysis Set 1 (phase 3 Placebo-controlled): This pool includes three phase 3 trials with 
placebo-controlled exposure to tirzepatide. The duration of exposure differs between 
the CWM trial GPHK (72 weeks total, thus 52 weeks at the maximum 15 mg 
maintenance dose) and the T2D trials GPGK and GPGI (40 weeks total, thus 20 weeks at 
the 15 mg maintenance dose). 76.5% of subjects in this pool are from trial GPHK.

• Analysis Set 2 (phase 3 dose effect): This pool integrates data from nine phase 3 trials 
that randomized subjects equally to treatment arms with tirzepatide 5 mg, 10 mg, or 15 
mg, thus allowing assessment of dose effect for adverse events. The pooled trials differ 
in population, duration, blinding, and comparators (see Table 35), and this applies to the
next two pooled analysis sets as well.

• Analysis Set 3 (phase 2/3): This pool includes twelve phase 2 and 3 trials, pooling 
tirzepatide doses without regard to comparators.

• Analysis Set 4 (phase 2/3 Comparator-controlled): This pool includes ten phase 2 and 3 
trials, pooling all tirzepatide doses and pooling all comparators (placebo and active).

**_Reviewer comment: The descriptions of the four pooled analysis sets above identify important
issues with each, including differences among the pooled trials in treatment duration, 
comparators, blinding, trial design, and dosages. For these reasons, the primary focus of my 
safety review (and the Applicant’s) was the two similar phase 3 CWM trials GPHK and GPHL, 
with the pooled analysis sets including T2D trial data used for further analysis of specific 
safety issues when relevant._**

The safety evaluation for this NDA was primarily based on the Applicant’s submitted datasets 
(analysis and tabulation) for trials GPHK/SURMOUNT-1 and GPHL/SURMOUNT-2 and 
information requests to the Applicant. Data from the Applicant’s pooled analysis sets described 
above were reviewed in a supportive fashion or to clarify safety signals that arose in the CWM 
trials.

I also reviewed the Applicant’s 120-day safety update submitted on August 8, 2023 (Table 37), 
which included safety data from:

• 20 ongoing studies (four phase 1, two phase 2, thirteen phase 3, and three phase 4), 19 
of which contributed safety data since GPHJ/SURMOUNT-5 had not randomized subjects 
at the data cutoff

• 2 recently completed studies (phase 3 GPHD and phase 1 GPGU)

• Postmarketing data through May 13, 2023 (see Section 8.9.1).

The Applicant concludes that new data from completed trials and mostly blinded data from 
ongoing trials is consistent with that described for tirzepatide in the CWM NDA, and I concur. 
This additional information provided in the 4-month safety update does not significantly affect 
the safety review below or key safety information included in proposed labeling.

for ≥52 weeks and 76.5% for ≥72 weeks

• 958 subjects received at least one dose of placebo, with a mean exposure duration of

### 8.2.2. Relevant characteristics of the safety population:

For the two phase 3 placebo-controlled CWM trials GPHK and GPHL (SURMOUNT-1 and -2), the safety population is identical to the efficacy population as shown above in Table 7.

The additional 11 trials in the pooled safety analysis sets (limited to BMI $ \geq27 $ kg/m <sup>2</sup> ) differ in baseline characteristics as shown in Table 40, with trial GPHK (SURMOUNT-1) shown for comparison. The CWM population in SURMOUNT-4 is similar to SURMOUNT-1, while the T2D populations in the other ten trials (5 global SURPASS trials, 3 regional SURPASS trials in Asia, and 2 phase 2 trials) differ in age, sex distribution, and BMI from SURMOUNT-1. These T2D populations are more similar to the trial population in CWM trial GPHL (SURMOUNT-2, see Table 10 above). Overall, the safety population is diverse reflecting an adequate number of subjects differing in sex, race, ethnicity, and BMI. A sufficient number of subjects were enrolled at trial sites in the U.S.

### 8.2.3. Adequacy of the safety database:

The adequacy of the safety database is based primarily on the phase 3 placebo-controlled CWM trials GPHK and GPHL (SURMOUNT-1 and -2). The Division generally expects a safety database of $ \geq 3000 $ subjects exposed to drug for one year at the maintenance dose, with $ \geq 1500 $ exposed to placebo in parallel groups. I find the safety database to be adequate with respect to three key features:

- Duration: Both trials are of sufficient duration to meet the Division's expectation, since

---

Clinical Review
Raymond Soccio, MD, PhD
NDA 217806
Zepbound (tirzepatide)

the 72-week total treatment duration gives 52 weeks after dosage escalation to the 
highest dose of tirzepatide 15 mg weekly.

• Size: The two trials include 2,519 subjects exposed to tirzepatide and 958 subjects 
exposed to placebo. Given the large amount of additional safety data in the NDA 
submission, primarily from the T2D development program and in the pooled analysis 
sets detailed above, I find the size of the safety database to be adequate.

• Demographics: The sex and age of the trial population generally reflect those who might 
seek CWM care in a primary care setting. Trial GPHL has a T2D population including 
more males, older subjects, and subjects with weight-related comorbidities. While the 
trials were somewhat over-representative of Hispanic or Latino ethnicity and underrepresentative of the Black or African American and Asian race in the U.S., overall these 
trials enrolled a population with adequate diversity by race and ethnicity.

## Adequacy of Applicant’s Clinical Safety Assessments

## Issues Regarding Data Integrity and Submission Quality

In general, the submission and data quality were very good. Trials GPHK and GPHL were 
analyzed to identify any data quality issues, and no significant issues were identified. I also 
compared site-specific efficacy and safety data for potential data integrity issues at specific 
sites. The findings from OSI inspections of selected sites are consistent with no meaningful sitespecific issues (see Section 4.1).

The Applicant’s data were consistent across datasets, and tabulations could be crossreferenced to case report forms and patient narratives. All clinical items requested in meetings 
with FDA leading up to and after submission were provided and easy to locate. As detailed 
below, I was able to confirm all key safety results from clinical trial reports and safety 
summaries with the provided analysis datasets.

## Categorization of Adverse Events

---

Clinical Review
Raymond Soccio, MD, PhD
NDA 217806
Zepbound (tirzepatide)

The Applicant performed safety analyses using a modified intention-to-treat (mITT) population 
(i.e., all subjects randomized who received at least one dose of IP) and all data (on or off study 
drug) from the start of treatment to the end of the 4-week safety follow-up. AE information 
obtained and documented in the eCRF included: the onset and duration, the seriousness and 
severity, relatedness to study drug, actions taken with respect to study treatment, and 
outcome.

The Applicant used the following definitions in their protocols and/or safety analyses:

• AE: any untoward medical occurrence in a subject or clinical investigation subject 
administered a pharmaceutical product and which does not necessarily have to have a
causal relationship with this treatment.

• Treatment-emergent AEs (TEAEs): AEs that first occurred after baseline or worsened in 
severity after baseline. All analyses below focus on TEAEs.

• Serious AE: any untoward medical occurrence that either resulted in death, was lifethreatening, required inpatient hospitalization or caused prolongation of existing 
hospitalization, resulted in persistent or significant disability/incapacity, was a 
congenital anomaly/birth defect, or was an important medical event

Adverse events were classified by System Organ Class (SOC) and/or Preferred Term (PT) and
coded based on Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities (MedDRA) version 25.1. The 
verbatim terms (i.e., AE CRF text, and analysis datasets) provided by the investigators and the 
MedDRA PTs for which these AEs were coded were screened for correctness, and the coding of 
these data appeared appropriate.

This Applicant’s package includes routine safety assessments and “Special Safety Topics 
Including Adverse Events of Interest.” The latter are potential or established incretin classrelated safety concerns, and these will be referred to as adverse events of special interest 
(AESI) throughout this review. The Applicant’s AESI were:

• GI AEs

• dehydration-related AEs

• renal safety

• hepatobiliary disorders (including acute gallbladder disorders)

• major depressive disorders/suicidal ideation

• exocrine pancreas safety

• cardiovascular safety

• malignancy

• thyroid safety

• hypoglycemia

• injection site reactions

• immunogenicity (including hypersensitivity reactions).

---

Clinical Review
Raymond Soccio, MD, PhD
NDA 217806
Zepbound (tirzepatide)

Four other AESI are only applicable to trial subjects with T2D, so these were assessed in CWM
trial GPHL (SURMOUNT-2) but not GPHK (SURMOUNT-1):

• metabolic acidosis

• hyperglycemia requiring rescue therapy

• diabetic retinopathy complications

• amputation or peripheral revascularization.

The Applicant identified AESI primarily by searching the AE datasets using standardized 
MedDRA queries (SMQs) and customized MedDRA queries. The Applicant’s search strategies for 
AESI appeared reasonable. All deaths, AEs of acute or chronic pancreatitis, and MACE, were 
adjudicated by an independent CEC. In my review, I also used FDA MedDRA queries (FMQs) for 
selected AEs and AESI, and cross-checked selected events with those provided with the NDA 
safety documents, with excellent agreement overall.

In conclusion, the Applicant’s safety definitions, coding, and approach to AESI are all 
acceptable.

## Routine Clinical Tests

Routine clinical testing in phase 3 CWM trials GPHK and GPHL was reasonable for the size and 
scope of the clinical development program.

The trials included routine clinical laboratory testing of blood and urine at baseline, every 12 
weeks during the treatment period (Weeks 12, 24, 36, 48, and 60), at the end of treatment
(Week 72) or early termination, and at the 4-week safety follow-up. Blood specimens for 
evaluation of lipid and glycemic parameters were collected under fasted conditions.

The vital signs pulse, blood pressure, and weight were collected at the visits above for 
laboratory tests, and also at scheduled visits every four weeks at the start of the treatment
period (Weeks 4, 8, 12, 16, and 20). Pulse and blood pressure were measured in triplicate in the 
sitting position using an automated machine. 12-lead ECGs were obtained at baseline, Week 24, 
Week 72, and safety follow-up. Physical examination was conducted at baseline, with only 
targeted physical examinations as needed at subsequent study visits.

The Applicant analyzed laboratory, vital sign, and ECG data using central tendency measures 
and categorical shifts (e.g., from normal or high to low, and from normal or low to high).

#### 8.4.1. Deaths

In CWM trials of tirzepatide submitted with this NDA, 13 deaths occurred in phase 3 trials GPHK and GPHL, and no deaths occurred in the phase 1 trial gastric emptying study GPHU. There was no apparent imbalance in deaths between the tirzepatide and placebo arms, with 9 deaths in tirzepatide arms (0.36%) and 4 in placebo arms (0.42%). There were no apparent trends in the causes of death, which appeared consistent with the expected morbidity and mortality in the trial population (Table 42).

Nine deaths occurred in subjects receiving tirzepatide:

- Three deaths were attributed to COVID-19 pneumonia and respiratory failure.

- Three deaths were due to accident or trauma:

Subject (b) (6) was a passenger in a taxi that had a road traffic accident, and he died from multiple injuries.

Subject (b) (6) was the victim of a homicide.

o Subject was in a house fire and found dead and burned by firefighters.

Reviewer comment: I agree with the Applicant's assessment that these six deaths due to COVID-19, accidents, or trauma are likely unrelated to tirzepatide.

- Two deaths were likely due to cardiovascular events:

The listed cause of death for subject is stroke. He was a 68-year-old Asian man with baseline BMI 28.5 kg/m2 and history of hypertension, dyslipidemia, and smoking. He was randomized to tirzepatide 10 mg, but on Day 30 he received his last dose tirzepatide 5 mg which was then permanently discontinued due to the adverse event of nausea. On study Day 325 (295 days after the last tirzepatide dose), the subject suffered a suspected stroke (as adjudicated by the CEC) and died.

The listed cause of death for subject is cardiorespiratory arrest. He was 60-year-old Hispanic man with baseline BMI 36.9 kg/m <sup>2</sup> and a history of T2D, hypertension, and COPD due to past smoking. He was randomized to tirzepatide 10 mg, but on Day 57 he received his last dose tirzepatide 7.5 mg. On Day 85, he died from presumed cardiorespiratory arrest which was adjudicated by the CEC as an undetermined death.

---

**_Reviewer comment: Obesity and diabetes raise the risk of ASCVD, and both subjects were 
men aged ≥60 years with multiple risk factors. It is unlikely that tirzepatide caused these two 
events, which occurred 28 and 295 days after the last doses of tirzepatide. The Applicant is 
conducting phase 3b trial GPIJ (SURMOUNT-MMO) which will evaluate whether tirzepatide 
reduces ASCVD events._**

(b) (6)
• One death was attributed to hepatic failure. The listed cause of death for subject 
is hepatic failure, but her case was complicated by alcohol and 
polysubstance abuse and COVID-19 infection, and acute respiratory failure. She was a 
45-year-old African American woman with a baseline BMI of 60.9 kg/m<sup>2</sup> and a history of 
cholecystectomy, hypertension, and prediabetes. Her liver laboratory tests were normal 
at baseline and at her last in-person visit on study Day 253, when she had lost 1.1% of 
her body weight taking tirzepatide 5 mg weekly. Prior to the events leading to death, 
the only adverse event she reported was arthralgia. Routine telephone visits on study 
Days 279 and 309 did not identify safety issues, but on Day 315 she was found 
unresponsive in her home by daughter and transported to the Emergency Department 
(ED) by ambulance. Per her daughter, in the days leading up to this event the subject 
had been drinking alcohol and taking increased amounts of ibuprofen and cannabidiol
(CBD) for joint pain; in the ED, her blood alcohol content was 54 mg/dl (upper limit 10 
mg/dl), and her urine drug screen was positive for cocaine and opiates. Her 
transaminases were markedly elevated (ALT 4185 U/L and aspartate aminotransferase
[AST] 4772 U/L), with a normal bilirubin and mildly elevated INR of 1.7. She was 
intubated and mechanically ventilated, then admitted to the intensive care unit (ICU) 
with the diagnosis of acute liver failure with hepatic encephalopathy, shock, and acute 
kidney injury in the setting of a positive COVID-19 test and evidence of alcohol and drug 
abuse. Despite aggressive ICU care, she died on study Day 319 and no autopsy was 
performed.

**_Reviewer comment: This is a complicated case. Although it cannot be ruled out that this event 
was due to drug-induced liver injury (DILI) caused by tirzepatide, there are multiple 
confounding factors including alcohol abuse. Furthermore, the subject’s low 5 mg dosage and 
lack of weight loss suggests potentially low exposure to drug, also making DILI unlikely. Liver 
safety of tirzepatide is addressed in detail below in Section 8.5.14._**

## Deaths in Ongoing Trials (Blinded)

The Applicant submitted a 4-month safety report update that reports 5 additional deaths in the 
CWM development program for tirzepatide, all of which remain blinded to treatment arm with 
no narratives provided:

• Subject <sup>(b) (6)</sup>in trial GPHK (SURMOUNT-1 extension study in subjects with 
prediabetes at baseline) died with adverse events of cardiac arrest, hypovolemic shock,

---

Clinical Review

Raymond Soccio, MD, PhD

NDA 217806

Zepbound (tirzepatide)

and multiple traumas.

• Subject <sup>(b) (6)</sup>in trial GPHM (SURMOUNT-3 for CWM after intensive lifestyle 
modification) died with acute myocardial infarction.

• Subject <sup>(b) (6)</sup>also in trial GPHM died with heart attack and pulmonary 
thrombosis.

• Subject <sup>(b) (6)</sup>in trial GPHN (SURMOUNT-4 for CWM after randomized 
withdrawal from tirzepatide) died with decompensated congestive heart failure.

• Subject <sup>(b) (6)</sup>also in trial GPHN died with metastatic colon adenocarcinoma.

**_Reviewer comment: Cardiovascular and cancer deaths are not unexpected in CWM trials, so 
these deaths in subjects on blinded tirzepatide or placebo do not change my safety 
assessment of tirzepatide._**

The 4-month safety update also lists other deaths in ongoing trials for other indications:

• In open-label T2D trial GPHD (comparing tirzepatide weekly to rapid-acting insulin 3 
times daily in subjects already on basal insulin), there were 7 deaths on tirzepatide 
(1.0%) compared to 11 deaths on insulin lispro (1.6%).

• In the blinded T2D CVOT GPGN, there were 561 deaths (4.2%) as anticipated for the 
population with established CV disease and thus elevated risk. Likewise, there has been 
one death to date in trial GPIJ (SURMOUNT-MMO) which is enrolling a population with 
established CV disease or at high CV risk.

• In the blinded heart failure trial GPID, there have been 15 deaths (2.3%) in a high-risk
population with chronic heart failure (NYHA class II to IV) with preserved ejection 
fraction.

**_Reviewer comment: Blinded cases are very difficult to interpret. Event rates will be calculated,
and causality assessments will be conducted when the trials are complete. Unblinded safety 
data from the ongoing CVOTs is being monitored by DMCs._**

## Serious Adverse Events

In the phase 3 CWM trials GPHK and GPHL, serious treatment-emergent adverse events (SAEs) 
were balanced between placebo and tirzepatide groups, and there was no apparent dose effect 
of tirzepatide (6.3%, 6.5%, and 6.3% for tirzepatide 5, 10, and 15 mg compared to 7.0% for 
placebo, see Table 41 above). SAEs by SOC are shown in Table 43.

**Source:** Generated by reviewer with Applicant’s pooled datasets for GPHK and GPHL (adsl, adae) using OCS Analysis Studio, 
Safety Explorer, and treatment emergent events in the safety population.

Relative Risk is calculated by comparing the TZP All to Placebo. A continuity correction of 0.5 (Yates, 1934) is used to calculate 
relative risk if any treatment arm has zero subjects.

The most common SOCs with SAEs occurring in >0.5% of subjects on tirzepatide were:

• “Infections and infestations” were balanced between the tirzepatide and placebo 
groups, with most common PTs being COVID-19 related.

• “Hepatobiliary disorders” were balanced and are discussed below in Sections 8.5.4 and
8.5.14.

• GI disorders were balanced and are discussed below as in Section 8.5.2.

• Neoplasms occurred more frequently in the placebo group and are discussed below in 
Section 8.5.15. An exploratory endpoint in the phase 3b trial GPIJ (SURMOUNT-MMO) 
will evaluate whether tirzepatide reduces the incidence of obesity-associated 
malignancies.

• Injury, poisoning and procedural complications occurred more often in the tirzepatide 
group, but there was no meaningful pattern to the PTs for these events as shown below 
in Table 44.

Four SAE SOCs occurred more than once in tirzepatide groups and not in placebo groups:

• Psychiatric disorders included anxiety, depression, substance abuse, and suicide 
attempt, as discussed below in Section 8.5.9.

• Metabolism and nutrition disorders included electrolyte abnormalities and dehydration, 
as discussed below in sections 8.4.6 and 8.5.3, respectively.

• The investigations were both COVID-19 testing and positivity, thus likely unrelated to 
tirzepatide.

• Pregnancy-related SAEs were an ectopic pregnancy and a spontaneous abortion,
discussed below in Section 8.8.2.

All treatment emergent SAEs are enumerated in Table 44. My review of these 160 SAEs in 
tirzepatide-treated subjects by PTs did not reveal meaningful differences between arms.

I reviewed SAEs in other trials from the tirzepatide development program, and these did not 
raise additional safety concerns:

• No SAEs occurred in the 36 subjects in the phase 1 CWM trial GPHU.

• The Applicant’s Summary of Clinical Safety and Integrated Summary of Safety provide 
tabulations of SAEs in analysis sets pooling CWM and T2D phase 2/3 trials.

• The Applicant’s 4-month Safety Update describes SAEs in blinded CWM trials, blinded 
and unblinded T2D trials, blinded outcomes trials, phase 1 trials, and trials for heart
failure, OSA, NASH, and CKD.

## Dropouts and/or Discontinuations Due to Adverse Effects

In the phase 3 CWM trials GPHK and GPHL (SURMOUNT-1 and -2), TEAEs leading to 
discontinuation from study treatment occurred more often in tirzepatide than placebo arms, 
with an apparent dose effect of tirzepatide (4.8%, 6.3%, and 6.7% for tirzepatide 5, 10, and 15 
mg, compared to 3.4% for placebo, see Table 41 above). TEAEs leading to discontinuation by 
SOC are shown in Table 45.

---

In summary, AEs leading to permanent discontinuation of trial product were higher with tirzepatide than with placebo, mostly due to GI AEs which are discussed below in Section 8.5.2.

---

#### 8.4.4. Significant Adverse Events

Table 46 summarizes significant TEAE categories of interest in the two pooled phase 3 CWM trials: events reported as severe, events reported as not recovered, and events leading to changes in study drug. The proportion of subjects with any AE was slightly greater in the tirzepatide than placebo groups (see Section 8.4.5), and events without recovery noted were balanced between tirzepatide and placebo arms. More subjects on tirzepatide than placebo had TEAEs leading to permanent and temporary treatment discontinuation and dose reduction, as expected given known common adverse reactions to incretin drugs described below (see Sections 8.4.5 and 8.5.2).

Clinical Review Raymond Soccio NDA 217806 Zepbound (tirzep

MD, PhD atide)

Two SOCs showed statistically significant differences in severe AEs between tirzepatide and placebo arms: severe GI AEs were more common with tirzepatide, while severe neoplasm AEs were less common with tirzepatide. Severe hepatobiliary and administration site AEs also showed apparent imbalances, and these and other severe or otherwise significant AESI are reviewed in depth in their respective subsections of Section 8.5, Analysis of Submission-Specific Safety Issues.

#### 8.4.5. Treatment Emergent Adverse Events and Adverse Reactions

Table 48 presents all TEAEs in trials GPHK and GPHL by SOC. The most frequently observed AEs were in the "Gastrointestinal disorders" SOC, with 55.7% of tirzepatide- versus 29.6% of placebo-treated subjects reporting at least 1 event. GI disorders are described in more detail in Section 8.5.2. Four other SOCs showed significant risk differences in the tirzepatide versus placebo groups:

- "Infection and Infestations" were less common in the tirzepatide groups.

- "General disorders and administration site conditions" were higher in the tirzepatide group, with the difference driven primarily by injection site reaction AEs (see Section 8.5.11).

- "Nervous system disorders" were higher in the tirzepatide group, with the difference driven primarily by dizziness AEs. Dizziness and nausea are related AEs that may be difficult to distinguish for some subjects, or occur concurrently, so TEAEs reports of dizziness could also reflect GI AEs.

- "Skin and subcutaneous tissue disorders" were higher in the tirzepatide group, with the difference driven primarily by hair loss AEs (see Section 8.5.12).

Table 50 shows an exploratory analysis to assess whether any common TEAEs differ meaningfully in occurrence in trial subjects with T2D (GPHL, SURMOUNT-2) compared to those without diabetes (GPHK, SURMOUNT-1). Subjects in trial GPHK (without T2D) were more likely to report any AE in tirzepatide arms (80.5%) than in placebo arm (72.0%), while subjects in trial GPHL (with T2D) were more likely to report any AE in placebo arms (75.9%) than in tirzepatide arms (74.5%). Some AEs appeared more common in subjects with or without T2D, based on differences in the risk difference of $ \geq1\% $ between trials GPHK and GPHL. For instance, tirzepatide-treated subjects in trial GPHK (without T2D) were 6-9% more likely to report AEs of nausea and constipation, but these differences do not meaningfully affect the overall safety profile.

*AEs that would differ in inclusion in Table 49 if this table were based on only trial GPHL (in subjects with T2D)

If the common TEAEs (from Table 49, informing the adverse reactions table in Section 6.1 of labeling) were based on either individual trial rather than the pooled data from trials GPHL and GPHK, there would be few minor differences as noted in Table 50. The only notable difference was that in trial GPHL, pyrexia TEAEs were reported in 27/623 (4.3%) of tirzepatide- compared to 4/315 (1.3%) placebo-treated subjects. Pyrexia (fever) is not addressed elsewhere in the labeling, and it is typically due to infection. However, common infections (with TEAE occurrence $ \geq 3\% $ in any arm) generally occurred less frequently in tirzepatide-treated subjects than placebo-treated subjects, even in trial GPHL where pyrexia occurred more frequently (Table 51).

Reviewer comment: While pyrexia was imbalanced in tirzepatide-treated subjects in trial GPHL, there was not a corresponding imbalance in common infections (which were less frequent in tirzepatide-treated subjects). Imbalanced pyrexia TEAEs were not observed in the larger GPHK trial for CWM, nor in the development of tirzepatide for treatment of T2D under NDA 215866. Therefore, the observed imbalance of pyrexia in trial GPHL is likely spurious and it is appropriate to exclude pyrexia from the labeled adverse reactions.

## Common Adverse Reactions for Labeling

For Section 6.1 of labeling, the applicant proposes a table of common adverse reactions that occur in $ \geq2\% $ of tirzepatide-treated subjects and more often than in placebo-treated subjects (Table 52). I agree with the proposed thresholds for inclusion of reactions, and my independent analysis of the safety database (see above) confirms the Applicant's incidence numbers in this table. My analysis above shows no meaningful differences in common TEAEs between trials GPHK and GPHL (i.e., subjects without and with T2D), so I also agree with having a single table with pooled results from both trials (as opposed to separate tables).

| Dizziness | 2 | 4 | 5 | 4 |
| Hypotensionf | 0 | 1 | 1 | 2 |

**Source:** Applicant’s proposed US package insert.

The footnotes for the proposed table include the following groupings, and I agree with them all:

• Diarrhea includes diarrhea, frequent bowel movements.

• Constipation includes constipation, feces hard.

• Abdominal pain includes abdominal discomfort, abdominal pain, abdominal pain lower, 
abdominal pain upper, abdominal tenderness.

• Injection site reactions includes multiple related adverse event terms, such as injection 
site bruising, injection site erythema, injection site pruritus, injection site pain, injection 
site rash, injection site reaction.

• Fatigue includes asthenia, fatigue, lethargy, malaise.

• Hypotension includes blood pressure decreased, hypotension, orthostatic hypotension.

**_Reviewer comment: I considered one other potential grouping of terms in this table: hair loss 
could include alopecia, alopecia areata, and androgenetic alopecia. As discussed below in_**
**_Section 8.5.12, the latter two PTs (which suggest mechanisms of hair loss) were reported only 
once each, so including these events in addition to the PT “alopecia” does not change the 
reported numbers in the table, which are rounded to the nearest whole number. A footnote 
noting these two specific types of alopecia would suggest incorrectly that these contribute 
meaningfully to the AE reports of hair loss. Therefore, I agree with including only the general 
term “Hair loss” (synonymous with “alopecia;” these two accounting for the great majority of
verbatim-reported terms) in this table without grouping multiple PTs._**

Overall, my review and analysis of common AEs agrees with the Applicant’s. I agree with the 
proposed common adverse reactions for labeling in the Section 6.1 table.

**_Reviewer comment: The common PT of “decreased appetite” (see Table 49) met the incidence 
threshold for inclusion in the label’s adverse reactions table, and it is labeled in this table for 
the tirzepatide for T2D. However, decreased appetite is not considered an adverse reaction_**

#### 8.4.6. Laboratory Findings

Changes in glycemic and lipid laboratory parameters were evaluated as efficacy endpoints and discussed in Section 6.1.2. Additionally, please refer to other relevant sections for information related to treatment-emergent changes in the following laboratory parameters: ADAs (Section 8.4.10), serum calcitonin (Section 8.5.1), pancreatic enzymes (Section 8.5.5), renal function (Section 8.5.3), and liver laboratory parameters (Section 8.5.14).

## Electrolytes

Table 53 summarizes shifts to abnormally high or low sodium, potassium, and calcium values in trials GPHK and GPHL. Shifts to low or high sodium values were balanced between tirzepatide and placebo groups, as were shifts to high potassium or calcium. Shifts to low potassium or calcium were significantly imbalanced (risk difference 95% CI not crossing 0) with more in subjects on tirzepatide than placebo:

- Those subjects with downward shifts in calcium had similar mean (±SD) and percentage declines from baseline in the tirzepatide group (-0.39 $ \pm $ 0.23 mmol/L, -16.6%) compared to the placebo group (-0.39 $ \pm $ 0.17 mmol/L, -16.4%). Two tirzepatide-treated subjects had postbaseline calcium values <1.5 mmol/L, and upon review both appeared to be laboratory errors given undetectable calcium values at the visit but normal values at the visits before and after.

- Those subjects with downward shifts in potassium had similar mean (±SD) and percentage declines from baseline in the tirzepatide group (-0.63 $ \pm $ 0.30 mmol/L, -15.8%) compared to the placebo group (-0.66 $ \pm $ 0.32 mmol/L, -16.2%). No subjects had postbaseline potassium values <2.8 mmol/L.

In conclusion, electrolyte changes and TEAEs related to abnormal electrolytes in subjects on tirzepatide were rare but appeared more common than on placebo. However, mean values for calcium, potassium, and sodium at end of treatment (Visit 21, Week 72) and their changes from Baseline did not differ meaningfully between tirzepatide and placebo arms. <sup>22</sup> Therefore, I do not think that electrolyte changes should be labelled for tirzepatide. AEs of vomiting and volume depletion will be labeled, and these can lead to electrolyte abnormalities that are clinically meaningful, so prescribers will already be alerted to this safety concern.

For these subjects with downward shifts in hemoglobin, the mean minimum hemoglobin ( $ \pm $ SD) was 11.16 g/dL in the placebo group and 11.14 g/dL in the tirzepatide group,with a mean decline from baseline of -1.70 g/dL in the placebo group and -1.93 g/dL in the tirzepatide groups.The difference of 0.23 g/dL is these changes in not clinically meaningful.

I reviewed other red blood cell (RBC) indices to investigate potential mechanisms of reduced hemoglobin. Shifts in mean corpuscular hemoglobin concentration are balanced between tirzepatide and placebo groups, suggesting no change in hemoglobin concentration per RBC. There is an imbalance in shifts to high mean corpuscular volumes (MCV), with more such shifts in the tirzepatide groups (Table 56). In all subjects at Week 72 (end of treatment), the mean MCV （ $ \pm $ SD) was 89.4 $ \pm $ 6.3 fl in the placebo groups (mean difference of -0.25% from baseline) and 90.2 $ \pm $ 5.6 fl in the tirzepatide groups (mean difference of +0.5% from baseline). Tirzepatide is also more associated more with shifts to low erythrocytes counts than with shifts to low hematocrits (see Table 55). Taken together, these data suggest that tirzepatide reduces the number of RBCs (erythrocyte count) and total blood hemoglobin concentration, with slightly larger RBCs (MCV) leading to a lesser decline in hematocrit, and unchanged hemoglobin concentration within RBCs (MCH).

In all subjects in phase 3 CWM trials GPHK and GPHL, the mean postbaseline minimum hemoglobin (g/dL) was 13.21 $ \pm $ 1.44 in the placebo group compared to 13.06 $ \pm $ 1.36, 12.97 $ \pm $ 1.46, and 12.97 $ \pm $ 1.44 in the tirzepatide 5, 10, and 15 mg groups. This difference in mean hemoglobin of 0.15-0.24 g/dL is not clinically meaningful.

Reviewer comment: These small decreases in hemoglobin and shifts to low hemoglobin values with tirzepatide are consistent across trials, as similar shift was observed in trials of tirzepatide for T2D as reviewed in NDA 215866. There is also an apparent dose dependence, with tirzepatide 5 mg having less of an effect on hemoglobin compared to 10 mg and 15 mg. The mechanism of this effect of tirzepatide on hemoglobin is unknown, but the small effect size (0.23 mg/dL) is unlikely to be clinically meaningful.

Downward shifts imbalanced in the tirzepatide over placebo groups are also noted for white blood cell (WBC) counts, including total leukocytes, lymphocytes, and neutrophils (but not monocytes, basophils, or eosinophils, Table 57). Similar to the results for hemoglobin in all subjects in trials GPHK and GPHL, the mean postbaseline minimum values for all WBC counts were lower in tirzepatide groups compared to placebo group, but the differences were small in magnitude (5-13%) and not likely to be clinically meaningful.

Reviewer comment: Unlike the effects of tirzepatide on RBC parameters, the effects on WBC counts did not appear to be dose-dependent. The small changes in WBC counts (~5% reduction in lymphocytes and neutrophils) are unlikely to be clinically meaningful. Low WBC counts could predispose to infections, so it is reassuring that tirzepatide-treated trial subjects had significantly fewer TEAEs in the SOC "infections and infestations" compared to placebo-treat subjects (risk difference -4.5%, see Table 48) and fewer common infections (see Table 51). Changes in RBC and WBC counts on tirzepatide merit further evaluation in the phase 3b development, in particular the ongoing long term outcomes trial GPIJ (SURMOUNT-MMO). To assess whether hematology parameters return to baseline after withdrawal of tirzepatide, the CSR for the completed randomized withdrawal trial GPHN (SURMOUNT-4) will also be relevant.

I also reviewed TEAEs in the SOC Blood and Lymphatic System disorders, none of which were serious or graded as severe in tirzepatide-treated subjects. There were numerically more TEAEs of anemia, leukopenia, and thrombocytopenia in tirzepatide than placebo groups (Table 58). Neutropenia TEAEs were rare and not imbalanced.

Source: Generated by reviewer

In conclusion, this review of hematology laboratory findings suggests that tirzepatide is associated with lower minimal values for RBC, WBC, and platelet counts. For RBC and some WBC counts, this led to shifts from normal to low values more frequently on tirzepatide than placebo, though no tirzepatide-treated subjects had serious or severe AEs corresponding to low blood counts. The apparent reductions in blood counts are small and unlikely to be clinically meaningful. Based on this existing data, I do not feel that the observed reductions in hematology laboratory values warrant inclusion in the tirzepatide labeling at this time.

## Creatine kinase

High levels of serum creatine kinase (CK) can indicate muscle damage or rhabdomyolysis. There was no imbalance in subjects with shifts to high CK values on tirzepatide (322/2181, 14.8%) compared to placebo (138/825, 16.7%). Table 59 shows the tirzepatide and placebo groups were balanced for subjects with maximum post-baseline CK >ULN (20.6% on tirzepatide versus 22.5% on placebo), but there was numerical imbalance for post-baseline CK $ \geq $ 5x ULN (2.1% tirzepatide versus 1.2% placebo) or CK $ \geq $ 10x ULN (1.0% tirzepatide versus 0.4% placebo). The TEAE "blood creatine phosphokinase increased" was balanced: 28/2519 (1.1%) tirzepatide versus 10/958 (1.0%) on placebo. The AE PT of "rhabdomyolysis" does not occur in the safety database, though two cases of markedly elevated CK due rhabdomyolysis are noted below (see Section 8.5.14 on li transaminase elevations in two subjects).

In conclusion, while CK elevations to $ \geq 5 x $ and $ \geq 1 0 x $ ULN were more frequent in subjects treated with tirzepatide compared to placebo, these elevations were rare, not dose-dependent (occurring more at lower tirzepatide dosages, yet similarly in the tirzepatide 15 mg and placebo arms), and not clearly clinically meaningful, and thus do not merit inclusion in labeling.

#### 8.4.7. Vital Signs

Tirzepatide is associated with reductions in blood pressure, which is an efficacy assessment described in Sections 6 and 7. This Section focuses on the increases in heart rate (HR) on tirzepatide, a known class effect of GLP-1R agonists. In the phase 3 trials GPHK and GPHL, at scheduled visits HR was measured in triplicate in a sitting position using an automated machine.

The tirzepatide-treated groups in trials GPHK (Figure 32): <sup>2 3</sup>

nd GPHL showed the following changes in HR

- Increases in HR over the first 20 weeks of treatment (dose escalation period), with mean changes at Week 20 of 2.8,5.1,and 4.1 bpm for tirzepatide 5,10,and 15 mg,compared to 0.1 bpm for placebo.

- Reductions in the degree of increased HR during the remaining treatment period (dose maintenance period), with mean changes from baseline at end of treatment Week 72 of 0.7,1.9,and 2.0 bpm for tirzepatide 5,10,and 15 mg,compared to -0.3 bpm for placebo.

- Reductions of HR to below baseline at the safety follow-up,4 weeks after treatment discontinuation, with mean changes from baseline of -2.5,-3.1,and -2.3 bpm for tirzepatide 5,10,and 15 mg,compared to -0.3 bpm for placebo.
