Compared to placebo, the occurrence of sinus tachycardia (defined as HR >100 bpm at any 
post-baseline visit) was more frequent in tirzepatide-treated subjects (5.1%, compared to 3.2% 
for placebo) and appeared to be dose-related (1.9%, 6.3%, and 6.0% on tirzepatide 5, 10, and 
15 mg, Table 60). Likewise, there were dose-related increases in the proportion of subjects with 
changes in HR from baseline >20 bpm at any visit (10.0% for tirzepatide versus 3.4% for
placebo), at two or more consecutive visits (2.6% versus 0.4%), or at three or more visits (1.4% 
versus 0.1%). However, persistent sinus tachycardia was rare and not meaningfully imbalanced 
between tirzepatide and placebo groups:

• HR >100 bpm at two or more consecutive visits: 1.2% versus 0.8%

• HR >100 bpm at three or more visits: 1.1% vs 0.9%

One subject in the tirzepatide 5 mg group had a post-baseline HR >130 bpm, but this value of

These tirzepatide-related increases in HR measured by routine vitals were confirmed by two 
other methods:

• Both trials GPHK and GPHL included routine ECG monitoring as detailed below in Section
8.4.8.

• Trial GPHK included ABPM substudy, which randomized 600 subjects 1:1:1:1 among 
placebo and the three tirzepatide dosages, with 24-hr ABPM conducted at baseline and

at Week 36, and these results (Table 61) were consistent with the increased HR
measured by routine vital signs around this timepoint during the treatment period (see
Figure 32).

This review sought to assess the clinical meaningfulness of increased heart rate. The TEAE of 
“palpitations” was balanced between tirzepatide (12/2519, 0.5%) and placebo (4/958, 0.4%), 
suggesting that tirzepatide does not affect subjects’ reporting of this symptom. More 
importantly, increased HR could contribute to MACE, but these were not increased by 
tirzepatide as detailed below in Section 8.5.13. It is also highly relevant whether tirzepatide 
increases the risk of supraventricular tachyarrhythmias like atrial fibrillation and flutter, and 
these events were rare in trials GPHK and GPHL with no increase in risk in tirzepatide compared 
to placebo groups (Table 64).

- Subject was 30-year-old man in Argentina randomized to tirzepatide 10 mg, who discontinued tirzepatide after study Day 249 due to the AE of depression. On study Days 353 and 375, he had two separate hospitalizations for atrial flutter and fibrillation, with an ablation procedure leading to recovery. Given that these events occurred over 14 weeks after stopping tirzepatide (and, as noted above, in trials mean HR returned to baseline or below at the 4-week safety follow-up), I agree with the Applicant's assessment that the serious atrial fibrillation and flutter TEAEs were likely unrelated to tirzepatide.

In the review of tirzepatide for T2D under NDA 215866, it was noted that the dose-related increases in HR were greater in magnitude in subjects enrolled at Japanese sites compared to sites elsewhere in the world. This same pattern was also observed in CWM trials GPHK and GPHL, with the greatest apparent increases in HR compared to baseline at relevant study visits (Weeks 20 and 72) observed in Japan, with lesser increases in the rest of Asia and the rest of the world (Table 65). Of note, subjects in trials GPHK and GPHL in Japan had lower mean baseline BMIs compared to subjects from the rest of Asia or the non-Asian regions (Table 66).

Table 66. Mean Baseline BMI in Japan Compared to Other Regions (Trials GPHK and GPHL)

| kg/m2, mean (SD) | TZP All | Placebo |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Japan | 31.5 (3.76) | 31.5 (3.61) |
| Rest of Asia | 34.1 (5.00) | 35.0 (5.52) |
| non-Asia Rest of World | 37.9 (6.72) | 38.2 (7.07) |

Source: Generated by reviewer

This suggests the potential for higher tirzepatide exposure in Japanese subjects based on body weight or other intrinsic or extrinsic factors. The same pattern in Japanese subjects (lower BMIs and greater HR increases) was observed in the tirzepatide trials for T2D reviewed in NDA 215866. As detailed in that review, the Office of Clinical Pharmacology (OCP) review team assessed the tirzepatide-associated HR increases based on PK and PD time-matched observations from seven phase 3 T2D trials. The OCP review team concluded that the concentration- $ \Delta $HR analysis showed a positive effect of tirzepatide exposure on HR increase, with a non-linear relationship between tirzepatide concentrations and $ \Delta $HR. The Japanese subjects appear to be more sensitive to HR increases than non-Japanese subjects under similar tirzepatide exposures. The exposure-response analysis suggests that the Japanese patients may need closer monitoring of their HR and the potential for associated cardiovascular events. However, a specific dose adjustment was not deemed necessary for this patient population, given that the proposed tirzepatide labeling recommends a gradual dose titration to an effective, tolerable dose, which may allow for control of potential AEs.

In conclusion, the observed dose-dependent elevations in HR on tirzepatide are consistent with other incretin drugs, and this merits inclusion in the label. The Applicant proposes labeling in section 6.1 on under the subheading "Other adverse reactions," consistent with the labeling of tirzepatide for T2D, and I agree.

Reviewer comment: There is no evidence that incretin-mediated increases in HR are clinically meaningful (symptomatic or leading to arrhythmias or CV events), so I agree that these do not rise to the importance of label Section 5 (Warnings and Precautions) for tirzepatide. A cardiovascular outcomes trial with tirzepatide in subjects with obesity is ongoing.

#### 8.4.9. QT

There were no events of Torsades de Points in tirzepatide trials for CWM, nor in the entire 
development program to date. A thorough QT study was not conducted for this CWM NDA, 
which relies on the QT assessment for tirzepatide under T2D NDA 215866, for which the 
Applicant performed *in vitro* evaluations and a concentration-QTc analysis of ECG data. The 
results of these analyses are discussed in the review of NDA 215866. Briefly, the 
Interdisciplinary Review Team (IRT) for Cardiac Safety Studies review (dated February 16,

addendum, Table ISS.4.56, page 1806 of 2079

Raymond Soccio, MD, PhD

Zepbound (tirzepatide)

2022)<sup>25</sup> noted that the submitted nonclinical and clinical data do not indicate any unexpected 
or important effects of tirzepatide on the QTc interval at clinically relevant exposures. 
Therefore, according to IRT, not including QT language in Section 12.2 of the label (Cardiac 
Electrophysiology) is consistent with the Agency’s labeling practices for peptides and large 
targeted proteins when a thorough QT study is not conducted.

#### 8.4.10. Immunogenicity

Tirzepatide is a peptide product that is highly immunogenic, based on its intrinsic potential as a 
peptide (based on endogenous GIP, and also sharing sequence homology with GLP-1), various 
quality parameters, and its SC administration route. This application was reviewed for 
immunogenicity by Drs. Faruk Sheikh and Harold Dickensheets from the FDA Office of 
Biotechnology Products (OBP), Division of Biotechnology Review & Research-2. The Applicant’s 
immunogenicity data is summarized below, and please see the OBP review dated October 10, 
2023, for further details. Overall, the OBP review recommended approval of this product from 
an immunogenicity perspective.

The Applicant had validated tirzepatide immunogenicity assays in a tiered system under T2D 
NDA 215866 but determined new assay “disease-specific cut points” in the CWM subjects, 
which the OBP review found acceptable. In phase 3 CWM trials GPHK and GPHL, treatment 
emergent ADAs were evaluable in 2467 tirzepatide-treated subjects; 1591 (64.5%) developed 
anti-tirzepatide ADAs, 1477 (59.9%) with treatment-induced ADAs and 114 (4.6%) with 
treatment-boosted ADAs (higher titers than baseline). The incidence of ADAs was slightly higher 
in trial GPHK (i.e., in subjects without T2D, 66.0%) compared to GPHL (i.e., in an older trial 
population with T2D, 59.9%) and to the shorter duration SURPASS trials for T2D (56.4%), but 
these differences are unlikely to be clinically meaningful and ADA development was high across 
all trials. ADAs also occurred similarly across tirzepatide dose groups but were consistently 
slightly higher at the higher tirzepatide doses (for instance, in GPHK/SURMOUNT-1 the 
treatment-induced ADA+ incidence was 60.1%, 61.1% and 63.7% with tirzepatide 5, 10, and 15 
mg).

The OBP review found the sampling times for measurement of ADA to be adequately designed. 
In all phase 3 trials for CWM or T2D, the development of ADA+ titer followed similar profiles
increasing over the treatment and follow-up period), with majority of ADA+ participants 
developing an ADA titer within 24 weeks and the number of participants developing an ADA 
titer almost doubling within 52 weeks. The median ADA titer was 1:160 for all studies, with a 
range of 1:20 to 1:20,480 in trials GPHK and GPHL (and up to 1:81,920 in the SURPASS trials). 
ADA titers peaked at Week 24, plateaued up to Week 48-52, and declined thereafter. High 
titers ≥1:5120 occurred similarly in ADA+ subjects in trial GPHK (60/1858, 3.2%) and trial GPHL

<sup>25</sup>Review of NDA 215866,

https://www.accessdata.fda.gov/drugsatfda_docs/nda/2022/215866Orig1s000OtherR.pdf

---

(19/609, 3.1%), and slightly less than in the SURPASS trials (137/3484, 3.9%).

Neutralizing antibodies (NAb) could not be assessed at sites in China, leaving 2444 subjects 
evaluable for NAb. Cross-reactive binding of tirzepatide ADAs to endogenous GIP and GLP-1 
occurred in 40.3% and 16.5% of these subjects, respectively, while cross-reactive NAb against 
endogenous GIP and GLP-1 occurred in 0.8% and 0.1% of subjects, both consistent with the 
greater homology of tirzepatide to GIP than GLP-1. NAb against tirzepatide activity at the GIPR 
and GLP-1R occurred in 2.8% and 2.7% of subjects, respectively.

There was no apparent effect of ADA or NAb on efficacy of tirzepatide for CWM. In trial GPHK 
of subjects without T2D, ADA-positive subjects lost a mean -20.5% of body weight on any dose 
of tirzepatide compared to -20.2% in ADA-negative subjects. Likewise, in trial GPHK of subjects 
with T2D, ADA-positive subjects lost a mean -15.0% compared to -13.8% in ADA-negative 
subjects (Figure 33).

Raymond Soccio, MD, PhD
NDA 217806
Zepbound (tirzepatide)

affect the PK of tirzepatide (refer to the Clinical Pharmacology review for further details) or the

**_Reviewer comment: High-titer ADAs or NAbs did not apparently affect the weight loss efficacy 
of tirzepatide. It remains unknown whether subjects who develop NAbs will have differences 
in weight regain (or glycemic control) after discontinuation of tirzepatide, as the 4-week 
safety follow-up in trials GPHK and GPHL is too short in duration to address this question. The 
completed trial GPHN (SURMOUNT-4) involves randomized withdrawal of tirzepatide for one 
year, so review of ADA data in this trial may help address this question._**

A greater proportion of ADA-positive subjects than ADA-negative subjects reported TEAEs of
hypersensitivity (6.2% versus 3.0%, see Section 8.5.6) or injection site reactions (11.3% vs 1.0%, 
see Section 8.5.11). These events occurred in subjects with ADA titers across the entire range, 
yet the 79 subjects with highest ADA titers (≥1:5120) appeared to experience slightly more
hypersensitivity (7/79, 8.9%) or injection site reactions (16/79, 20.3%) than subjects with lower 
ADA titers. There were no severe or serious injection site reactions. Three tirzepatide-treated 
subjects experienced severe but non-serious hypersensitivity reactions, and one with severe 
rash was ADA-positive while the other two were ADA-negative. The Applicant reports no 
pattern to the temporal relationship between ADA status or titer and these hypersensitivity or 
injection site reaction AEs.

In conclusion, no clinically meaningful association was observed between immunogenicity and 
tirzepatide drug exposure or weight loss efficacy. Immunogenicity was associated with the 
increased AEs of hypersensitivity and injection site reactions, and these will be addressed in the 
product label Section 6.1.

## Analysis of Submission-Specific Safety Issues

### 8.5.1. Thyroid Safety

<u>Medullary Thyroid Cancer and C-cell hyperplasia</u>

MTC is a relatively rare neuroendocrine tumor of the parafollicular C-cells of the thyroid gland, 
accounting for ~3% of all thyroid cancers.<sup>27</sup> Product labeling of long-acting incretin drugs
include a boxed warning of the risk of thyroid C-cell tumors, and include statements that these 
products cause in rodents thyroid C-cell tumors, including MTC, and that the relevance of these 
findings in humans has not been determined. Due to the nonclinical findings, long-acting 
incretin drugs are contraindicated in patients with a personal or family history of MTC or MEN2. 
Similarly, the tirzepatide labeling for T2D and proposed labeling for CWM include this boxed 
warning and contraindication.

<sup>26</sup>Applicant ISI Addendum, pages 35-42 of 46

<sup>27</sup>Jaber T, Dadu R, Hu MI. Medullary thyroid carcinoma. Curr Opin Endocrinol Diabetes Obes 2021; 28:540-6.

---

In a 2-year carcinogenicity study conducted in the tirzepatide nonclinical program, increases in
thyroid C-cell hyperplasia and neoplasia were observed in male and female rats. The incidences
of thyroid C-cell adenomas and/or carcinomas were increased in the tirzepatide 0.15, 0.5, and
1.5 mg/kg dose groups. The 1.5 mg/kg tirzepatide dose in rats is approximately equivalent to 
the proposed maximum exposure in humans of 15 mg weekly.

In the tirzepatide phase 3 CWM trials GPHK and GPHL, there no events of MTC or C-cell 
hyperplasia. Furthermore, no such events have been reported to date across the entire 
tirzepatide clinical development program.

## Other Thyroid Cancers or Neoplasms

There were two TEAEs of papillary thyroid cancer in the two trials, both in trial GPHK 
(SURMOUNT-1), one on tirzepatide (0.04%) and one on placebo (0.10%), and this more 
common thyroid cancer is not associated with incretins. No other malignant thyroid cancers 
(follicular, anaplastic, Huerthle cell, or poorly differentiated) were reported in these trials, and 
events related to benign thyroid nodules were rare and balanced between tirzepatide and 
placebo groups (Table 68).

**Table 68. Thyroid Neoplasm TEAEs (Trials GPHK and GPHL)**

| Any AE | 8 (0.3) | 4 (0.4) | 0.76 (0.23, 2.52) | ● |
| Thyroid mass | 6 (0.2) | 2 (0.2) | 1.14 (0.23, 5.64) | ● |
| Benign neoplasm of thyroid gland | 1 (0.0) | 1 (0.1) | 0.38 (0.02, 6.07) | ● |
| Papillary thyroid cancer | 1 (0.0) | 1 (0.1) | 0.38 (0.02, 6.07) | ● |

## Calcitonin

Serum calcitonin concentrations were monitored during trials GPHK and GPHL to assess 
whether tirzepatide affects C-cell function, potentially indicating the development of C-cell 
hyperplasia or MTC.

For both trials GPHK and GPHL, subjects were excluded for elevated calcitonin values at 
baseline, and significant elevations in calcitonin during the trial were defined based on eGFR:

• if eGFR ≥60 mL/min/1.73 m<sup>2</sup>

o subjects were excluded for calcitonin ≥20 ng/L at Visit 1

o in-trial calcitonin value ≥20 ng/L and <35 ng/L AND ≥50% increase from the screening value leads to repeat measurement in one month, and study drug 
discontinuation and endocrine follow-up if the calcitonin values showed a ≥10% 
increase

o in-trial calcitonin value ≥35 ng/L AND ≥50% over the screening value leads to 
study drug discontinuation and endocrine follow-up

• if eGFR <60 mL/min/1.73 m<sup>2</sup>

o subjects were excluded for calcitonin ≥35 ng/L at Visit 1

o in-trial calcitonin value ≥35 ng/L AND ≥50% over the screening value leads to 
study drug discontinuation and endocrine follow-up.

The Applicant presents data showing statistically significant increases in serum calcitonin upon 
tirzepatide treatment, with up to 20% increases at the end of treatment (72 weeks) which 
decline towards baseline 4 week later at safety follow-up. Figure 34 shows the percent change 
from baseline in serum calcitonin in trial GPHK, while Figure 35 shows the same data in trial 
GPHL.

**_Reviewer comment: These data strongly indicate that tirzepatide, similar to other GLP-1R
agonists, has direct effects on human C-cells to raise serum calcitonin levels. However, a 
normal calcitonin value is <5 ng/L and concerning values for C-cell hyperplasia or MTC are >50 
or >100 ng/L, so it unlikely that the 10-20% increase in calcitonin on tirzepatide is clinically 
meaningful._**

Calcitonin elevations to >35 ng/L (red line) were rare in the trial.

The Applicant presents analyses of shifts in serum calcitonin values during the trial. All baseline 
calcitonin values were ≤20 ng/L except for one subject in trial GPHL whose baseline value was
23.9 ng/L. Maximum postbaseline calcitonin levels were:

• >35 ng/L to ≤50 ng/L in 3 subjects: 2 on tirzepatide and 1 on placebo

• >50 ng/L to ≤100 ng/L in 5 subjects: 3 on tirzepatide and 2 on placebo

• >100 ng/L in one subject on placebo

There was no imbalance between tirzepatide and placebo groups in the number of subjects 
reaching these calcitonin thresholds.

An exploratory analysis of AEs was conducted, identifying the TEAE “blood calcitonin increased” 
in 33 subjects in Trials GPHK and GPHL, without imbalance between the tirzepatide and placebo 
groups (Table 69). None of these events were considered severe or serious, and 4 subjects on 
tirzepatide had drug withdrawn due to this AE.

## Thyroid Safety Conclusion

There was no evidence of increased risk of MTC, C-cell hyperplasia, or persistently elevated calcitonin levels with the use of tirzepatide based on the clinical data submitted. Therefore,

---

Clinical Review
Raymond Soccio, MD, PhD
NDA 217806
Zepbound (tirzepatide)

routine monitoring of serum calcitonin or thyroid ultrasound in patients receiving tirzepatide is 
unnecessary.

Since 2010, Sponsors of long-acting GLP-1R agonists have been required to participate in a MTC
surveillance study (registry-based case series of at least 15 years in duration) as a 
postmarketing requirement (PMR) to systematically monitor the annual incidence of MTC in the 
United States (US), and to identify any increase in incidence related to the introduction of longacting GLP-1R agonists into the U.S. marketplace. Considering the relatively short trial durations 
in the tirzepatide clinical development program, the latency of MTC, and in accordance with 
Section 505(o)(3) of the Federal Food, Drug, and Cosmetic Act (FDCA), the Applicant will be 
asked to conduct a postmarketing study to assess a signal of a serious risk for medullary thyroid 
carcinoma.

## Gastrointestinal Disorders

GI TEAEs are common with all incretin drugs, which typically employ dose escalation strategies 
to improve GI tolerability. Tirzepatide is started at 2.5 mg weekly and increased by 2.5 mg every 
4 weeks, thus taking 8, 16, and 20 weeks to reach maintenance doses of 5, 10, 15 mg weekly. 
Tirzepatide phase 3 trials include strategies to mitigate and manage GI symptoms, including 
advice on small meals, temporary use of symptomatic antidiarrheal or antiemetic medications, 
and study drug interruption, dose reduction, or discontinuation. Across trials GPHK and GPHL, 
antiemetics were used in 419/2519 (16.6%) of tirzepatide- and 62/958 (6.5%) of placebotreated subjects, and antidiarrheals were used in 168/2519 (6.7%) tirzepatide- and 15/958 
(1.6%) placebo-treated subjects.<sup>28</sup>

For tirzepatide CWM phase 3 trials GPHK and GPHL, TEAEs in the SOC “Gastrointestinal 
Disorders” occurred in occurred in 55.7% of tirzepatide- versus 29.6% of placebo-treated 
subjects (see Section 8.4.5, Table 48). Common TEAEs in this SOC include the PTs of nausea, 
diarrhea, constipation, dyspepsia, vomiting, abdominal pain, reflux, eructation, abdominal 
distension, and flatulence (see Section 8.4.5, Table 49). The occurrence of all GI AEs was not 
dose-related, but their maximum severity was dose-related: mild GI AEs were more common in 
the tirzepatide 5 mg arm while moderate and severe events were more common in the 10 and 
15 mg arms (Table 71).

pancreas safety.

To capture other related TEAEs, I analyzed the Applicant’s database using broad and narrow 
FMQs for nausea, vomiting, diarrhea, constipation, abdominal pain, and dyspepsia (Table 73). 
All showed a significant risk difference for tirzepatide over placebo, and all except constipation 
showed a dose effect with more events on tirzepatide 10 and 15 mg compared to 5 mg. 
Constipation showed the opposite dose effect, with more events on lower tirzepatide dosages.

Reviewer comment: There is animal data that GIP agonists exert anti-nausea effects, suggesting the possibility that tirzepatide's dual agonism at the GIP and GLP-1 receptors might allow better GI tolerability than GLP-1 only agonists. <sup>29</sup> This hypothesis is difficult to test since tirzepatide is a single molecule with both agonist activities. Potential differences in GI AEs between the GIP/GLP-1 dual agonist tirzepatide and GLP-1 only agonists are difficult to compare across trials, but future CWM trials with head-to-head comparisons in parallel arms could address this question.

## Gastrointestinal Disorders Conclusion

The prevalence, severity, and seriousness of GI TEAEs with tirzepatide are consistent with the extensive clinical experience with GLP-1R agonists, are monitorable and reversible, and severe and common GI adverse reactions will be clearly addressed in Sections 5 and 6 of labeling of tirzepatide for CWM.

### 8.5.3. Acute Kidney Injury

Acute kidney injury (AKI) and worsening of CKD may occur as hypovolemia due to drug-related GI adverse effects (e.g., nau lead fluid losses and potentially reduced intake.

result of dehydration or ea, vomiting, diarrhea) which can

## Dehydration

I analyzed the Applicant's safety database for trials GPHK and GPHL using the narrow FMQ for "volume depletion," which includes the PTs "dehydration" and "hypovolemia." These AEs were

<sup>29</sup> Borner, Tito et al. "GIP Receptor Agonism Attenuates GLP-1 Receptor Agonist-Induced Nausea and Emesis in Preclinical Models." Diabetes vol. 70,11 (2021): 2545-2553. doi:10.2337/db21-0459

Clinical Review
Raymond Soccio, MD, PhD
NDA 217806
Zepbound (tirzepatide)

rare, occurring in 15/2519 (0.60%) tirzepatide-treated subjects compared to 1/958 (0.10%) of 
placebo-treated subjects, for a 0.49% risk difference that was significant (95% CI 0.13 to 0.85). 
The Applicant’s SMQ-based analysis gave identical results. The Applicant reviewed case 
narratives<sup>30</sup> for the 15 tirzepatide-treated subjects with dehydration:

• 5 reported GI AEs of diarrhea and/or vomiting shortly before or at the time of 
dehydration AE. 3 of the 5 subjects had a medical history of CKD, and 1 also had 
concomitant medications of hydrochlorothiazide and empagliflozin.

• 3 reported GI AEs of nausea only.

• 4 reported other concurrent medical conditions shortly before or at the time of 
dehydration AE (pyelonephritis, pneumonia, acute gastroenteritis caused by food 
poisoning, and upper GI bleeding/peptic ulcer).

• 1 had CKD at baseline as the only identifiable risk factor for dehydration.

• 2 did not report any GI TEAEs and did not have known risk factors for dehydration.

3 tirzepatide-treated subjects had serious or severe dehydration AEs:

• Subject  <sup>(b) (6)</sup>was a 56-year-old female randomized to tirzepatide 15 mg 
with a severe but not serious dehydration AE with vomiting at the time of onset, who 
recovered with dose reduction.

• Subject  <sup>(b) (6)</sup>was a 55-year-old male randomized to tirzepatide 10 mg with 
a severe and serious dehydration AE in the setting of gastroenteritis due to food 
poisoning and AKI, who recovered in two days with a dose interruption.

• Subject  <sup>(b) (6)</sup>was a 60-year-old male randomized to tirzepatide 15 mg 
with a serious dehydration event of moderate severity, in the setting of nausea and 
vomiting, CKD, empagliflozin and hydrochlorothiazide use. Study drug was interrupted,
and he recovered in 2 days.

Only one subject  <sup>(b) (6)</sup>discontinued study drug due to dehydration, a 62-yearold female randomized to tirzepatide 10 mg who also had vomiting and AKI at the time of 
moderate dehydration, who recovered in one day.

## Acute Kidney Injury

I analyzed the Applicant’s safety database for trials GPHK and GPHL using the narrow FMQ for 
“acute kidney injury,” which includes the PTs “acute kidney injury” and “renal injury.” The 
“renal injury” TEAE was apparently a physical injury (“left kidney injury” occurring along with 
contusion/left chest bruise”) and should not be included. “Acute kidney injury” TEAEs were 
rare, occurring in 13/2519 (0.52%) tirzepatide-treated subjects compared to 2/958 (0.21%) of 
placebo-treated subjects, for a 0.31% risk difference of that was not significant. The Applicant’s 
SMQ-based analysis gave similar results using the narrow SMQ “acute renal failure”: 0.64% and 
0.31% for tirzepatide and placebo groups, respectively.<sup>31</sup>

<sup>30</sup>Details are in the SCS addendum, Table 2.7.4.6, page 30 of 73

<sup>31</sup>ISS addendum, Table ISS.4.24, page 1417 of 2079

---

The Applicant identified 11 subjects in trials GPHK and GPHL with serious or severe “acute 
kidney injury” or “renal failure” events, 9 (0.36%) in tirzepatide groups and 2 (0.21%) in placebo 
groups. Case narratives were reviewed for the 9 tirzepatide-treated subjects:

• 5 subjects had concurrent serious medical conditions leading to acute kidney injury:

o Subject  <sup>(b) (6)</sup>had AKI (severe/serious) in the setting of acute liver 
failure and multiorgan failure which led to death, as discussed in Section 8.4.1.

o Subject

o Subject  <sup>(b) (6)</sup>had AKI (severe/nonserious) in the setting of the 
severe AE of ulcerative esophagitis, which likely led to dehydration.

• 2 subjects had nausea and vomiting leading to dehydration:

o Subject  <sup>(b) (6)</sup>had AKI (severe/serious) in the setting of food 
poisoning, gastroenteritis, and dehydration (discussed above), who recovered in 
14 days with drug interruption.

o Subject  <sup>(b) (6)</sup>had AKI (severe/serious) in the setting of nausea, 
vomiting, and diarrhea, and recovered in 11 days with drug interruption.

• 2 subjects had renal injury unlikely related to tirzepatide:

o Subject  <sup>(b) (6)</sup>had renal failure (moderate/serious) and was 
diagnosed with granulomatous tubulointerstitial nephritis, which not associated
with dehydration or with tirzepatide or other incretin drugs.

o Subject  <sup>(b) (6)</sup>had AKI (severe/serious) over one year after having 
discontinued study drug.

Limiting the analysis to serious adverse events (thus excluding the two events above graded as 
severe but non-serious), these occurred with slight imbalance in:

• Trial GPHK, in 4/1896 (0.21%) tirzepatide-treated versus 1/643 (0.16%) placebo-treated.

• Trial GPHL, in 3/623 (0.48%) tirzepatide-treated versus 1/315 (0.31%) placebo-treated.

• Both trials, in 7/2519 (0.27%) tirzepatide-treated versus 2/958 (0.21%) placebo-treated.

Five tirzepatide-treated subjects (all in trial GPHK) had non-serious mild or moderate severity 
acute kidney injury events:

• Three had GI AEs as likely contributors to kidney injury:

o Subject <sup>(b) (6)</sup>reported moderate vomiting on Day 62, and on Day 
71 reported severe vomiting, dehydration, AKI, metabolic acidosis, and 
hypokalemia, leading to study drug discontinuation due to severe vomiting.

o <sup>(b) (6)</sup>reported nausea and hypotension on study Day 28, renal 
impairment on study Day 29, and discontinued study drug on Day 29 due to nausea. eGFR (mL/min/1.73 m<sup>2</sup>) was 62 at baseline, minimum 47 at Week 4, and 
near baseline 61 at Week 36.

o Subject <sup>(b) (6)</sup>reported vomiting on study Days 71-80, then 
hypotension and AKI on Day 92, all recovered without change to study drug 
dose.

• One had dehydration without reported GI AEs:

o Subject <sup>(b) (6)</sup>reported on Day 154 AKI, dehydration, hypotension, 
and hypokalemia. All were mild-to-moderate and recovered without change to 
study drug dose.

• One had confounding baseline renal insufficiency and ibuprofen use:

o Subject <sup>(b) (6)</sup>had baseline renal insufficiency (eGFR 49 
mL/min/1.73 m<sup>2</sup>) and ibuprofen use, with AKI reported on Day 113 and 
ibuprofen stopped on Day 162. Minimum eGFR (26 mL/min/1.73 m<sup>2</sup>) was on Day 
175, it returned to near baseline at the safety follow-up (44 mL/min/1.73 m<sup>2</sup>). 
Study drug dose was not changed.

Considering the serious and non-serious TEAEs potentially related to AKI, I also pooled the PTs 
“acute kidney injury,” “renal impairment,” and “renal failure,” showing:

• Imbalance in trial GPHK, occurring in 12/1896 (0.63%) tirzepatide-treated versus 1/643 
(0.16%) placebo-treated.

• No imbalance in trial GPHL, occurring in 4/623 (0.64%) tirzepatide-treated versus 2/315 
(0.63%) placebo-treated.

• Small imbalance in both trials pooled, in 16/2519 (0.63%) tirzepatide-treated versus 
3/958 (0.31%) placebo-treated.

**_Reviewer comment: Given that serious AKI events were rare (<0.5%) with minimal imbalance 
between tirzepatide and placebo arms (0.27% vs. 0.21%), and all such events were also rare 
(<0.7%) and imbalanced only in trial GPHK but not trial GPHL, these numerical incidences do 
not merit inclusion in label section 5 (Warnings and Precautions). This label section will
include a general warning about risk of AKI, likely secondary to GI AEs and dehydration._**

## Renal Safety

The Applicant also assessed renal TEAEs more broadly with the SMQs “acute renal failure” and 
“chronic kidney disease,” identifying such AEs in 19 (0.75%) tirzepatide-treated and 6 (0.63%) 
placebo-treated subjects. These events were more common in subjects with impaired renal 
function based on eGFR category at baseline, in both the placebo and tirzepatide arms (Table 
75).

**Source:** Applicant ISS addendum, Table 2.7.4.7, page 34 of 73

The Applicant also assessed renal function by laboratory measurement of eGFR and urine 
albumin to creatinine ratio (UACR), analyzing changes from baseline and categorial shifts. These 
laboratory results in trials GPHK and GPHL did not raise concerns about renal safety, as 
summarized by the Applicant:<sup>32</sup>

• Mean reductions from baseline in eGFR to Week 72 were small, with no clinically 
meaningful difference between tirzepatide groups (range: -0.1 to -1.1 mL/min/1.73 m<sup>2</sup>) 
and placebo (-0.4 mL/min/1.73 m<sup>2</sup>).

• The percentages of participants shifting to higher eGFR categories were comparable in 
the tirzepatide (3.3%) and placebo groups (3.6%). The percentage of participants shifting 
to lower eGFR categories was 22.9% for tirzepatide and 19.8% for placebo.

• The percent reduction in mean UACR from baseline at Week 72 was significantly greater 
in tirzepatide groups (range 19.1-24.7%), compared to placebo (6.6%).

• The percentage of participants who shifted to a higher UACR category was numerically 
smaller with tirzepatide (7.0%) compared to placebo (10.3%), while the percentage of 
participants that shifted to a lower UACR category was numerically greater with 
tirzepatide (7.5%) compared to placebo (4.7%).

**_Reviewer comment: I reviewed the Applicant’s data on renal laboratory assessments in trials_** 
**_GPHK and GPHL, as well as the pooled analysis set in the Summary of Clinical Safety, and I 
agree with the Applicant’s conclusions. The data for eGFR show no evidence of renal harm 
from tirzepatide, and the data for UACR suggest that tirzepatide may have a renal protective 
effect reducing microalbuminuria. The Applicant has renal endpoints in the ongoing phase 3b 
trial GPIJ (SURMOUNT-MMO), to test the hypothesis that tirzepatide improves renal 
outcomes._**

## Conclusion on Renal Safety

Events of dehydration and AKI were rare but occurred more often in tirzepatide compared to 
placebo groups, and renal TEAEs occurred more often in subjects with renal impairment at 
baseline. Case narratives for severe and serious events support the correlation with GI adverse 
events like nausea, vomiting, and diarrhea. Therefore, a labeled warning for AKI, already

<sup>32</sup>Detailed results are ISS addendum, Tables ISS.4.27-30, page 1429 oof 2079

Raymond Soccio, MD, PhD
NDA 217806
Zepbound (tirzepatide)

labeled for tirzepatide for T2D, is appropriate for tirzepatide for CWM. This risk of AKI can be 
mitigated by monitoring, so the label will advise providers to monitor renal function in patients 
reporting adverse reactions to tirzepatide that could lead to volume depletion.

## Acute Gallbladder Disease

Obesity and rapid weight loss are associated with an increased risk for gallstone formation.<sup>33</sup>
The Applicant notes that the most common historical illness in the GPHK and GPHL trial 
population was cholecystectomy (11.6%). Incretin drugs have also been associated with 
gallstone formation and related disorders, including cholelithiasis and cholecystitis, though it 
remains uncertain whether there are weight-independent effects. For one GLP-1R agonist 
(liraglutide for CWM), the label notes that “the incidence of acute gallbladder disease was 
greater in SAXENDA-treated patients than in placebo-treated patients even after accounting for 
the degree of weight loss.”<sup>34</sup>

The Applicant identified gallbladder-related PTs and note some imbalances with higher 
incidence in tirzepatide- than placebo-treated subjects. For instance, combining the PTs 
“cholecystitis” and “acute cholecystitis,” the incidence was 0.67% for tirzepatide and 0.21% for 
placebo. To capture a broader range of PTs that could indicate acute gallbladder disease, I 
queried the AE database with the broad FMQ “cholecystitis” (Table 76).

<sup>33</sup>Stinton LM, et al. Epidemiology of gallstones. Gastroenterol Clin North Am 2010 Jun; 39(2): 157-69, vii.

<sup>34</sup>Saxenda (liraglutide) prescribing information

There was no apparent dose-effect of tirzepatide for these gallbladder events, and this FMQ showed no statistically significant or clinically meaningful imbalance (1.95% tirzepatide versus 1.57% placebo). The Applicant's analysis <sup>35</sup> found a very similar result (1.98% tirzepatide versus 1.67% placebo) defining acute gallbladder disease by the narrow SMQs "gallbladder-related disorders," "gallstone-related disorders," and "biliary tract disorders."

However, both my FMQ and the applicant's SMQ analyses include two frequent PTs that may be problematic for defining acute gallbladder disease: "chronic cholecystitis" is inconsistent with acute disease and "cholelithiasis" could, in the absence of other AEs, represent asymptotic gallstones. Therefore, I generated a custom MedDRA query for acute gallbladder disease with the other terms from the "cholecystitis" broad FMQ excluding "cholelithiasis" and "chronic cholecystitis," and these AEs occurred in 23/2519 (0.91%) of tirzepatide-treated subjects and 2/958 (0.21%) of placebo-treated subjects, for a significant risk difference of 0.70% (Table 77).

The Applicant also conducted an analysis of acute gallbladder disease by maximum weight loss 
achieved.<sup>36</sup> 144 placebo-treated subjects achieved a weight loss from baseline of ≥10% to 
<20%, and events of “cholecystitis” or “cholecystitis acute” occurred in 1/144 (0.69%), 
compared to 5/897 (0.56%) of tirzepatide-treated subjects with the same degree of weight loss. 
No placebo-treated subjects lost ≥20% of body weight, and these cholecystitis AEs occurred in 
10/1114 (0.89%) of tirzepatide-treated subjects with ≥20% weight loss.

**_Reviewer comment: These results do not indicate a weight-independent effect of tirzepatide 
on acute cholecystitis, as the incidence was similar in placebo- and tirzepatide-treated 
subjects with 10-20% weight loss. Therefore, a labeling statement about potential weightindependent effects on acute gallbladder disease (like for liraglutide above) is not justified, 
and I agree with excluding such a statement from the tirzepatide label. The Applicant 
proposes a labeling statement that acute gallbladder disease is correlated with degree of 
weight loss, and, based on the data above, I agree that this statement is relevant to labeling._**

In the review of semaglutide for CWM (NDA 215256), in a randomized withdrawal trial after a 
semaglutide run-in, it was noted that gallbladder TEAEs occurred more frequently in the 
placebo than the semaglutide group, suggesting the possibility of a drug withdrawal effect. 
Tirzepatide trial GPHN (SURMOUNT-4) has a similar design, so when submitted the data from 
this trial will be reviewed for this potential withdrawal effect on gallbladder-related events.

## Acute Gallbladder Disease Conclusion

Acute gallbladder disease is a labeled warning (in Section 5) for most incretin drugs including 
tirzepatide for T2D, and I agree with the Applicant that it should be labeled here for tirzepatide 
for CWM. The caveats above about which PTs to include a combined query for acute 
gallbladder disease are beyond what can be included in the label, so I recommend that the label 
states incidences of the PTs cholelithiasis, acute cholecystitis (including, as the applicant

<sup>36</sup>ISS Addendum, Table ISS.4.39, page 1648 of 2079 proposes, unqualified “cholecystitis” but not “chronic cholecystitis”), and cholecystectomy.

## Exocrine Pancreas Safety (including Acute Pancreatitis)

Pancreas safety, including concerns about acute pancreatitis, has historically been an area of 
interest with incretin drugs. However, as the Applicant notes, both obesity and T2D raise the 
risk of acute pancreatitis, and it remains uncertain whether incretin therapy affects this risk. 
Post-marketing reports of acute pancreatitis, including fatal and non-fatal hemorrhagic or 
necrotizing pancreatitis with incretin drugs have led to Section 5 warnings in labeling regarding 
pancreatitis for the class. An imbalance in acute pancreatitis was reported in clinical trials (T2D 
and CWM) for liraglutide, but not for semaglutide.

An imbalance in acute pancreatitis is also noted in the labeling of tirzepatide for T2D.<sup>37</sup> In the 
trials for T2D reviewed under NDA 215866, 14 events of acute pancreatitis were confirmed by 
adjudication in 13 tirzepatide-treated patients (0.23 patients per 100 years of exposure) versus 
3 events in 3 comparator-treated patients (0.11 patients per 100 years of exposure).

The Applicant’s nonclinical program did not find evidence of pancreatitis, pancreatic cancer or 
tirzepatide-related pancreas pathology in mice, rats, or monkeys. Subjects with a history of 
pancreatitis were excluded from clinical trials of tirzepatide for CWM and T2D, so it is unknown 
whether a history of pancreatitis raises the risk of future events on tirzepatide. Trial subjects 
with diagnoses of acute or chronic pancreatitis were also required to discontinue study drug.

To maximally capture cases of acute pancreatitis in the tirzepatide clinical development 
program for CWM, the Applicant had broad criteria to send cases for adjudication of 
pancreatitis:

• all suspected cases of acute or chronic pancreatitis

• AEs of severe or serious abdominal pain of unknown etiology

• cases of pancreatic amylase or lipase values ≥3×ULN with symptoms

• asymptomatic cases of amylase or lipase values ≥3×ULN that underwent additional 
diagnostic follow-up.

Suspected cases were then adjudicated by an independent, blinded CEC, and the diagnosis of 
acute pancreatitis included at least two of the following three criteria:

• abdominal pain characteristic of acute pancreatitis

• serum amylase or lipase at least 3x ULN

• characteristic finding by cross-sectional imaging by computerized tomography (CT) or 
magnetic resonance imaging (MRI).

<sup>37</sup>Mounjaro (tirzepatide) prescribing information

---

Clinical Review Raymond Soccio NDA 217806 Zepbound (tirzep

MD, PhD atide)

In trials GPHK and GPHL, 31 potential cases of pancreatitis were sent for adjudication, and 7 cases were positively adjudicated, 5/27 in tirzepatide-treated subjects and 2/4 in placebo-treated subjects. <sup>38</sup> More subjects in tirzepatide than placebo arms were sent for adjudication due to AEs of abdominal pain and elevations in pancreatic enzymes. No cases were adjudicated as chronic pancreatitis or unknown (unable to determine), and no cases of acute pancreatitis were adjudicated as severe or critical. There was no imbalance in adjudication-confirmed pancreatitis, as 5/2519 (0.20%) tirzepatide- and 2/958 (0.21%) in placebo-treated subjects were each confirmed to have 1 event of acute pancreatitis. The Applicant also calculated the exposure-controlled incidence rates for adjudication-confirmed acute pancreatitis as 0.14 per 100 patient-years for tirzepatide versus 0.15 per 100 patient-years for placebo.

Reviewer comment: It is notable that an imbalance in adjudicated pancreatitis was noted in the tirzepatide phase 3 T2D trials under NDA 215866, but not for the phase 3 CWM trials GPHK and GPHL here. The balanced exposure-controlled rates of adjudication-confirmed acute pancreatitis in trials GPHK and GPHL are similar to those calculated by the Applicant using analysis set 4 of comparator-controlled phase 2/3 trials for T2D and CWM (including GPHK but not GPHL): 0.18 per 100 patient-years for tirzepatide compared to 0.19 per 100 patient-years for all comparators. Nonetheless, since an imbalance in acute pancreatitis events is noted in tirzepatide's label for T2D, it is reasonable to include the imbalanced T2D data in the proposed label for CWM since patients with T2D are contained within the indicated population.

I conducted a narrow FMQ for pancreatitis in the TEAE database, and found similar results, with 5 tirzepatide-treated and 1 placebo-treated subjects with TEAEs of "acute pancreatitis" or "pancreatitis." The FMQ also identified the TEAE "obstructive pancreatitis" reported in two placebo-treated subjects, and this TEAE is more relevant to the hepatobiliary disorders in Section 8.5.4 above. This analysis confirms that investigator-assigned pancreatitis TEAEs largely agreed with the CEC adjudication, though there were 3 differences.

I reviewed case narratives for the 7 CEC-adjudicated cases of pancreatitis, which did not raise concerns and the adjudications appeared appropriate.

## Changes in Amylase and Lipase

GLP-1R agonists increase amylase and lipase as a class effect, and in the development of tirzepatide for T2D under NDA 215966 it increased these by approximately 30-40%. In CWM trials GPHK and GPHHL, the results were similar, with increases noted by the first post-baseline measurement at Week 12 that were maintained until Week 72 (end of treatment), declining towards baseline at the 4-week safety follow-up. The increases were dose-dependent with greater increases in tirzepatide 10 mg and 15 mg arms compared to tirzepatide 5 mg. Table 81 shows the mean values and percent changes in pancreatic enzymes at Week 72, and Figure 39 shows their time profiles:

The Applicant pre-specified increase in pancreatic enzyme >3x ULN for additional diagnostic follow-up (mandated if symptomatic and by investigator judgement if asymptomatic).

- In Trial GPHK,15 subjects (all tirzepatide-treated,no placebo-treated) had amylase >3x ULN,and 3 cases were sent for adjudication. One case was positively-adjudicated as acute pancreatitis (subject in the tirzepatide 10 mg group had amylase >3x and $ \leq 5 x $ ULN and was also symptomatic with abdominal pain), while the other two were negatively-adjudicated.

- In Trial GPHK, 21 subjects (20 tirzepatide-treated, 1 placebo-treated) had lipase >3x ULN and 5 cases were sent for adjudication. Three of these are discussed above with amylase elevations: subject $ ^{(b)}(6) $ with lipase >3x and $ \leq5x $ ULN was positively-adjudicated, and two others were negatively adjudicated. Another subject was negatively adjudicated, and subject $ ^{(b)}(6) $ （in the tirzepatide 15 mg group, symptomatic with abdominal pain and nausea）was positively adjudicated.

- In trial GPHL,28 subjects (20 tirzepatide-treated, 8 placebo-treated) had amylase >3x ULN,5 cases were sent for adjudication,and all were negatively adjudicated.27 subjects (19 tirzepatide-treated,8 placebo-treated) had lipase >3x ULN,4 cases were sent for adjudication,and all were negatively adjudicated.

- In both trials, only subject had either amylase or lipase post-baseline maximum of >10x ULN (both met this threshold), but this subject was negatively adjudicated for pancreatitis.

## Pancreatic Cancer

In trials GPHK and GPHL, there was one case of pancreatic cancer in a placebo-treated subject.

## Pancreas Safety Conclusion

Since acute pancreatitis is a potentially life-threatening event that is labeled for the incretin drug class, I agree with the Applicant that it should be listed in label Section 5 (Warnings and Precautions). Elevations in pancreatic enzymes are of uncertain significance, so I agree with the Applicant that this should be included in the label Section 6.1 (Clinical Trials Experience) as a subheading under laboratory abnormalities. In tirzepatide CWM trials GPHK and GPHL, screening for elevations in amylase and lipase was of limited utility for identifying cases of acute pancreatitis, and positively-adjudicated cases all had other symptoms in addition to pancreatic enzyme elevations. Therefore, the labeling language for tirzepatide for T2D and for incretin drugs (i.e., the clinical significance of elevations in amylase or lipase is unknown in the absence 
of other signs and symptoms of pancreatitis) is also appropriate for tirzepatide for CWM.

### 8.5.6. Hypersensitivity

Hypersensitivity is generally included in Section 5 (Warnings and Precaution) of labeling for 
other incretin drugs, with a Section 4 contraindication for individuals with a history of a serious 
hypersensitivity reaction to the respective incretin drugs. Additionally, hypersensitivity 
reactions associated with incretin drugs, including anaphylaxis, have been reported in the 
literature.

In the tirzepatide clinical development program, the Applicant identified hypersensitivity cases 
by the following SMQs:

• Anaphylactic reaction (narrow and algorithm terms)

• Angioedema (narrow terms)

• Severe cutaneous adverse reactions (narrow terms)

• Hypersensitivity (narrow terms)

• Vasculitis (narrow terms).

Hypersensitivity was also classified as immediate (within 24 hours of study drug administration) 
or non-immediate (occurring more than 24 hours after study drug administration, but prior to 
the next administration).

By these definitions, in trials GPHK and GPHL,<sup>39</sup> 54/2519 (2.14%) tirzepatide- versus 4/958 
(0.42%) placebo-treated subjects experienced one or more immediate hypersensitivity 
reactions. There was less imbalance for non-immediate hypersensitivity reactions (3.49% 
tirzepatide versus 2.71% placebo). There was not an apparent dose-related effect of tirzepatide 
on these immediate (1.75%, 2.43%, and 2.13% for 5, 10, and 15 mg) or non-immediate (3.49%, 
3.16%, 3.83% for 5, 10, and 15 mg) hypersensitivity reactions. Pooling immediate and nonimmediate reactions (5.12% with tirzepatide versus 3.13% with placebo), the most common PTs 
were cutaneous (e.g., rash, dermatitis, urticaria, eczema). These PTs could reflect generalized 
skin reactions, or they may indicate local reactions at the injection site (see Section 8.5.11
below). Non-cutaneous hypersensitivity PTs identified by this analysis (clearly indicating 
systemic rather than local reactions) were rare and not meaningfully imbalanced (with 
percentages shown for tirzepatide versus placebo arms):

• rhinitis (0.23% vs 0.63%) or conjunctivitis (0.04% vs 0.21%)

• angioedema (0.04% versus 0%)

• bronchospasm (0% tirzepatide versus 0.10% placebo)

• eye swelling or eyelid edema (0.08% vs 0%)

• lip swelling, mouth swelling, face swelling, or perioral dermatitis (0.08% vs 0.20%)

<sup>39</sup>These results are summarized from the Applicant’s ISS addendum, Tables ISS.4.65-67, Page 1860 of 2079

---

Raymond Soccio, MD, PhD
NDA 217806
Zepbound (tirzepatide)

• mast cell activation syndrome (0.04% vs 0%).

No hypersensitivity reaction TEAEs were considered serious, and 3 were graded as severe (all in 
trial GPHK/SURMOUNT-1), one immediate and two non-immediate:

• Subject  <sup>(b) (6)</sup>randomized to tirzepatide 10 mg, had an immediate 
reaction of severe hypersensitivity on Day 114 (no further details of the reaction are 
provided) which resolved the same day. The same subject reported a separate 
moderate severity anaphylactic reaction on Day 505. Neither event led to treatment 
interruption or discontinuation, and the subject completed the trial on study drug.

• Subject <sup>(b) (6)</sup>, randomized to tirzepatide 10 mg, had a non-immediate 
reaction of severe rash, starting on study Day 80 and resolving 15 days later. Study drug 
was discontinued due to this rash (with last dose on Day 72). There was no rechallenge 
of study drug to test causality.

• Subject <sup>(b) (6)</sup>, randomized to tirzepatide 15 mg, had a non-immediate 
reaction of severe dermatitis on Day 74, progressive from mild dermatitis initially
reported on Day 70, with study drug interrupted then discontinued with last dose on 
Day 64. The severe dermatitis resolved by Day 81, and there was no rechallenge of study 
drug to test causality.

There were no treatment discontinuations due to immediate hypersensitivity reactions, and 
there were 4 discontinuations due to non-immediate hypersensitivity (2 for severe rash and 
severe dermatitis as above, plus 2 others for mild rash and mild injection site rash).

## Hypersensitivity conclusions

In CWM trials GPHK and GPHL, TEAEs consistent with immediate and non-immediate 
hypersensitivity occurred more frequently in tirzepatide than placebo arms, consistent with the 
results in the tirzepatide development program for T2D (reviewed in NDA 215866). Most AEs 
were cutaneous, few clearly indicated systemic hypersensitivity reactions, and none were 
serious. Overall, 3/2519 tirzepatide-treated subjects (0.13%) and no placebo-treated subjects in 
trials GPHK and GPHL had hypersensitivity reactions graded as severe, with one immediate 
reaction of hypersensitivity resolving the same day with no dose adjustment, and two nonimmediate reactions of rash or dermatitis resulting in study drug discontinuation.

Hypersensitivity will be labeled in Sections 4 (Contraindications), 5 (Warnings and Precautions), 
and 6.1 (under Common Adverse Reactions). The latter Section of labeling will report incidences 
of immediate and non-immediate hypersensitivity reactions as this information is relevant to 
prescribers, and it will describe the association of hypersensitivity reactions with tirzepatide 
ADA positivity (see Section 8.4.10 on Immunogenicity).

### 8.5.7. Hypoglycemia

GLP-1R agonists were originally developed as anti-hyperglycemic drugs to treat T2D. The 
Applicant cites references on incretin physiology, noting that both GLP-1 and GIP increase 
glucose-stimulated insulin release, but not in the setting of hypoglycemia. Furthermore, GIP is 
reported to stimulate glucagon release in the setting of hypoglycemia,<sup>40</sup> suggesting that the 
dual GIP and GLP-1 agonist tirzepatide may have less risk of hypoglycemia compared to pure 
GLP-1 agonists. For GLP-1 agonists, the risk of hypoglycemia is higher in subjects with T2D 
taking insulin or insulin secretagogues, and the same was seen in tirzepatide’s development for 
T2D reviewed in NDA 215866. Based on such findings, there is class label warning for 
hypoglycemia in T2D for incretin drugs.

Hypoglycemia is reviewed separately for trial GPHK (SURMOUNT-1) in subjects without T2D and 
trial GPHL (SURMOUNT-2) in subjects with T2D. To minimize the risk for hypoglycemia in trial 
GPHL, subjects taking insulin were excluded and those taking insulin secretagogues (like 
sulfonylureas [SUs]) at baseline were to have their dose halved (or stopped if already on the 
lowest dose) at randomization.

In both trials, the Applicant defined two categories of hypoglycemia:

• Severe hypoglycemia: an episode with severe cognitive impairment requiring the 
assistance of another person to actively administer carbohydrate, glucagon, or other 
resuscitative actions. These episodes may have been associated with sufficient 
neuroglycopenia to induce seizure or coma. Blood glucose (BG) measurements may not 
have been available during such an event, but neurological recovery attributable to the 
restoration of BG to normal was considered sufficient evidence that the event was 
induced by a low BG concentration.

• Hypoglycemia with BG <54 mg/dL (<3.0 mmol/L): primary analyses exclude any episodes 
that occurred after the initiation of a new anti-hyperglycemic therapy.

**_Reviewer comment: Rescue therapy for persistent hyperglycemia was much more common in 
placebo-treated (32.1%) than tirzepatide-treated (2.7%) subjects in trial GPHL (see Section_** 
**_8.5.16 below), so excluding episodes after rescue therapy initiation is appropriate to 
accurately compare the tirzepatide and placebo arms. Including such episodes would increase 
hypoglycemic events in the placebo group and thus reduce the signal for imbalance compared 
to the tirzepatide group._**

Severe hypoglycemia occurred in one subject without T2D in trial GPHK: subject  <sup>(b) (6)</sup>who 
had hypoglycemia in the hospital while experiencing hepatic and multiorgan failure, resulting in 
ICU admission and death (see detailed case narrative in Section 8.4.1 on deaths). Severe

<sup>40</sup>Christensen MB, Calanna S, Holst JJ, et al. Glucose-dependent insulinotropic polypeptide: blood glucose 
stabilizing effects in patients with type 2 diabetes. J Clin Endocrinol Metab. 2014;99(3): E418-E426. 
https://doi.org/10.1210/jc.2013-3644 hypoglycemia did not occur in any subjects with T2D in trial GPHL.

Table 83 shows that hypoglycemia with BG<54 mg/dl occurred:

- In placebo-treated subjects: rarely in those without T2D (0.16%) and more frequently in those with T2D (1.27%), mainly in those with T2D on SUs (3.19%) versus not on SUs (0.45%).

- In subjects without T2D: more in tirzepatide-treated subjects than placebo-treated subjects (1.53% versus 0.16%), with no dose-effect.

- In subjects with T2D: more in those tirzepatide-treated than placebo-treated (4.17% versus 1.27%),with a potential dose-effect (4.82% with 15 mg versus 3.53% with 10 mg).

- In tirzepatide-treated subjects: more in subjects with T2D (4.17%) than without T2D (1.53%), and most frequently in those with T2D on SUs (10.26%), much more than those with T2D not on SUs (2.14%, more similar to those without T2D [1.53%]).

The Applicant also calculated aggregated annual rates of hypoglycemia, but these were not informative in trial GPHL since 3 placebo-treated subjects on SUs had 30 hypoglycemic events, skewing the event rate in the placebo group, and minimizing the difference from the tirzepatide group.

While Table 83 shows the overall incidence of glucose <54 mg/dL, the Applicant provided additional context for the hypoglycemia occurring in subjects without T2D in trial GPHK, which did not include the routine use of glucometers, glucose diaries, and case report forms to systematically capture and report hypoglycemia as done in studies for T2D. In trial GPHK, 29 tirzepatide-treated patients reported 34 episodes of blood glucose <54 mg/dL and 1 placebo-treated patient reported 1 episode. 25/34 recorded episodes with tirzepatide (73.5%) occurred during oral glucose tolerance tests (OGTTs), with all 25 low glucose values reported at post-load timepoints. **_Reviewer comment: The final Section 6.1 of labeling will describe the incidences of 
hypoglycemia in subjects without T2D in trial GPHK under “Other Adverse Reactions”._** 
**_Episodes of reactive hypoglycemia post-load during OGTTs are not considered clinically 
meaningful, as they occur commonly in healthy individuals<sup>41</sup> and lack of specificity and 
sensitivity for diagnosis of hypoglycemia.<sup>42</sup> Therefore, this section of labeling will report trial_**
**_GPHK hypoglycemia outside of OGTTs: 6/1896 (0.3%) with tirzepatide versus none with 
placebo._**

## Hypoglycemia conclusion

Section 5 (Warning and Precautions) of labeling will include a subsection on “Hypoglycemia,” 
describing the risks in patients with and without T2D. For patients with T2D, the label will note 
the increased risk of hypoglycemia in patients taking insulin secretagogues (based on the data 
above from trial GPHL) or insulin (since this combination was well-studied in the development 
program for T2D, and is and labeled for tirzepatide for T2D). For patients without T2D, Section 5 
of labeling will include a general statement: “Hypoglycemia has also been associated with 
ZEPBOUND and GLP-1 receptor agonists in adults without type 2 diabetes mellitus,” with the 
incidences of hypoglycemia (outside OGTTs as noted above) in subjects without T2D reported in 
Section 6.1 of labeling. The risk of hypoglycemia is clearly greater in subjects with T2D, but the 
potential risk in those without T2D will also be addressed in Sections 5 and 6 of the label.

### 8.5.8. Diabetic Retinopathy

In patients with T2D, rapid improvements in glycemic control may be associated with 
temporary worsening of diabetic retinopathy. Increases in diabetic retinopathy complications 
were observed in a CVOT (SUSTAIN-6) of the GLP-1R agonist semaglutide, leading to Section 5 
labeling (Warnings and Precautions) about diabetic retinopathy complications for most incretin 
drugs.

In the phase 3 CWM trial GPHL (SURMOUNT-2) in subjects with T2D (as well phase 3 trials for 
T2D reviewed under NDA 215866), subjects with a history of proliferative diabetic retinopathy, 
diabetic macular edema, or nonproliferative diabetic retinopathy that required acute treatment 
were excluded based on a dilated fundoscopic examination performed by a qualified eye care 
professional during screening. Follow-up dilated fundoscopic examination was performed when

tolerance test: Cross-sectional analyses from a UK multi-ethnic population. Diabetes Research and Clinical Practice. 
2014;104(3):427-434. doi.org/10.1016/j.diabres.2014.02.013.

<sup>42</sup>Hall M et al. Metabolic parameters in patients with suspected reactive hypoglycemia. Journal of Personalized 
Medicine. 2021;11(4):276. doi: 10.3390/jpm11040276 clinically indicated by any AE suspected of worsening retinopathy. The applicant used a 
customized MedDRA search for potential diabetic retinopathy complications (Table 84).

The Applicant conducted similar reviews of diabetic retinopathy safety in the development of tirzepatide for T2D, as detailed in the review of NDA 215866 and the Applicant's Summary of Clinical Safety for CWM. These results in T2D trials are generally in agreement with the results above in CWM trial GPHK in subjects with T2D:

- No imbalances between tirzepatide and comparators were found in potential diabetic retinopathy-related PTs by custom MedDRA queries, with adjusted incidence rates of approximately 0.8%, comparable to trial GPHL.

- Some serious AEs (such as retinal detachment and retinal vein occlusion) occurred in the T2D development, but these were rare and balanced between tirzepatide and comparator groups (0.11% versus 0.10%).

- Across the phase 3 T2D program, worsening of fundoscopic exam results occurred in 0.36% of tirzepatide-treated subjects and 0.25% comparator-treated participants.The higher rates observed in trial GPHL may reflect the longer treatment duration.

The Applicant proposes listing diabetic retinopathy complications in label Section 5, Warnings and Precautions, and I concur. The Applicant is also conducting a dedicated diabetic retinopathy substudy as part of CVOT GPGN, which will better inform the risk of new onset or worsening

## Psychiatric Disorders

Suicidality and depression are safety issues of concern for all centrally acting obesity drugs.<sup>43</sup>
Among GLP-1R agonists for CWM, an imbalance in suicidal behavior and ideation was observed 
for liraglutide but not for semaglutide. Nonetheless “suicidal behavior and ideation” is currently 
labeled as a class effect for all CWM drugs, including GLP-1R agonists.

## Suicidality

There were no completed suicides in tirzepatide phase 3 CWM trials GPHK and GPHL. Across 
the tirzepatide clinical development program, there has been one completed suicide. Subject

<sup>(b) (6)</sup>randomized to tirzepatide 15 mg in the T2D phase 3 trial GPGH (SURPASS-3, 
reviewed in NDA 215866) had a history of depression and had the fatal serious adverse event 
“depression suicidal” on study 320. However, this subject had discontinued study drug on Day
78 due to elevated pancreatic enzymes, so this suicide (occurring 242 days after drug 
discontinuation) was unlikely related to tirzepatide.

In the tirzepatide phase 3 CWM trials GPHK and GPHL, subjects with severe past or present 
psychiatric disorders were not eligible for enrollment. Both trials deployed the Patient Health 
Questionnaire-9 (PHQ-9) and the Columbia Suicide Severity Rating Scale (C-SSRS)
questionnaires, which are often used in screening and monitoring of CWM phase 2 and 3 trials.
Specific exclusions were:

• History of significant active or unstable Major Depressive Disorder or other severe 
psychiatric disorder (for example, schizophrenia, bipolar disorder, or other serious mood 
or anxiety disorder) within the last 2 years. (Patients with depression or generalized 
anxiety disorder whose disease state is considered stable and expected to remain stable 
throughout the course of the study, in the opinion of the investigator, may be 
considered for inclusion if they are not on excluded medications.)

• Any lifetime history of a suicidal attempt

• Baseline PHQ-9 score of ≥ 15

• Any suicidal ideation or behavior on the C-SSRS that occurred in the past month: 
ideation of type 4 (Active Suicidal Ideation with Some Intent to Act, Without Specific 
Plan) or type 5 (Active Suicidal Ideation with Specific Plan and Intent), or any suicide 
related behavior (Actual Attempt, Interrupted Attempt, Aborted Attempt, Preparatory 
Act or Behavior).

Psychiatric diagnoses are common in patients with obesity or T2D. Per the Applicant, at 
baseline 18.3% of subjects in trials GPHK and GPHL reported at least 1 event in the SOC of

(phentermine/topiramate), and NDA 200063 (naltrexone/bupropion).

Psychiatric disorders. The most commonly reported terms were anxiety (7.7%), depression 
(6.8%), and insomnia (5.9%). Additionally, based on the PHQ-9 total scores, 24.0% of subjects

(b) 
The safety database for trials GPHK and GPH <sub>(4)</sub>were reviewed for suicidal ideation and
behavior. The sponsor used the “suicide/self-injury” SMQ, and I used the “self-harm” FMQ and 
custom MedDRA queries for all terms including suicide (i.e., Columbia suicide severity rating 
scale abnormal, Completed suicide, Depression suicidal, Suicidal behavior, Suicidal ideation, 
Suicide attempt, Suicide threat, Suspected suicide, Suspected suicide attempt). These analyses 
each identified the same three subjects:
(b) (6)

• Subject randomized to placebo had suicidal ideation.

• Subject <sup>(b) (6)</sup>randomized to tirzepatide 10 mg had two suicide attempts. 
She was a 22-year-old Hispanic female with baseline BMI 38.7 kg/m<sup>2</sup> with a history of 
anxiety. At baseline she answered “No” to all C-SSRS questions about suicidal ideation or 
behavior in her lifetime, and her PHQ-9 total score was 8 at Day -14 (her maximum 
recorded during the trial, scores of 5-9 indicate mild depression) and 3 at baseline on 
Day 0. On study Day 341 she had lost -22.1% of her baseline body weight and had no 
suicidal ideation or behavior by C-SSRS and a PHQ-9 score of 5. On study Day 360, she 
had a first event reported as a suicide attempt. This event was described as ingesting a 
large amount of alcohol, becoming anxious and agitated, then taking an unknown 
number of sertraline and fluoxetine pills before leaving home for 5 hours, then returning 
home to sleep. No action was taken with study drug since the subject did not report this 
event at the time, and her C-SSRS was negative for suicidal ideation or behavior 9 days 
after the incident (study Day 369). On study Day 370, a second suicide attempt was 
reported, with the subject experiencing agitation and back pain, and wanting to “take 
her life to end the pain.” She took an unknown number of clonazepam, quetiapine, 
bromazepam, and venlafaxine pills and went to sleep. She was found “sleepy” by her 
partner but was not taken to the emergency department and recovered. The subject 
also had adverse events of “panic syndrome” on Days 363 and 373, before and after the 
second suicide attempt. She saw her psychiatrist for medication adjustments on study 
Day 373, and study medication was discontinued by the investigator on Day 376. Three 
potential life events or triggers were identified in the case narrative: an abortion 5 years 
ago, two children she cared for recently moving to another city, and dissatisfaction with 
her body due to weight loss (“breasts falling to the abdomen”). Her only affirmative 
answers to C-SSRS questions on suicidal ideation and behavior were on Day 411, 
referring to the event since her last visit on Day 369, and subsequent answers were all 
negative on Days 431, 445, 519, and 539. Her PHQ-9 total scores ranged from 2-5 during 
treatment (Days 90-341) and from 1-5 after treatment was discontinued (Days 431-539). 
An association with weight loss or tirzepatide cannot be ruled out, though the case is 
confounded by pre-existing psychiatric disease and likely substance abuse.

• Subject <sup>(b) (6)</sup>randomized to tirzepatide 15 mg had one suicide attempt. 
She was a 30-year-old Black female with baseline BMI 42.2 kg/m<sup>2</sup> and a history of

depression and post-traumatic stress disorder (PTSD). At baseline she answered “No” to 
all C-SSRS questions about suicidal ideation or behavior in her lifetime, and her PHQ-9 
total scores showed no depression at baseline (1 on Day -14 and 0 on Day 0). On study 
Day 120 she had lost -10.9% of her baseline body weight and had no suicidal ideation or 
behavior by C-SSRS and a PHQ-9 total score of 2 on Day 88. On study Day 140, she was 
hospitalized for “an interrupted suicide attempt by scissors with intent to cut her 
wrists.” She had been taking sertraline but had recently discontinued it because the 
prescription ran out and she was unable to refill it. She reported a trigger event related 
to past sexual abuse by her step-brother, whom she had just seen on a family video 
chat. Sertraline was resumed and the dose was increased, and the study drug was 
interrupted from Days 139 to 207, when it was resumed after consultation with her 
psychologist. Her only affirmative answers to C-SSRS questions on suicidal ideation and 
behavior were on Day 152, referring to the event since her last visit on Day 120, and 
subsequent answers were all negative on Days 196, 224, 258, 280, 308, 336, 365, 393, 
424, 448, 480, 503, and 580. After the suicide attempt, her PHQ-9 total scores indicated 
moderate depression (defined as scores 10-15) on Days 258, 424, and 503 (14, 10, and 
10, respectively) but indicated no depression on Day 336 and 580 (scores of 2). This case 
was confounded by baseline depression, nonadherence to her antidepressant 
medication, and exposure to a triggering event for her PTSD.

These three subjects in trials GPHK and GPHL with events of “suicide attempt” or “suicidal 
ideation” were balanced at 2/2519 (0.08%) and 1/958 (0.10%) in tirzepatide- and placebotreated subjects. No other subjects with suicide-related AEs were identified by the Applicants 
“suicide/self-injury” SMQ and by my “self-harm” FMQ.

Based on “yes” answers to C-SSRS questions 1-5 there were additional subjects with lesser 
degrees of suicidal ideation, also balanced at 14/2519 (0.6%) and 7/958 (0.7%). The only 
subjects with suicidal behavior by C-SSRS questions 6-10 were the two above with suicide 
attempt AEs.

I also performed an exploratory analysis of psychiatric TEAEs using FMQs for “Depression” and 
“Anxiety.” Both of these FMQs, either narrow or broad, suggested a reduction in these events in tirzepatide- compared to placebo-treated subjects, with negative risk differences of 0.5-1% that were not significant (the 95% CIs cross 0, Table 89).

The Applicant analyzed shifts in PHQ-9 score categories (none, mild, moderate, or moderately severe) to identifying changes in depressive symptoms. <sup>44</sup> In this analysis, shifts to worsened depression were more common in placebo-treated (21.7%) than tirzepatide-treated (17.7%) subjects; conversely shifts to improved depression were more common in tirzepatide-treated (17.9%) than placebo-treated (13.7%) subjects.

Reviewer comment: Both my FMQ analysis for depression/anxiety and the Applicant's shift analysis of PHQ-9 scores suggest that tirzepatide may improve these conditions. These findings may be consistent with the observed improvements in psychosocial patient-reported outcomes (PROs) described above (Figure 20 and Figure 21, though these endpoints were exploratory and the changes were small and of uncertain clinical meaningfulness).

## Psychiatric Disorders Conclusion

In the tirzepatide CWM phase 3 program, and consistent with the T2D program reviewed under NDA 215866, there was no evidence to suggest an increased risk of psychiatric adverse events or suicidal behavior or ideation on tirzepatide. However, since suicidal ideation and behavior is listed as warning for other CWM products (including the GLP-1R agonist semaglutide which also has no apparent risk imbalance in clinical trials), for consistency with the class labeling I believe this warning should be included in Section 5 of the tirzepatide label for CWM.

### 8.5.10. Hypotension

To identify hypotension-related AEs in trials GPHK and GPHL, the Applicant defined a "Hypotension narrow" cluster including the PTs hypotension, orthostatic hypotension, and blood pressure decreased. These are the same 3 relevant terms that I independently identified in these trials using the narrow FMQ "hypotension." By these 3 terms in the narrow FMQ,

In addition to antihypertensive medications, other TEAEs could be causally associated with hypotension, such as hypovolemia due to GI TEAEs of nausea, vomiting, or diarrhea (as discussed in Section 8.5.3 above for AKI). Four of the 40 (10%) tirzepatide-treated participants with hypotensive event also reported temporally-related hypovolemia and/or AKI TEAEs. The Applicant conducted a manual review to identify potential causes of hypotension events (44 events in 40 tirzepatide-treated subjects). For 33 events (75%), no other relevant (potentially causal AEs) were temporally-associated with hypotension. The other 11 events, all in different subjects, had relevant potentially causal AEs detailed in Table 92. Based on this analysis, six subjects had diarrhea or vomiting as potential contributing causes to hypotension.

## Hypotension Conclusion

Hypotension will be included in Section 6.1 of the label, with a subheading under Common 
Adverse Reactions, noting the risk factors of concomitant antihypertensive therapy and GI
TEAEs leading to dehydration.

## Injection Site Reactions

Injection site reactions are common with incretin products administered by SC injection. In the 
tirzepatide development program, these reactions were identified using predefined MedDRA 
queries for spontaneous reports with high level terms “injection site reaction,” “administration 
site reaction,” and “infusion site reaction.” Based on this analysis, more subjects in trials GPHK 
and GPHL had injection site reactions in tirzepatide (192/2519, 7.62%) than placebo (17/1958, 
1.77%) groups, with a dose-effect of tirzepatide (5.71%, 8.33%, and 8.18% for 5, 10, and 15 mg). 
None of these reactions were serious or graded as severe. Two subjects discontinued 
tirzepatide due to multiple mild injection site reaction events:

• Subject <sup>(b) (6)</sup>(TZP 10 mg) had 5 mild events of injection site rash.

• Subject <sup>(b) (6)</sup>(TZP 15 mg) had 2 mild events of injection site reaction.

The Applicant also solicited qualitative information on injection site reactions using electronic 
case report forms (eCRFs). Based this analysis,<sup>46</sup> the Applicant notes that:

• Approximately one-half of tirzepatide-treated participants with injection site reactions 
experienced at least 2 events.

• Events typically occurred more than 6 hours after drug administration (71.8% of events), 
with 38.5% of events occurring between 24 hours and 14 days after tirzepatide 
injection.

• The most common symptoms were erythema and pruritus.

• The overall results as reported on the dedicated injection site reaction eCRF were 
consistent with the analysis based on the MedDRA search strategy in terms of the 
nature of events, and were also consistent with the pooled Analysis Set 1 (of placebocontrolled tirzepatide trials) in terms of the timing of reaction and reaction signs and 
symptoms.

<u>Injection site reactions conclusion</u>

Injection site reactions, including the association with ADA positivity (see Section 8.4.10 on 
immunogenicity), will be included as a subheading under common reactions in Section 6.1 of 
the label.

<sup>46</sup>From ISS addendum Table ISS.4.72-73, page 1891 of 2079

Clinical Review Raymond Soccio NDA 217806 Zepbound (tirzep 8.5.12.H

MD, PhD (atide) hair Loss

Weight loss by various methods (lifestyle, surgery, medications) is associated with alopecia, or hair loss. Telogen effluvium is thinning or shedding of hair resulting from the early entry of hair in the telogen phase (the resting phase of the hair follicle), which commonly occurs after severe physical, emotional, or metabolic stress, and it has been noted since the 1970s in patients rapidly losing weight by following extreme diets. <sup>47</sup> There is an extensive literature on hair loss following bariatric surgery, <sup>48</sup> which may be partially attributed to the stress of surgery and postoperative nutritional deficiencies and rapid weight loss. Hair loss is listed in common adverse reaction tables in the label for the most effective approved CWM drug, semaglutide 2.4 mg weekly (3% of semaglutide- versus 1% of placebo-treated adult subjects, with higher incidence also noted in pediatric subjects [4% versus 0%]).

As detailed in Section 8.4.5 under common adverse reactions, the PT "Alopecia" occurred more often in tirzepatide- (4.7%) than placebo-treated (0.8%) subjects. This PT accounted for 126 of the 128 subjects with hair-loss related TEAEs identified in trials GPHK and GPHL; one subject reported "alopecia areata" (autoimmune hair loss) and another "androgenetic alopecia" (male pattern baldness) and these two PTs are also included in the analyses below. I also reviewed the lower level terms (Table 93) and verbatim-reported terms (Table 94) for these AEs, and "hair loss" and "alopecia" were most common for both, and other events like "hair thinning" or "worsening of alopecia" were rare.

Notably, hair loss was reported almost exclusively in female trials subject (123/128 subjects with hair loss events, 96%) , and 7.2% of tirzepatide-treated females reported hair loss

---

Clinical Review
Raymond Soccio, MD, PhD
NDA 217806
Zepbound (tirzepatide)

compared to 1.3% of placebo-treated females. Therefore, I analyzed the incidence of hair loss 
in different demographic subgroups of only female trial subjects (Table 96). The incidence of 
hair loss (~7%) and relative risk (~5-fold higher with tirzepatide than placebo) in females were 
consistent across subgroups by trial (i.e., the overall difference between trials GPHK and GPHL 
was due to different sex ratios), age, race, ethnicity, country, and comorbidity, with two 
exceptions:

• The incidence of hair loss reports was notably higher in females at trial sites in Brazil 
(22.8% of tirzepatide- versus 4.8% of placebo-treated subjects), though the relative risk 
was consistent with other countries at 4.8-fold.

• The relative risk of hair loss appeared higher (at around 10-fold) in female subjects with 
anxiety/depression or osteoarthritis at baseline.

It is also relevant to labeling whether reported hair loss AEs were considered severe, led to changes in study drug, or resolved over time. In the 120 tirzepatide-treated subjects reporting

---

Raymond Soccio, MD, PhD

NDA 217806

Zepbound (tirzepatide)

hair loss TEAEs:

• 95 (79.2%) were reported as mild, 25 (20.8%) as moderate, and none as severe.

• One subject (0.8%) interrupted treatment, and none discontinued treatment due to hair 
loss. (One placebo-treated subject discontinued treatment due to hair loss).

• By the end of the trial, 62 (51.7%) were reported as recovered, 19 (15.8%) as recovering, 
38 (32.5%) as not recovered, and one (0.8%) as unknown. The Applicant notes that the 
trial duration may have been too short to fully capture the recovery from telogen 
effluvium events, and I concur.

I performed a time-to-event analysis of hair loss AEs in female trial subjects (Figure 41), 
confirming a lack of dose-effect. This analysis also showed a divergence of the tirzepatide and 
placebo curves at around Day 100, and a plateau around Day 300 (when the planned treatment 
period of 72 weeks continued for about 200 more days).

Finally, I analyzed weight loss at the end of treatment (Week 72) in subjects reporting hair loss 
during the trial compared to those not reporting hair loss (Figure 45). At each tirzepatide dose, 
subjects reporting a hair loss AE had an absolute >6% greater mean weight loss at Week 72
versus those not reporting hair loss: -21.8% vs -15.7% for tirzepatide 5 mg, -25.1% vs -17.9% for 
tirzepatide 10 mg, and -25.8% vs -19.5% for tirzepatide 15 mg (all significant differences based 
on non-overlapping 95% CIs). The few placebo-treated subject reporting hair loss had -2.6% 
mean weight loss compared to -3.3% in those not reporting hair loss.

**_Reviewer comment: As noted above, substantial or rapid weight loss by various methods can 
increase the risk of hair loss, and it is unknown whether tirzepatide further increases this risk 
by weight-independent mechanisms._**

Alopecia AEs were rarely observed in the development of tirzepatide for T2D. I queried the 
Applicant’s pooled analysis set 4 (all comparator-controlled trials) eliminating trial GPHK 
(SURMOUNT-1) for CWM, leaving data from all subjects (regardless of BMI) in phase 2 trials 
GPHB and GPGF, SURPASS trials 1-5, and SURPASS-J Mono. Hair loss AEs were reported in

---

<0.5% of all subjects and <1.0% of females in T2D trials (Table 97, note the results are not corrected for trial duration and exposure).
