Source: Generated by review from Applicants pooled dataset adsl.xpt and adsl.xpt

Reviewer comment: This is consistent with the review of tirzepatide for T2D under NDA 215866, which did not identify hair loss as a common adverse reaction. Even without correction for trial duration, the much lower incidence of hair loss TEAE reports in tirzepatide T2D trials compared to CWM trials (including trial GPHL in subjects with diabetes) is surprising. Even a T2D trial duration of 40 weeks (280 days) covers the typical period of rapid weight loss and hair loss TEAE reporting noted above.

## Hair loss conclusion

The Applicant proposed including "Hair Loss" in the label Section 6.1 table of common adverse reactions, and I concur. My analysis above showed markedly sex-imbalanced reporting of hair loss in CWM trials GPHK and GPHL, and the clear positive correlation of hair loss with degree of weight loss. I believe this information would be useful to prescribers and justifies a dedicated sub-header and paragraph in Section 6.1 addressing these higher risk subgroups.

### 8.5.13. Cardiovascular Safety

Some cardiovascular effects of tirzepatide are considered elsewhere in this review:

- Effects on blood pressure are considered for efficacy in Section 6.1.2.

- Effects of tirzepatide on pulse, ECG findings, and arrythmia risk are reviewed in sections 8.4.7 and 8.4.8.

- Hypotension safety is reviewed in Section 8.5.10 above.

- Cardiovascular deaths are described in Section 8.4.1.

This section will focus on MACE, which were CEC-adjudicated in trials GPHK and GPHL. Overall, MACE occurred in 16 (0.64%) tirzepatide- and 9 (0.94%) placebo-treated subjects (Table 98).

MACE events were more common in trial GPHL (SURMOUNT-2, 1.12% with tirzepatide and 
1.27% with placebo), which enrolled T2D trial population that was older with more other CV 
risk factors than in trial GPHK (SURMOUNT-1, 0.47% with tirzepatide and 0.78% with placebo). I 
reviewed case narratives for MACE, and these did not raise safety concerns for tirzepatide. 
These CWM trials (as well as T2D trials reviewed under NDA 215866) showed a trend towards 
numerically fewer MACE events in tirzepatide-treated subjects, and the Applicant is conducting 
phase 3b CVOTs in the T2D and CWM programs to test the hypothesis that tirzepatide reduces 
risk of MACE events in high-risk populations.

## 8.5.14.Liver Safety

Trials GPHK and GPHL excluded subjects with:

• acute or chronic hepatitis

• signs and symptoms of any liver disease other than NAFLD

• ALT >3×ULN

• Alkaline phosphatase (ALP) >1.5×ULN

• Total bilirubin (TBL) >1.2×ULN (except for cases of known Gilbert’s syndrome).

The Applicant identified hepatic TEAEs using a MedDRA SMQ approach. Overall, hepatic TEAEs 
were balanced between tirzepatide (1.95%) and placebo (2.09%) arms.<sup>49</sup> This agrees with my
FMQ analysis for “Hepatic injury” (Table 99).

<sup>49</sup>Applicant ISS Addendum, Table 1SS.4.31, page 1456 of 2079

Review of individual PTs for hepatic injury AEs was not informative, as these were a combination of clinical diagnoses (like "hepatic steatosis") and laboratory abnormalities ("transaminase increased") often co-occurring in the same subjects. Therefore, this review focuses on those subjects with severe and serious hepatic events and extreme laboratory abnormalities, in order to identify potential cases of DILI.

Six tirzepatide- (0.24%) and no placebo-treated subjects reported severe and/or serious hepatic adverse events:

- There was one serious event:

O Subject (b) (6) was the 45-year-old female randomized to tirzepatide 5 mg with the serious fatal AE of hepatic failure. Her death from hepatic and multiorgan failure in the setting of substance abuse and COVID-19 positivity is described in Section 8.4.1.

- One severe event led to study drug discontinuation:

O Subject was a 42-year-old female randomized to tirzepatide 10 mg who had the severe AE "liver function test increased." Her baseline ALT and AST were normal but were elevated to 482 (ALT >16x ULN) and 190 U/L, respectively on study Day 85 when she received her last dose of study drug. She underwent a comprehensive liver evaluation which identified positive antinuclear antibodies (1:40 titer in a speckled pattern). While systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE) often manifests as abnormal liver enzymes, no final diagnosis was reported. On study Day 225, last available results showed all liver laboratory tests in the normal range.

- Three severe events were isolated enzyme elevations that returned to normal without study drug discontinuation:

( b ) ( 6 ) was a potential Hy's Law case described below, with DILI ruled out due to a clear alternate etiology.

O Subject (b) (6) (tirzepatide 10 mg) who had elevations in ALT (434 IU/L), AST (not reported), and ALP (231 IU/L) on study Day 85. At baseline, ALT and AST were normal and ALP was mildly elevated (138 IU/L, ULN 129), and on Day 92, ALT and ALP were normal, and AST was mildly elevated (52 IU/L, ULN 40). Study drug was interrupted for one week, with dosing on Day 85 and resuming on Day 92. All hepatic labs were normal on study drug on study Days

---

167,253,334,421,and 505.

O Subject (b) (6) (tirzepatide 10 mg) had an isolated elevation in ALT. On Study Day 421, ALT was 197 U/L (4 $ \times $ULN), but on retest 8 days later, ALT was within normal limits. The subject had normal hepatic lab values for the remainder of the study and completed the trial on study drug.

- One subject had a severe event transaminase elevation noted during safety follow-up over one year after discontinuing study drug:

O Subject (tirzepatide 10 mg) had ALT and AST values within normal range at baseline, with only slight fluctuations in AST not exceeding 48 U/L during the treatment period. On Day 541, at the safety follow-up visit and more than one year after last dose of study drug, ALT was 264 U/L $ (5\times $ ULN) and AST was 437 U/L $ (10\times $ ULN), in the setting of a serious adverse event of acute kidney injury (see Section 5.5.3).

The Applicant also analyzed trial subjects with elevated maximum post-baseline values in liver blood tests above defined thresholds (Table 100). Such shifts were rare, but some shifts were numerically imbalanced occurring more in tirzepatide- than placebo-treated subjects.

The Applicant provided cases narratives for all subjects with elevations in ALT and/or AST $ \geq 5x $ ULN. I reviewed these and summarize below the 9 tirzepatide-treated subjects with identified post-baseline ALT and/or AST $ \geq 10x $ ULN (0.36%, compared to 0.10% in placebo-treated subjects):

- Two subjects had potential Hy's Law cases (DILI ruled out) described further below:

---

Zepbound (tirzepatide)

• Two subjects had transaminase elevation AEs graded as severe and described above:

o Subject <sup>(b) (6)</sup>(tirzepatide 10 mg) discontinued study drug with 
subsequent liver enzyme normalization.

o Subject  <sup>(b) (6)</sup>(tirzepatide 10 mg) had elevated transaminases 
noted more than a year after study drug discontinuation.

• One subject had isolated transaminase elevations that returned to normal without study 
drug discontinuation:

o Subject <sup>(b) (6)</sup>(tirzepatide 10 mg) had isolated Day 84 elevations of 
ALT and AST to 656 and 606 IU/L, respectively. These were potentially spurious 
since all other liver chemistries were normal before and after, and there was no 
interruption of study drug.

• Two subjects had elevated transaminases attributable to gallstone-related events (see 
Section 5.5.4), making DILI unlikely:

o Subject <sup>(b) (6)</sup>(tirzepatide 5 mg) had Day 54 elevations of ALT and AST 
to 658 and 246 IU/L, respectively, in the setting of a cholelithiasis AE. Study drug
was interrupted after Day 78 prior to elective lap cholecystectomy surgery on 
Day 84, which was complicated by acute pancreatitis on Days 88-95. By study 
Day 112, liver blood tests returned to normal and study drug was resumed on 
Day 117.

o Subject <sup>(b) (6)</sup>(tirzepatide 10 mg) had Day 337 elevations of ALT and 
AST to 447 and 288 IU/L, respectively, in the setting of a cholelithiasis AE. Study 
drug was interrupted on Day 351, on Day 370 the subject had cholecystitis and 
cholecystectomy. Transaminases declined towards normal and study drug was 
resumed on Day 395.

• Two subjects had on ALT elevations ≥10x ULN, both with a likely source from muscle 
rather than liver as events occurred in the setting of extreme exercise and creatine 
kinase elevations:

o Subject <sup>(b) (6)</sup>(tirzepatide 5 mg) on Day 338 had AST ≥10x ULN and 
marked CK elevation (29,745 U/L) in setting on new vigorous gym exercise, and 
AST normalized 8 days later.

o Subject <sup>(b) (6)</sup>(tirzepatide 15 mg) completed study drug treatment on 
Day 498, and at safety follow-up of Day 533 was noted to have AST 575 and ALT 
175 IU/L with marked CK elevation (34,257 U/L) in setting on new intense
exercise with personal trainer since end of treatment to maintain weight loss.

Transaminase elevations are more concerning for DILI if associated with elevations in TBL. For 
evaluation of drug-induced serious hepatotoxicity (eDISH), the Applicant generated eDISH plots 
to identify 3 possible Hy’s law cases (all in tirzepatide-treated subjects in trial GPHK). I 
conducted two similar analyses of the Applicant’s laboratory data from trials GPHK and GPHL
(using JMP clinical in Figure 46 below, and OCS Analysis Studio’s Hepatic Explorer [not shown]),

etiologies for the abnormal liver chemistry results:

• Subject  <sup>(b) (6)</sup>was a 27-year-old male randomized to tirzepatide 5 mg, whose 
elevated liver enzymes were confounded by concomitant use of albendazole (an

after albendazole was discontinued, such that study drug was resumed. He reported 
right upper quadrant abdominal pain from Study Days 250 through 252, while taking 
albendazole from Study Days 250 through 255. On Study Day 253, an isolated elevation 
of ALT activity was observed, followed by elevation of multiple liver enzymes, including 
ALT, AST, ALP, gamma glutamyl transferase, and TBL, between Days 278 and 284. On 
Day 278, ALT was 335 U/L, AST was 433 U/L, and total bilirubin was 0.7 mg/dL. On Day
284, laboratory tests revealed ALT elevated >3× ULN and TBL increased >3× ULN. On Day
281, he was diagnosed with cholecystitis, cholelithiasis, and hepatic steatosis, which 
were all reported to be ongoing upon study completion. Liver enzyme activity, including 
ALT, AST, ALP, and total bilirubin, returned to normal at the next laboratory testing, on 
Day 337. No further increase in any liver enzyme and bilirubin was observed from Day
337 through the end of the treatment period (Day 539). Study drug was temporarily 
interrupted from Day 253 and then reinitiated on Day 344. The subject completed the 
treatment period on study drug.

• Subject <sup>(b) (6)</sup>(tirzepatide 15 mg) was 47-year-old female whose liver 
chemistry abnormalities were attributable to acute gallbladder disease (see Section 
8.5.4) and returned to normal after endoscopic and surgical treatment, such that study 
drug was resumed. Her baseline weight was 100.3 kg, and on Day 169 she had achieved 
a weight loss of 18.6 kg. On Day 227 she last received study drug prior to interruption, 
on Day 236 she had the serious AE of choledocholithiasis with biliary duct obstruction 
(based on CT and MRCP), on Day 238 she was treated by endoscopic retrograde 
cholangiopancreatography (ERCP), on Day 276 she had a cholecystectomy, on Day 298 
study drug was resumed, and on Day 337 liver testing had returned to normal.

• Subject <sup>(b) (6)</sup>(tirzepatide 10 mg) was a 55-year-old male whose liver 
chemistry abnormalities were attributable to Hepatitis B, which resulted in study drug 
discontinuation. On Day 421, his ALT and AST were ≥10x ULN and TBL was 2.11 mg/dl in 
the setting of the AE Hepatitis B, and study drug was discontinued. He last received 
study drug on Day 422, and on Day 511 his ALT and AST remained mildly elevated (90
and 80 IU/L, respectively) with normal TBL.

## Shifts and Changes in Transaminases

The Applicant notes that there were statistically-significant decreases in mean transaminase 
values for tirzepatide groups compared to placebo groups in trials GPHK and GPHL.<sup>51</sup> For 
instance, Table 101 summarizes these data at end of treatment (Week 72) and safety follow-up 
(SFU) in trial GPHK. In tirzepatide-treated subjects, there were up to 30.3% declines in mean 
ALT and 14.2% declines in mean AST in subjects randomized to tirzepatide 15 mg (still 
significant at 12.3% and 3.9%, respectively, when placebo-corrected).

**_Reviewer comment: These reductions in liver enzymes could represents improvements in_** 
**_NAFLD, since the GPHK trial population had about 7% of subjects with baseline diagnoses of_** 
**_NAFLD, and undiagnosed NAFLD (including NASH) is likely present in even more subjects._** 
**_Given this hypothesis, I was surprised to see the reduced mean transaminases at Week 72 
increasing back towards baseline at Week 72. I reasoned that since weight loss is the likely 
mechanism of NALFD improvement, and weight loss is mostly maintained at the 4-week SFU, 
then reduced transaminases should also be maintained at SFU. However, the design of trial_** 
**_GPHK includes the 2-year treatment extension for subjects with prediabetes (who are also 
most likely to have NAFLD/NASH), and these subjects do not have 4-week SFU data due to the
extension, likely contributing to skewed transaminase results at SFU._**

<sup>51</sup>These changes over time are detailed in the ISS addendum, Tables ISS.4.35-36, page 1472 of 2079.

Reviewer comment: This exploratory analysis of trial GPHK subjects with elevated ALT values at baseline (likely reflecting NAFLD/NASH) shows potentially meaningful reductions in mean ALT values during the trial consistent with weight loss achieved, and largely maintained at SFU.

I also conducted a shift analysis for ALT values in trials GPHK and GPHL. Subjects with abnormal high ALT values at baseline were more likely to have normal values at Week 72 if randomized to tirzepatide versus placebo (89.2% versus 63.5%, Table 102). Conversely, subject with normal ALT values at baseline are less likely to have abnormal high values at Week 72 if randomized to tirzepatide versus placebo (2.9% versus 4.5%, Table 103).

Reviewer comment: The results above for mean ALT values and shifts in ALT values suggest a beneficial effect of tirzepatide in reducing or preventing ALT elevations, perhaps due to improvements in NAFLD/NASH. Consistent with this hypothesis, the treatment-emergent PT of hepatic steatosis was spontaneously reported in fewer tirzepatide-treated subjects (13/2519, 0.52%) than placebo-treated subjects (8/958, 0.84%). (b) (4)

## Liver Safety Conclusion

The review above includes case vignettes for all hepatic events in tirzepatide-treated subject graded as serious (n=1) or severe (n=6), all transaminase elevations $ \geq 1 0 x $ ULN (n=9), and all potential Hy's Law cases (n=3). Each case has a clear non-DILI etiology, except for subject (b)(6)

with the severe TEAE of transaminase elevation leading to study drug discontinuation. This subject underwent a comprehensive liver evaluation identifying antinuclear antibodies, but a diagnosis of SLE was not made which may have provided an alternate etiology. Given these overall results in CWM trials, and results across the development of tirzepatide, there is no evidence of risk for DILI with tirzepatide at this time.

### 8.5.15. Neoplasm Safety

For trials GPHK and GPHL, the incidences of pancreatic cancer (one in a placebo-treated subject) and MTC or C-cell hyperplasia (no cases) are discussed under exocrine pancreas and thyroid safety (Sections 8.5.5 and 8.5.1).

Trials GPHK and GPHL excluded subjects with:

- a family or personal history of MTC or MEN2

- a history of an active or untreated malignancy or are in remission from a clinically significant malignancy (other than basal or squamous cell skin cancer, in situ carcinomas of the cervix, or in situ prostate cancer) for less than 5 years

**_Reviewer comment: These malignancy-related events are rare, and I do not note any 
potential imbalances that are concerning._**

Most malignancy AEs were considered serious, and the malignancy AEs considered non-serious 
included skin cancers (basal and squamous cell) and papillary thyroid cancer. Serious AEs in the 
SOC “Neoplasms benign, malignant and unspecified (incl cysts and polyps)” occurred in 15/2519
(0.59%) tirzepatide- versus 13/958 (1.36%) placebo-treated subjects (see Table 43).

**_Reviewer comment: This analysis of serious neoplasm-related event suggests reduced risk 
with tirzepatide. The Applicant is conducting a phase 3b trial GPIJ (SURMOUNT-MMO) with 
an exploratory endpoint testing the hypothesis that tirzepatide reduces the incidence of 
obesity-associated cancers._**

I also searched the GPHK and GPHL safety database for benign neoplasms occurring in in two or 
more tirzepatide-treated subjects, which I identified as the five PTs identified in these trials in 
SOC “Neoplasms benign, malignant and unspecified (incl cysts and polyps)” but not in Table 104
above of potentially malignant neoplasms. Colorectal adenoma, lipoma, and meningioma had 
balanced occurrence in tirzepatide- versus placebo-treated subjects (Table 105). However, the 
PTs “uterine leiomyoma” and “leiomyoma” occurred in 12 of tirzepatide- versus no placebotreated subjects, for a significant risk difference.

1.18%). No reported TEAEs of leiomyoma were in trial GPHL (SURMOUNT-2, with T2D), and all 
were in trial GPHK (SURMOUNT-1), which enrolled subjects without T2D and a trial population 
that was younger with more females.

An information request was sent to the Applicant regarding called fibroids). The Applicant noted that:

the risk of uterine leiomyomas (also

- Two subjects had temporal relationships inconsistent with drug causality: subject discontinued study drug 395 days before leiomyoma was reported, and subject (b) (6) had leiomyoma reported on study Day 17.

- The 10 remaining subjects had marked obesity at baseline: all 10 had BMI $ \geq35.0 $ kg/m <sup>2</sup> and 5 had BMI $ \geq40 $ kg/m $ ^{2}. $

- Weight loss was generally substantial at the time of leiomyoma reports: 9 of these 10 subjects had lost $ \geq 10\% $ at the time of leiomyoma report, and 5 had lost $ \geq 15\% $.

- The two subjects with serious leiomyoma AEs had hysterectomies due to apparent mass effects: subject  <sup>(b) (6)</sup>for a pelvic mass diagnosed as leiomyoma, subject
<sup>(b) (6)</sup>elected for surgery as “she was worried about the feeling of 
abdominal mass.”

• The prevalence of leiomyomas is high, and 30% to 50% of premenopausal women with 
no previous diagnosis have ultrasound evidence of fibroid tumors, and obesity is
associated with increased risk of fibroids.

• There is no known biological plausibility to explain the apparent treatment difference.

**_Reviewer comment: I also conducted an exploratory literature search and found no potential 
connection between uterine leiomyomas and incretins like tirzepatide._**

The Applicant proposed detection bias due to weight loss, and the high general prevalence of 
leiomyomas, as an alternate explanation for the apparent treatment difference, with the 
following points supported by literature citations:

• The abdominal pannus limits the ability to palpate the uterus and ovaries,<sup>52</sup> and weight 
loss may have increased the ability of healthcare practitioners to detect gynecological 
masses.

• Obesity is associated with PCOS and ovulatory dysfunction. Weight loss restores 
menstrual cycle,<sup>53</sup> and in turn, this may have led to changes in symptoms related to 
menstrual periods, prompting further investigations, and increasing detection of 
leiomyomas.

• Weight bias in the health care setting has been associated with decreased routine 
gynecological screening.<sup>54</sup> Weight reduction may have increased the occurrence of 
routine gynecological exams in female participants.

I also queried the Applicant’s pooled analysis set 3 including SURPASS trials of tirzepatide for 
T2D. In these trials, leiomyoma was reported in 12/6884 (0.17%) tirzepatide-treated subjects, 
compared to 0/312 placebo-treated, 2/1580 (0.13%) subjects treated with active comparator 
insulin, and 0/622 subjects treated with other GLP-1R agonist active comparators (dulaglutide 
or semaglutide). The Applicant conducted a query of leiomyoma events in female subjects in 
pooled analysis set 4 and noted similar small numbers with imbalance that was not statistically 
significant (Table 107). The Applicant notes that the lower incidence of leiomyoma in trial 
subjects with T2D (CWM trial GPHL/SURMOUNT-2 and T2D SURPASS trials) is potentially 
consistent with underlying populations differences (older age and lower BMI leading to fewer 
leiomyomas) and treatment effects (less weight loss in subjects with T2D, leading to less of the 
proposed detection bias).

<sup>52</sup>Breitkopf, D.M. Lateral decubitus position to facilitate pelvic examination of the patient with severe obesity. 
BMC Women's Health. 2021;21(1):143. https://doi.org/10.1186/s12905-021-01289-2

<sup>53</sup>Lee R, Mathew CJ, Jose MT, et al. A review of the impact of bariatric surgery in women with polycystic ovary 
syndrome. Cureus. 2020;12(10): e10811. https://doi.org/10.7759/cureus.10811

<sup>54</sup>Aldrich T, Hackley B. The impact of obesity on gynecologic cancer screening: an integrative literature review. J 
Midwifery Womens Health. 2010;55(4):344-356. https://doi.org10.1016/j.jmwh.2009.10.001

---

**_Reviewer comment: The Applicant’s case for leiomyoma detection bias in trial GPHK
(SURMOUNT-1) is plausible, and only two subjects had serious events leading to hysterectomy
(apparently due to mass effects not bleeding). Given this and the lack of preclinical or 
mechanistic data supporting causality of tirzepatide, I agree with not describing leiomyoma 
risk in the current label. Leiomyoma events should be monitored in future trials and postmarketing data._**

## Neoplasm conclusion

Tirzepatide will include the class labeling for GLP-1R agonists about MTC, though no cases have 
been reported to date in clinical trials. There was no apparent increase in malignant tumors in 
tirzepatide-treated subjects. There was an apparent imbalance in the benign tumor leiomyoma
with higher incidence in tirzepatide-treated subjects, which may be attributable to detection 
bias.

## Other Diabetes-Related Safety Issues

The Applicant reviewed three additional AESIs relevant to subjects with T2D in trial GPHL 
(SURMOUNT-2, for CWM in subjects with T2D). For these AESIs, data from trial GPHL were 
consistent with the known safety profile of tirzepatide in subjects with T2D (reviewed in NDA 
215866).

## Metabolic Acidosis

The Applicant conducted a customized MedDRA search to identify potential TEAEs of metabolic 
acidosis in trial GPHK are SURPASS trials for T2D. The PTs queried were:

• diabetic ketoacidosis

---

Clinical Review
Raymond Soccio, MD, PhD
NDA 217806
Zepbound (tirzepatide)

• ketoacidosis

• euglycemic diabetic ketoacidosis

• ketonuria

• diabetic ketosis

• diabetic nonketotic hyperglycemic coma

• ketosis

• lactic acidosis

• urine ketone body present

• blood ketone body

• blood ketone body increased

• urine ketone body

• blood ketone body present

This search strategy identified no potential event of metabolic acidosis in CWM trial GPHL, 
consistent with no such potential events in either tirzepatide or comparator arms of trials of 
tirzepatide for T2D.

The Applicant concludes that the trial data do not suggest an association between tirzepatide 
and occurrence of metabolic acidosis, and I concur. There are rare postmarketing and literature 
reports of ketoacidosis in subjects taking tirzepatide and other incretins, but these are difficult 
to interpret.

## Hyperglycemia Requiring Rescue Therapy

The protocol for trial GPHL prespecified criteria (Table 108) that investigators were to use when 
considering the addition of rescue therapy in participants not reaching glycemic targets. 
Investigators were to first confirm the participant was fully compliant with the assigned 
therapeutic regimen and there was not an acute condition causing persistent hyperglycemia.

**Table 108. Criteria for Persistent Hyperglycemia Requiring Rescue Therapy in Trial GPHL**

| Criteria | Time period postrandomization |
| --- | --- |
| >270 mg/dL (>15.0 mmol/L)a | Weeks 0-8 |
| >240 mg/dL (>13.3 mmol/L)a | Week 9-16 |
| >200 mg/dL (>11.1 mmol/L)a | Beyond Week 16 |
| HbA1c ≥8.5% (≥69 mmol/mol) | On and after Week 24 (only 1 value needed) |

a Two confirmed fasting SMBG values over at least a consecutive 2-week period.

A total of 17 (2.7%) tirzepatide- and 101 (32.1%) placebo-treated participants were prescribed
rescue therapy due to persistent hyperglycemia in trial GPHL.<sup>55</sup> These results are consistent 
with the percentage of tirzepatide-treated subjects receiving rescue therapy due to 
hyperglycemic events across studies in the phase 3 T2D clinical program (0%-3.3%).

<sup>55</sup>CSR for trial GPHL, Table GPHL.8.16

---

Clinical Review
Raymond Soccio, MD, PhD
NDA 217806
Zepbound (tirzepatide)

The percentage of tirzepatide-treated subjects receiving rescue therapy due to persistent
hyperglycemia was low and meaningfully reduced compared to placebo-treated subjects. The 
Applicant concludes that there is no association of tirzepatide use with persistent
hyperglycemia, and I concur. Indeed, due to its antihyperglycemic effects, tirzepatide reduced 
events of hyperglycemia and the need for rescue therapy in trial GPHL as expected.

## Amputation or Peripheral Revascularization

The Applicant searched the trial GPHL database for the MedDRA PTs “amputation” and 
“peripheral revascularization,” and searched the Lilly Safety System case narratives for 
amputation events.

A total of 4 (0.64%) tirzepatide- and 3 (0.95%) placebo-treated subjects in trial GPHL had an 
amputation or peripheral revascularization events (Table 109).

The Applicant notes the following:

- Two of the 7 subjects had preexisting peripheral vascular disease.

---

Clinical Review Raymond Soccio, MD, PhD NDA 217806

## Zepbound (tirzepatide)

- There was a total of 6 amputations (3 in tirzepatide- and 3 in placebo-treated subject, of which 1 (tirzepatide 10 mg) was due to an accident (limb traumatic amputation). The 2 non-accidental events in the tirzepatide groups correspond to an event rate of 0.25 events per 100 patient-years.

- There was one peripheral revascularization event in a tirzepatide-treated subject in trial GPHL, compared to none in placebo-treated subjects, and corresponding to an event rate of 0.12 events per 100 patient-years.

- The event rates of amputation and peripheral revascularization in tirzepatide-treated trial GPHL subjects were consistent with those reported for participants with obesity/overweight in the phase 3 T2D studies (0.14 events per 100 patient-years for both amputation and peripheral revascularization).

The Applicant concludes that rates of amputation and peripheral revascularization in tirzepatide-treated participants in CWM trial GPHL were consistent with the known safety profile of tirzepatide for T2D, and the results do not suggest an association of tirzepatide with amputation or peripheral revascularization. I concur.

## Other Diabetes-related Safety Issues Conclusion

These three T2D-related AESIs (metabolic acidosis, hyperglycemia, and amputation) are not included in the tirzepatide label for T2D, and based on the review above I do not think they merit inclusion in the label for CWM.

## 8.5.17.Rare events

## Fall

My review of trials GPHK and GPHL noted the rare AE of "Fall" occurring in more tirzepatide (0.71%) than placebo-treated (0.21%) subjects, with a statistically significant risk difference (95% CI does not cross zero) and an apparent inverse dose-dependence (highest incidence of fall in subjects randomized to tirzepatide 5 mg, Table 110).

• The reported terms generally do not provide mechanism (except one “slipped on

• The study Days of the falls occur spread throughout the treatment period without a

explain the falls.

The Applicant notes that there was no pattern of potential causes or reasons for falls, and thus 
did not consider falls to be a safety concern for tirzepatide, and I concur. Based on the analysis 
above, the imbalance in fall events is unexplained but unlikely due to tirzepatide. Therefore, I 
do not think the tirzepatide label should include the rare TEAE of falls.

## Gastroparesis

There are post-marketing reports of severe gastroparesis events in patients treated with 
incretin drugs, and some events were reported to persist after drug discontinuation. Delayed 
gastric emptying is an incretin pharmacodynamic effect that may contribute to the weight loss 
efficacy and to GI AEs. However, “delayed gastric emptying” is also the PT used for reported 
terms of “gastroparesis.” Other PTs may also indicate gastroparesis-related events, so I used 
the following 10 PTs for a custom MedDRA query:

• Impaired gastric emptying

---

Clinical Review Raymond Soccio NDA 217806 Zepbound (tirzep

MD, PhD atide)

- Diabetic gastroparesis

- Gastroparesis postoperative

- Gastric atony

- Gastric emptying study

- Gastric hypertonia

- Gastric hypomotility

- Gastric ileus

- Gastric residual assessment

- Gastric residual increased

In trials GPHK and GPHL, two subjects reported the PT "impaired gastric emptying" (both AEs non-serious, mild in severity, and resulting in no change in study drug dose), one subject reported non-serious event "diabetic gastroparesis" (moderate severity resulting in study drug discontinuation, Table 112). All three subjects were tirzepatide-treated, and no gastroparesis events were reported in placebo-treated subjects.

In response to an information request, the Applicant provided patient narratives for the three cases:

- Subject was a 41-year-old male with T2D for 5 years taking metformin with a baseline HbA1c of 7.3%, who reported a TEAE of diabetic gastroparesis (moderate, nonserious) on Day 128 while on tirzepatide 12.5 mg. There was no available information regarding whether a gastric emptying study was performed to confirm the diagnosis. Itopride and pantoprazole were prescribed for the treatment of diabetic gastroparesis. Dose of tirzepatide was first reduced to 10 mg on Day 141; then 2 doses of tirzepatide were interrupted (on Days 148 and 162) due to the event. His last dose of tirzepatide (10 mg) was on Day 170, the event resolved on Day 184, study drug was permanently discontinued due to diabetic gastroparesis on Day 197, and the subject permanently discontinued from the study due to diabetic gastroparesis on Day 281. The investigator considered this event to be related to the study drug. During the study, the participant also reported a TEAE of vomiting (mild, nonserious) on Day 123, five days prior to the onset of diabetic gastroparesis.

---

Zepbound (tirzepatide)

Ondansetron was prescribed to treat vomiting, and this event resolved on Day 124 while 
on study drug.

• Subject  <sup>(b) (6)</sup>was a 64-year-old White female with T2D taking metformin 
and baseline HbA1c 7.7% randomly assigned to tirzepatide 15 mg who reported 
impaired gastric emptying (mild, nonserious) on Study Day 303 while taking placebo. 
Prior to the event of impaired gastric emptying, she reported decreased appetite on 
Study Day 256, and study drug dose was reduced from tirzepatide 7.5 mg (last dose on 
Day 274) to placebo in a blinded fashion due to decreased appetite on Day 287. She 
completed the study on placebo, and the impaired gastric emptying event was ongoing 
at the end of the study (Day 545). There was no available information regarding whether 
a gastric emptying study was performed to confirm the diagnosis. She also reported 
multiple other GI-related TEAEs during the tirzepatide treatment period, including 
nausea, eructation, vomiting, abdominal distension, and abdominal pain. Ondansetron 
and famotidine were prescribed to treat nausea and vomiting. All these GI-related
events were mild or moderate in severity and resolved during the study.

• Subject  <sup>(b) (6)</sup>was a 30-year-old Asian female without T2D and baseline 
HbA1c of 5.2% who reported impaired gastric emptying (mild, nonserious) on Day 156
while taking tirzepatide 10 mg. There was no available information regarding whether a 
gastric emptying study was performed to confirm the diagnosis. Concomitant 
medications included multiple traditional Chinese medicines and unspecified herbal 
medicines. No action was taken with study drug due to the impaired gastric emptying
reported on Day 156, but the subject permanently discontinued study drug on Day 426 
due to “withdrawal by subject” (further described as “adopt other ways to lose 
weight”). Her last dose of tirzepatide (10 mg) was taken on Day 414 and impaired gastric 
emptying was resolving at the end of the study (Day 540). Throughout the study 
treatment period, she also reported multiple other GI-related AEs, including nausea, 
diarrhea, constipation, vomiting, chronic gastritis, GERD, helicobacter infection, and 
gastritis erosive. Amoxicillin, bismuth, clarithromycin, and esomeprazole were
prescribed for the eradication of helicobacter infection. Chronic gastritis, helicobacter 
infection, and gastritis erosive were moderate in severity and were resolving at the end 
of the study (Day 540). GERD was mild in severity and was resolving at the end of the 
study. All remaining GI-related events were mild in severity and resolved during the 
study treatment period.

**_Reviewer comment: Based on these narratives, it remains unclear how the diagnosis of 
gastroparesis was made in these subjects, and all three had other common GI AEs. Many 
other trials subjects without reported AEs of gastroparesis had similar GI AEs, including many 
that led to study drug discontinuation. Gastroparesis was nonetheless a rare event reported 
in tirzepatide treated subjects (3/2519, 0.12%), and the three narratives are consistent with 
these events not being serious or severe._**

Section 5 (Warning and Precautions) of labeling on “Severe Gastrointestinal Diseases” will note

---

Clinical Review
Raymond Soccio, MD, PhD
NDA 217806
Zepbound (tirzepatide)

that tirzepatide “has not been studied in patients with severe gastrointestinal disease, including 
severe gastroparesis, and is therefore not recommended in these patients.” At this time, 
further language regarding gastroparesis in labeling is not supported. However, post-marketing 
reports of gastroparesis for tirzepatide or other incretin drugs will continue to be monitored, 
and labeling will be updated as needed.

## Safety Analyses by Demographic Subgroups

The Applicant assessed frequent TEAEs (>5%: abdominal pain, alopecia, COVID-19, constipation, 
decreased appetite, diarrhea, dizziness, dyspepsia, eructation, headache, hyperglycemia, 
nausea, and vomiting) by the following subgroups for trials GPHK and GPHL:

• age <65 and ≥65

• sex

• race (American Indian/Alaska Native, Asian, Black/African American, White, Multiple)

• ethnicity (Hispanic or Latino, not Hispanic or Latino, not reported)

• baseline BMI (<30, ≥30 and <35, ≥35, and ≥40 kg/m<sup>2</sup>)

• baseline eGFR (<60, ≥60 mL/min/1.73 m<sup>2</sup>).

In this analysis, Forest plots showed all subgroup-by-treatment interactions p-values were ≥0.1
(non-significant), using a logistic regression model, with two exceptions (Figure 51):

• Hyperglycemia and eGFR: There was no apparent reduction in hyperglycemia by 
tirzepatide in the small number of subjects in the low eGFR subgroup, but only 4 
subjects reported hyperglycemia in this subgroup.

• Eructation and Latino ethnicity: There was an isolated lack of difference between 
tirzepatide 5 mg and placebo arms.

Notably for tirzepatide’s proposed indication of CWM, the Applicant found no interactions by

BMI. I found the same lack of apparent interaction by T2D status (see Section 8.4.5), indicating 
that common TEAEs occur at similar frequency in subjects with and without T2D.

• North America

• Central/South America and Mexico

• Asia (excluding Japan)

## Specific Safety Studies/Clinical Trials

Based on the Division’s guidance, the Applicant conducted two substudies in trial GPHK (DXA 
and ABPM) to support the safety and favorable benefit/risk of tirzepatide.

## Trial GPHK Dual-Energy X-ray Absorptiometry (DXA) Addendum

The design of this substudy involved sites performing body composition DXA scans at Baseline 
(between Visits 2 and 3, Weeks -1 and 0) and at end of treatment (Visit 21, Week 72, or early 
termination), with the objectives and endpoints in Table 114.

The main eligibility criteria included participation at sites with either Hologic™ or Lunar™ DXA 
scanners with total body composition capabilities and a body size permitted by the DXA 
manufacturer’s specification. Despite the latter exclusion, the full range of BMIs including ≥40 
kg/m<sup>2</sup> was represented in the 255 subjects enrolled in the DXA substudy (Table 115). Of note, 
the placebo group in the DXA substudy had higher body weights, BMI, and waist circumference 
than the tirzepatide 5 mg group, though the 10 mg and 15 mg groups were more similar to 
placebo. Given the prespecified pooling of tirzepatide arms for the efficacy analyses, the lower 
weights in the 5 mg group are unlikely to affect overall results. The DXA substudy trial 
population was also notable for being 72.2% female, 82.0% white race, and 53.7% enrolled in 
the U.S.

**_Reviewer comment: The observed loss in fat mass is supportive for efficacy in CWM, but from 
the FDA perspective the main reason for analyzing body composition is to demonstrate 
safety. Per the Division’s guidance, DXA sub-studies serve to ensure that weight loss is caused 
primarily by reduction in fat content rather than lean body mass. The results for tirzepatide 
(with about 3-fold more loss of fat mass than lean mass) are consistent with other approved 
medications for CWM and do not raise safety concerns._**

## Trial GPHK Ambulatory Blood Pressure Monitoring (ABPM) Addendum

The design of this substudy involved sites performing 24-hour ABPM at baseline (between Visits 
2 and 3, Weeks -1 and 0) and Week 36 (Visit 12) during the treatment period when all 
tirzepatide arms had reached maintenance dosages for at least 16 weeks. Each ABPM session 
was considered technically acceptable if at least 70% of the readings were valid, otherwise a 
second session could be performed. The two ABPM substudy endpoints are in Table 117.

---

| Objectives | Endpoints |
| --- | --- |
| Primary objective, 36 weeks |  |
| To assess tirzepatide (5, 10, and 15 mg) compared with placebo for• 24-hour mean HR and• 24-hour mean BP (SBP/DBP) | • Mean change in 24-hour mean HR from baseline• Mean change in 24-hour mean BP from baseline |

[Image: Im0]
**Source:** Applicant GPHK CSR, page 406 of 3021

The main eligibility criteria for the ABPM substudy included:

• well-controlled BP that was less than 140/90 mmHg, regardless of antihypertensive 
treatment

• stable hypertension treatment regimen for at least 3 months prior to screening (if 
treated for hypertension)

• no history of frequent, intermittent, or chronic tachycardia syndromes

• no shift work (worked during the 22:00 to 07:00 hours period)

• no unavoidable strenuous manual labor during the monitoring period

• nondominant arm circumference ≤ 55 cm and able to obtain a valid baseline ABPM 
reading.

Despite the latter exclusion, the full range of BMIs including ≥40 kg/m<sup>2</sup> was represented in the 
600 subjects enrolled in the ABPM substudy, and weight parameters were balanced across the 
arms (Table 118). The ABPM substudy trial population was also notable for being 68.7%% 
female, 66.8% white race, and 82.3% enrolled in the U.S.

**_Reviewer comment: The observed reduction in systolic blood pressure in tirzepatide arms 
measured by ABPM is consistent with the secondary efficacy endpoint measured by routine 
vitals, but from the FDA perspective the main reason for ABPM is to demonstrate safety.<sup>56</sup> For 
drugs intended for chronic use, FDA recommends conducting ABPM in the indicated 
population to rule out drug-induced sustained elevations in blood pressure (excluding a 3-
mmHg increase in 24-hour average systolic blood pressure), as well as daytime or nighttime 
elevations. Since tirzepatide notably reduces systolic blood pressure, there is no safety 
concern for a pressor effect, though tirzepatide has the expected GLP-1R agonist class effect 
of increasing heart rate (see Section 8.4.7, which details that these changes are unlikely to be 
clinically meaningful). Indeed, a key reason for the ABPM substudy and interim analysis was 
to generate early data on heart rate effects of tirzepatide._**

## Additional Safety Explorations

## Human Carcinogenicity or Tumor Development

Refer to Section 8.5.15 on Neoplasm Safety.

## Human Reproduction and Pregnancy

In the Applicant’s nonclinical program, fetal growth reductions and abnormalities were 
reported in pregnant rats administered tirzepatide at clinically relevant exposures during 
organogenesis. Similarly, fetal growth reductions occurred in rabbits administered tirzepatide 
during organogenesis. The observed effects in animals were potentially attributed to the 
pharmacological effects of tirzepatide on maternal weight and food consumption. Based on 
these data, there may be risks to the fetus from tirzepatide exposure during pregnancy.

The Division of Pediatric and Maternal Health (DPMH) was consulted to assist with the 
pregnancy and lactation subsections of labeling, and Dr. Wenjie Sun’s review is dated 
September 11, 2023. Briefly, Dr. Sun notes that the prevalence of obesity in U.S. women of 
reproductive age (20-39 years) is 39.7%, and that maternal obesity increases the risk of 
spontaneous abortions, recurrent miscarriage, certain fetal malformations, and various 
complications in the antepartum, intrapartum, and postpartum periods. Additional screening is 
recommended for pregnant women with obesity, including early screening for gestational

<sup>56</sup>FDA guidance “Assessment of Pressor Effects of Drugs”

---

Clinical Review
Raymond Soccio, MD, PhD
NDA 217806
Zepbound (tirzepatide)

diabetes by oral glucose tolerance test, additional antenatal fetal surveillance, and anesthesia 
consultation for those with OSA. The CDC recommends weight gain during pregnancy even for 
women with overweight or obesity (15-25 pounds if BMI is 25.0-29.9 kg/m<sup>2</sup> [overweight] and 
11-20 pounds if ≥30 kg/m<sup>2</sup> [obese]).<sup>57</sup>

$$
230kg/m^2
$$

In the tirzepatide clinical development program, women who were pregnant or breastfeeding
were excluded from study participation due to insufficient data to determine possible drugassociated risks. During the trials, women of childbearing potential also were to undergo
pregnancy testing (serum and urine) and use an adequate method of contraception. In the 
event of a pregnancy, study drug was to be stopped and the investigators were to collect data 
on the outcome for both mother and fetus.

In trials GPHK and GPHL, there were 26 pregnancies with maternal exposure to study drug, with 
6 in subjects randomized to placebo. The 20 cases of maternal tirzepatide exposure correspond 
to a rate of 2.15% in women of child-bearing potential. The pregnancy outcomes for these 20 
pregnancies were:

• 7 reported full-term delivery

• 4 reported pre-term delivery

• 4 terminated their pregnancies

• 1 reported spontaneous abortion

• 1 reported ectopic pregnancy

• 3 outcomes unknown/not reported

• There were no reported major or minor fetal malformations in the offspring of subjects 
exposed to tirzepatide.

**_Reviewer comment: Dr. Sun’s review notes that 4 of 11 reported live births in these trials were 
classified as pre-term; although this is higher than the expected background rate, the small 
number of cases are difficult to interpret and labeling is not currently warranted. Two
pregnancy-related PMRs, for a registry and a descriptive study, will be issued to collect 
additional post-marketing data on pregnancy outcomes (see Section 12)._**

Three tirzepatide-treated subjects reported maternal complications during pregnancy:

• 1 reported pre-eclampsia

• 1 reported hypertension and gestational diabetes

• 1 reported hyperglycemia.

**_Reviewer comment: Based on the enrolled trial populations, these maternal events are not 
unexpected._**

In trials GPHK and GPHL, there were 5 pregnancies with paternal exposure to study drug, with 2

<sup>57</sup>https://www.cdc.gov/reproductivehealth/maternalinfanthealth/pregnancy-weight-gain.htm

---

Clinical Review
Raymond Soccio, MD, PhD
NDA 217806
Zepbound (tirzepatide)

in subjects randomized to placebo. Of the three pregnant partners of male tirzepatide-treated 
subjects, one reported no maternal complications, maternal complications are unknown for the 
other two, and fetal outcomes are unknown for all three.

To date, there have been 7 other pregnancies with maternal exposure to tirzepatide in clinical 
trials (5 reviewed in T2D NDA 215866<sup>58</sup> and two others), and an additional 20 exposed 
pregnancies reported from postmarket experience. Please refer to Dr. Sun’s review for details 
of these exposures.

## Dr. Sun’s DPHM review reached the following conclusions:

• <u>Pregnancy:</u> Although tirzepatide is not recommended to be used during pregnancy due 
to its weight loss effect, it will likely be used in females of reproductive potential, and 
there may be unintended exposures during the first trimester before the pregnancy is 
recognized. Because there are currently insufficient data regarding tirzepatide use in
pregnancy, use of tirzepatide in pregnant animals resulted in fetal growth reduction and 
fetal malformations, and unintended pregnancy exposure has occurred with the already 
approved tirzepatide product, it is important to evaluate the safety of tirzepatide during 
pregnancy via Post-Marketing Requirements (PMRs, see Section 12).

• <u>Lactation:</u> There are insufficient data currently to characterize the effects of tirzepatide 
on the breastfed infant, as postmarketing surveillance identified only 2 spontaneously 
reported cases of tirzepatide exposure during lactation (neither with reported AEs). 
Other peptide GLP-1R agonists are present in human milk, so tirzepatide is likely to be 
present in human milk. FDA issued a PMR for a clinical lactation study at the time of T2D 
NDA 215866 approval, and since this study is underway another PMR for a clinical 
lactation study is not required for this NDA.

• <u>Fertility:</u> Animal reproductive studies did not show any adverse effects of tirzepatide on 
fertility, and there are no human data on tirzepatide adversely affecting male or female 
fertility. Four postmarket cases of decreased libido or sexual problems do not raise 
concerns due to the likely high background occurrence of such issues. Like tirzepatide 
for T2D (Mounjaro), the label for this NDA should include language on delayed gastric 
emptying and oral contraceptive use (see Section 4.5).

## Pediatrics and Assessment of Effects on Growth

Not applicable; this submission only included studies in adults (i.e., for eligibility subjects had to 
be ≥18 years of age). Please refer to Section 12 for information related to Postmarketing 
Requirements and Commitments for pediatric assessments.

<sup>58</sup>Review of NDA 215866,

https://www.accessdata.fda.gov/drugsatfda_docs/nda/2022/215866Orig1s000OtherR.pdf

## Overdose, Drug Abuse Potential, Withdrawal, and Rebound

In clinical development of tirzepatide, overdose was defined as taking more than 15 mg of 
tirzepatide in less than 72 hours. In CWM trials GPHK and GPHL, a total of 11 subjects met this 
definition of overdose, each with one event. No tirzepatide overdose events were serious or 
severe, or resulted in study drug discontinuation. One subject experienced AEs at the time of 
the overdose event: subject  <sup>(b) (6)</sup>had two pre-existing mild TEAEs of nausea and 
dyspepsia increase in severity to moderate on the day of the overdose, but there was no 
subsequent dose adjustment, interruption, or discontinuation.

**_Reviewer comment: Given the rarity of tirzepatide overdoses, risk evaluation is limited. GI AEs
are the likeliest outcome of a tirzepatide overdose, but the risk of more significant effects 
(e.g., hypoglycemia, acute renal failure due to volume losses) cannot be ruled out._**

The Applicant used “Drug abuse and dependence” SMQs to screen for drug abuse events in 
trials GPHK and GPHL, and only one AE of “substance abuse” was found: subject<sup>(b) (6)</sup>

is described in Section 8.4.1 on deaths, as she died of acute hepatic failure and multiorgan 
failure after being found unresponsive in the setting of alcohol use, a positive urine drug screen 
for opiates and cocaine, and COVID-19 infection.

**_Reviewer comment: I feel that an association of tirzepatide with substance abuse in this 
subject is unlikely, and this one case with many confounding factors does not change the 
overall safety profile of tirzepatide._**

The Applicant used a “Drug withdrawal” SMQ to screen for potential withdrawal and rebound 
events in trials GPHK and GPHL, and none were found.

The findings for overdose, drug abuse, withdrawal and rebound in tirzepatide trials for CWM
are consistent with trials for T2D as reviewed in NDA 215866, and with other incretin products. 
In conclusion, the concern for overdose, drug abuse, withdrawal, or rebound is low with the use 
of tirzepatide.

## Safety in the Postmarket Setting

## Safety Concerns Identified Through Postmarket Experience

Tirzepatide was FDA-approved for treatment of T2D in the U.S. on May 13, 2022, and it has 
since been authorized for T2D in 42 other countries. It is estimated that <sup>(b) (4)</sup>patients 
have received tirzepatide worldwide.

---

Clinical Review
Raymond Soccio, MD, PhD
NDA 217806
Zepbound (tirzepatide)

To identify post-marketing safety issues, the Office of Pharmacovigilance and Epidemiology, 
Division of Pharmacovigilance-I (DPV-I), has documented their Postmarket Drug Safety 
Surveillance Summary for Mounjaro. Please refer to Dr. Christine Chamberlian’s report on 
October 13, 2023, for details. Briefly, DPV-1 conducted:

• a search of the FAERS database covering May 13, 2022, to August 3, 2023

• a search of the medical literature

• a review of the Periodic Safety Update Reports (PSURs) and Periodic Adverse Drug 
Experience Reports (PAEERs) submitted for tirzepatide.

DPV-I summarized the following New Identified Safety Signals (NISS) for tirzepatide, for 
evaluation in post-marketing data:

• Hypoglycemia (in patients without concomitant use of insulin or insulin secretagogues) 
was identified as a NISS in the most recent surveillance summary and is in preevaluation

• Impaired gastric emptying is in pre-evaluation

• Alopecia, Aspiration, and Suicidal Ideation are under evaluation

• Intestinal obstruction (ileus) and Dysgeusia had action taken

• Eosinophilia, Hypersensitivity, and Cutaneous Amyloidosis are closed.

I also reviewed the Applicant’s 120-day safety update submitted on August 8, 2023 (Table 120), 
which included postmarketing safety data through May 13, 2023. The Applicant concludes 
these data are consistent with the initial tirzepatide CWM NDA, and I concur.

## Expectations on Safety in the Postmarket Setting

The tirzepatide clinical development program provides a substantial amount of safety 
information to characterize the safety profile of tirzepatide in adult populations for CWM
and/or T2D. Older patients (age ≥75 years), patients with renal insufficiency, and patients with 
high cardiovascular risk were not highly represented in phase 3a trials for approval, but these 
populations are being studied in ongoing trials including CVOTs.

While the Division does not endorse off-label use, I anticipate that tirzepatide may be used offlabel for weight loss in people with BMI <27 kg/m<sup>2</sup> and without T2D, a population in which 
safety has not been established. Although tirzepatide could be reasonably expected to produce
weight loss in these patients, there is no expected clinical benefit of weight loss in patients with 
normal/healthy baseline BMI, and it is unknown whether weight loss in patients who are 
overweight but without weight-related comorbidities would translate to improved clinical 
outcomes (such as cardiovascular benefit). Therefore, given the uncertain benefit/risk, the 
Division does not currently indicate weight management drugs for use individuals with normal 
weight (BMI <25 kg/m<sup>2</sup>) or mild overweight (BMI 25.0 to <27 kg/m<sup>2</sup>), or in those with 
overweight (BMI 27.0 to <30 kg/m<sup>2</sup>) without comorbidities. The pharmacokinetics of tirzepatide
(see Section 4.5) indicate higher exposures at lower body weight or BMI, though no weightbased dose adjustment is recommended due to the dose titration approach and routine clinical 
monitoring. Therefore, it is unknown whether those with lower BMIs using tirzepatide off-label
may be at increased risk for some dose-related adverse events.

The observed safety concerns identified in these submissions can be adequately addressed with 
proposed product labeling and routine pharmacovigilance for the indicated population. In 
conclusion, no additional risk evaluation and mitigation strategy (REMS) is recommended for 
this product.

## Additional Safety Issues from Other Disciplines

Safety issues from other disciplines are discussed elsewhere in this review (see Section 4).

## Integrated Assessment of Safety

The safety of tirzepatide for CWM was primarily evaluated in two phase 3 trials (GPHK and 
GPHL), in which 2519 subjects were exposed to at least one dose. The safety profile of 
tirzepatide in the CWM program is generally consistent with the safety profile characterized in 
the tirzepatide T2D program (at the same dosages) as well as other GLP-1R agonists, including 
liraglutide and semaglutide for CWM.

In trials GPHK and GPHL, deaths and serious TEAEs were balanced between tirzepatide and

---

Clinical Review
Raymond Soccio, MD, PhD
NDA 217806
Zepbound (tirzepatide)

placebo groups and did not raise safety concerns. More TEAEs graded as severe and/or leading 
to study drug discontinuation were reported in tirzepatide groups, driven by GI events. Overall 
subjects with any TEAEs were relatively balanced between tirzepatide and placebo (with slight 
imbalances tirzepatide > placebo in trial GPHK and placebo > tirzepatide in trial GPHL).

Section 8.5 on Analysis of Submission-Specific Safety Issues has 17 subsections reviewing
categories of AESI or special safety concerns.

Nine of these safety issues (Sections 8.5.1-9) detail known safety concerns for tirzepatide and 
other incretin drugs. These issues will be labeled in Section 5 (Warnings and Precautions) of the 
USPI and elsewhere in the tirzepatide label for CWM:

• <u><strong>Thyroid safety (8.5.1):</strong></u> Though no cases of MTC or C-cell hyperplasia were observed in 
trials, there was the expected small increase in blood calcitonin that was not clinically 
meaningful. Label Section 5 will include “Risk of Thyroid C Cell Tumors” describing 
nonclinical data, and Section 4 will include the contraindication for personal or family 
history of MTC of MEN2. Since MTC is rare, tirzepatide will also have a postmarketing 
MTC registry as required for other incretin drugs.

• <u><strong>Gastrointestinal disorders (8.5.2):</strong></u> GI TEAEs including serious and severe events 
occurred as expected for tirzepatide. Label Section 5 will include “Severe 
Gastrointestinal Disease” and Section 6.1 “Gastrointestinal Adverse Reactions.”

• <u><strong>Acute kidney injury (8.5.3):</strong></u> This risk is labeled in Section 5 for other incretin drugs for 
T2D or CWM, noting the association with hypovolemia caused by GI TEAEs. Rare but 
slightly imbalanced events of AKI and dehydration occurred in tirzepatide CWM trials, so 
this labeling in Section 5 is appropriate.

• <u><strong>Acute gallbladder disease (8.5.4):</strong></u> This risk is labeled in Section 5 for other incretin drugs
and will be for tirzepatide for CWM. Rare but imbalanced gallbladder events (correlated 
with degree of weight loss) occurred in tirzepatide CWM trials, so this labeling in Section 
5 is appropriate.

• <u><strong>Pancreas safety (8.5.5):</strong></u> Risk of acute pancreatitis is labeled in Section 5 for other 
incretin drugs, noting any numerical imbalances in exposure-controlled incidences 
(events per 100 patient years of exposure). Such an imbalance (0.23 with tirzepatide 
versus 0.11 with comparators) is noted in the label of tirzepatide for T2D, but imbalance 
was not observed in the CWM trials (0.14 with tirzepatide versus 0.15 with placebo). 
Therefore, labeling of “acute pancreatitis” in Section 5 will include all four of these 
exposure-controlled incidences. Tirzepatide was also associated with the expected small 
increases in blood amylase and lipase that was not apparently clinically meaningful, and 
this will be noted in Section 6.1 under laboratory abnormalities.

• <u><strong>Hypersensitivity reactions (8.5.6):</strong></u> These are in label Section 5 for tirzepatide for T2D, 
noting postmarketing reports of serious events in tirzepatide-treated patients. In the 
CWM trials, serious hypersensitivity reactions were very rarely (0.1%) reported in 
tirzepatide-treated subjects and not in placebo-treated subjects, and this will be noted 
in Section 5 of the CWM label. Mild and moderate hypersensitivity reactions were common and will be listed in the Section 6.1 table of common adverse reactions, with a 
subsection noting the distinction between immediate and non-immediate 
hypersensitivity reactions, and the association with ADA-positivity.

• <u><strong>Hypoglycemia (8.5.7):</strong></u> Hypoglycemia in patients with T2D is in label Section 5 for 
tirzepatide for T2D, with similar findings in CWM trial GPHL justifying the similar 
language in Section 5 of the label for CWM. In trial GPHK of subjects without T2D, there 
was also Imbalanced occurrence of blood glucose <54 mg/dl, so potential risk of 
hypoglycemia in patients without T2D will be addressed in label Section 5, with the 
incidence numbers in Section 6.1.

• <u><strong>Diabetic retinopathy complications in T2D (8.5.8):</strong></u> These are in label Section 5 for 
tirzepatide for T2D, with similar findings in CWM trial GPHL justifying the similar 
language in Section 5 of the label for CWM.

• <u><strong>Psychiatric Disorders (8.5.9):</strong></u> All CWM products currently include a Section 5 warning 
and precaution about “Suicidal behavior and ideation,” even when no imbalances are 
found in the respective in clinical trials, as was the case for tirzepatide as detailed above.

Three safety issues (Sections 8.5.10-12) detail common TEAEs for tirzepatide and some other 
incretin drugs that merit subheadings in label Section 6.1:

• <u><strong>Hypotension (8.5.10):</strong></u> These events were imbalanced, and the review above found 
associations with antihypertensive medication use, and with GI AEs leading to 
dehydration, so these will be noted in label Section 6.1.

• <u><strong>Injection site reactions (8.5.11):</strong></u> These events were imbalanced, and the review above 
found associations with ADA-positivity, and this will be noted in label Section 6.1.

• <u><strong>Hair loss (8.5.12):</strong></u> No other incretin products to date have a Section 6.1 subheading on 
hair loss (it is noted in the table of common of adverse reactions for semaglutide for 
CWM without a subheading), but this is justified for tirzepatide for CWM. The detailed 
review of hair loss above demonstrates higher occurrence in female subjects correlated 
with the degree of weight loss, and these will be noted in the label.

The remaining five safety issues (Sections 8.5.13-17) were reviewed in detail and did not raise 
any safety concerns that would be included in labeling:

• <u><strong>Cardiovascular safety (8.5.13):</strong></u> MACE events were rare and CEC-adjudicated in 
tirzepatide CWM trials, with a higher incidence in the placebo than tirzepatide arms. A 
CVOT is ongoing.

• <u><strong>Liver safety (8.5.14):</strong></u> There were 3 potential Hy’s Law case and other cases of 
transaminase elevations in the development of tirzepatide for CWM, but a review of 
these cases revealed no concern for DILI that would merit inclusion in labeling.

• <u><strong>Neoplasm safety (8.5.15):</strong></u> Malignant neoplasm TEAEs were rare in tirzepatide CWM
trials, with a higher incidence in the placebo than tirzepatide arms. Reports of the
benign neoplasm of uterine leiomyoma showed a higher incidence in tirzepatide than 
placebo arms, but these events were rare and potentially attributable to detection bias.

• <u><strong>Other diabetes-related safety issues (8.5.16):</strong></u> The Applicant analyzed CWM trial GPHL

---

Clinical Review
Raymond Soccio, MD, PhD
NDA 217806
Zepbound (tirzepatide)

in subjects with T2D for occurrence of metabolic acidosis, hyperglycemia, and 
amputation or peripheral revascularization. The same analyses were conducted in the 
development of tirzepatide for T2D, and there were no concerning findings in either 
development program that would lead to inclusion in labeling.

• <u><strong>Rare events (8.5.17):</strong></u> Falls were identified as a rare but imbalanced AE, but a case-level 
review revealed no safety concern for tirzepatide. Regarding risk of gastroparesis, 
Section 5 of the tirzepatide label for T2D and proposed label for CWM notes that 
tirzepatide “has not been studied in patients with severe gastrointestinal disease, 
including severe gastroparesis, and is therefore not recommended in these patients.” 
Review of 3 potential gastroparesis TEAEs in the two CWM trials also did not raise a 
safety concern that would merit further mention in labeling.

In addition, incretin drugs increase heart rate as a class effect, and this was the case for 
tirzepatide. As detailed in Section 8.4.7 on vital signs, these pulse increases are small and 
unlikely to be clinically meaningful, so inclusion as a subheading in label Section 6.1 is 
appropriate and consistent with the labeling of tirzepatide for T2D and most other incretin 
drugs.

The most significant uncertainty regarding the safety of tirzepatide and other incretin drugs 
regards long-term use. The indications of CWM and T2D require chronic use, and of course the 
clinical trials are limited in duration. Given the widespread use of incretin drugs like tirzepatide, 
post-marketing and real-world data will inform the long-term safety profile of this class and the 
individual drugs. Despite this inevitable uncertainty, tirzepatide merits approval since it meets 
the Division’s expectations for CWM products, with one-year data in a large development 
program with two adequate and well-controlled trials and a sufficiently-sized safety database.

The safety of tirzepatide in pregnancy is another area of uncertainty. Weight loss is not 
recommended in pregnant patients, but patients may become pregnant while taking tirzepatide 
for CWM, prior to discontinuing it as recommended when pregnancy is discovered. Limited 
available clinical data do not raise concerns for fetal or maternal harm on tirzepatide, but two
PMRs for pregnancy surveillance are recommended to collect more data (see Section 12).

In summary, taking into consideration tirzepatide’s safety profile as expected for an incretin 
and its substantial efficacy for CWM in adults, I conclude that the safety data submitted to this 
NDA is acceptable for approval of this product.

## Prescription Drug Labeling

The Applicant submitted proposed prescribing information (PI) for ZEPBOUND, which was 
reviewed for accuracy, consistency with labeling of tirzepatide for T2D (MOUNJARO), 
consistency with labeling of other products (including other CWM drugs and GLP-1R agonists, 
particularly semaglutide/WEGOVY), and to remove any language that might minimize safety 
findings or imply efficacy claims. Edits to the PI were made directly in that document as part of 
labeling negotiations with the Applicant. Those label sections with relevant issues identified in 
this review are discussed below.

## <u>Section 1. Indications and Usage</u> (b) (4) • was removed as a weight-related comorbid condition,

## Section 2. Dosage and Administration

• A section 2.1 on “Patient Selection” including a Table 1 “BMI conversion chart” was 
added based on FDA feedback. Although I do not believe such a chart is necessary as 
most patients and providers use online BMI calculators, it will be included for 
consistency with other CWM product labels.

• A section 2.3 on “Recommendations Regarding Missed Dose” was added, moving 
information on missed doses and administration day changes to here.

• Section 2.2 on “Recommended Dosage”:

o The final label will include language to “consider treatment response and 
tolerability when selecting the maintenance dosage.” This language allows 
providers and patient flexibility in dose selection without unnecessarily pushing 
for higher doses that may be poorly tolerated.

o The FDA review team reached consensus that, based on the trial designs, three 
“recommended maintenance dosages” of 5, 10, and 15 mg will be labeled. Only 
the 2.5 mg dosage will be specifically excluded (“for treatment initiation and not 
intended for weight management”), allowing prescribers the flexibility to use 
intermediate dosages of 7.5 mg and 12.5 mg in selected patients.

o The 5 mg maintenance dosage was not studied for CWM in subject with T2D 
(Trial GPHL), but as detailed in Section 7.1.1 and Section 8.4.5, there is evidence 
supporting 5 mg as an effective and safe recommended maintenance dosage in 
patients with or without T2D.

o The label will include information on dose reduction due to intolerability, 
recommending reduction to a lower maintenance dosage..

Labeling for this NDA was also reviewed collaboratively by Dr. Kelly Jackson from the Division of 
Medical Policy Programs (DMPP) and Dr. Ankur Kalola from the Office of Prescription Drug 
Promotion (OPDP). Their consult (dated October 17, 2023) reviews that the Applicant’s 
proposed PI, Medication Guide (MG), and Instructions for Use (IFU). Their review:

• simplified wording and clarified concepts where possible in all labeling including the PI

• ensured that the MG and IFU are consistent with the PI

• removed unnecessary or redundant information

DMEPA also reviewed this NDA for safety, with a review from Drs. Ariane Conrad and Idalia 
Rychlik filed on August 17, 2023. They performed a risk assessment of the proposed PI, IFU, 
container labels, and carton labeling for Zepbound to identify areas of vulnerability that may 
lead to medication errors and other areas of improvement. They found the PI and IFU were 
acceptable from a medication error perspective, while they identified some areas of concern 
for the proposed carton and container labels, recommending changes to improve clarity. These 
changes included capitalization and font colors, formatting of the expiration date, separation of
the two barcodes on each container, and inclusion of product identifier on each carton, as 
detailed in Dr. Conrad’s DMEPA review. The Applicant submitted revised container and carton 
labeling, and Drs. Conrad and Rychlik filed a memo on October 31, 2021, confirming that the 
Applicant had implemented their recommendations. The DMEPA-1 human factors team also 
recommended labeling of the needle end of the auto-injector to avoid user error, and the 
Applicant agreed to this (see Section 4.7).

Comments from DMPP, OPDP, and DMEPA, in addition to other review disciplines, were sent to 
the Applicant during labeling negotiations, and the final labeling is pending at the time of this 
review.

## 11. Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategies (REMS)

A REMS is not necessary for this NDA, as the safety issues can be addressed through labeling, 
postmarking requirements, and routine pharmacovigilance.

Below in bold are the 6 postmarketing requirements (PMRs) that will be issued for tirzepatide; 
no postmarketing commitments (PMCs) are recommended for this application. The text 
provides rationale from a clinical perspective. Note that a milk-only lactation study is a PMR for

tirzepatide under T2D NDA 215866 (see Section 8.8.2), so this study is not a PMR for the current

Under the Pediatric Research Equity Act (PREA), all applications for new active ingredients, new 
indications, new dosage forms, new dosing regimens, or new routes of administration are 
required to contain an assessment of the safety and effectiveness of the product for the 
claimed indication in pediatric patients unless this requirement is waived, deferred, or 
inapplicable. As part of the agreed initial Pediatric Study Plan (iPSP) for tirzepatide for CWM 
under IND 139721, the Division agreed to waiving the pediatric study requirement for ages 0 
through 5 years (inclusive), and to deferring the studies in ages 6-11 and 12-17 (inclusive). The 
Applicant’s deferred pediatric studies are required as postmarketing studies.

As detailed in the iPSP, the phase 3 CWM trial in adolescent subjects should be initiated once 
preliminary safety and efficacy are established in adults aged ≥18 years, by trials GPHK and 
GPHL in the current NDA. The final protocol I8F-MC-GPHP (SURMOUNT-ADOLESCENTS) was
submitted, so there is no protocol submission milestone for this PMR.

**1) Conduct a 72-week, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, multicenter, 
parallel-arm study to evaluate the safety and efficacy of Zepbound (tirzepatide) as an 
adjunct to lifestyle intervention for chronic weight management in pediatric patients 
aged 12 to 17 years (inclusive) with obesity.**

**Study Completion:  February 2027**

## Final Report Submission: August 2027

For younger pediatric subjects aged 6-10 years, the Applicant is conducting a PK study for dose 
selection prior to the phase 3 safety and efficacy study. The final PK protocol I8F-MC-GPHV was 
submitted, so there is no protocol submission milestone for this PMR.

**2) Conduct a safety, tolerability, pharmacokinetic, and pharmacodynamic study of** 
**Zepbound (tirzepatide) administered subcutaneously in pediatric patients aged 6 to 11 
years (inclusive) with obesity. Data from this study should be considered when 
choosing dose(s) for the safety and efficacy trial in this pediatric population.**

**Study Completion:  May 2025**

**Final Report Submission: October 2025**

Since approval of the first long-acting GLP-1R agonist in 2010, sponsors of long-acting GLP-1R 
agonists have been required to participate in a MTC surveillance study (registry-based case 
series of at least 15 years in duration) as a PMR to systematically monitor the annual incidence 
of MTC in the U.S., and to identify any increase in incidence related to the introduction of longacting incretin drugs into the U.S. marketplace.

**6) Conduct a medullary thyroid carcinoma registry-based case series of at least 15 years 
duration to systematically monitor the annual incidence of medullary thyroid 
carcinoma in the United States and to identify any increase related to the introduction 
of Zepbound (tirzepatide) for the treatment of overweight and obesity into the 
marketplace. This study will also establish a registry of incident cases of medullary 
thyroid carcinoma and characterize their medical histories related to the use of** 
**Zepbound (tirzepatide) for the treatment of obesity/overweight.**
